PREFACE 

This is a second edition of Outlines on Equity originally prepared in 1926 for the use of the students of Brooklyn Law School of St. Lawrence University. The many important decisions since its preparation, together with the exhaustion of the original edition, have made necessary a revision of the Outlines. 

The jurisdiction of equity is so extensive that any work which attempts to cover all of its branches with any degree of thoroughness must, of necessity, be a voluminous treatise. 

For this reason, many authors have preferred to treat various branches of the law of equity separately and have given us valuable reference books on Specific Performance, Injunctions and other equitable remedies. There has been a distinct need, however, for a brief, concise treatment of the law of equity, which will give to the student the fundamental principles with references to cases in which these principles have been applied. This book has been written in an attempt to fill that need. In order that the main purposes of brevity and conciseness might not be sacrificed, the outline form has been used and only the most important equitable rights and remedies have been considered in any detail. In the citation of authorities preference has been given to the leading cases in New York State and to the recent decisions of the New York Court of Appeals. 

July 1, 1931.

LEON GRANT GODLEY.

EQUITY CHAPTER I HISTORICAL DEVELOPMENT § 1. Equity Defined. 

Equity has been defined as "that portion of remedial justice which was formerly administered in England by the high court of Chancery by virtue of its extraordinary jurisdiction as extended, limited and modified by statute and adapted to our conditions by judicial construction. 

It is not synonymous in meaning with 'justice,' or ‘natural justice'.. ., although sometimes it has been used in that sense." 21 C. J., p. 22. As pointed out by Bispham in his

Principles of Equity, 9th ed., sec. 1, "this definition is rather suggestive than precise and invites inquiry rather than answers it." Moreover, a study of the historical development of equitable jurisprudence and of the theory on which equitable relief is based leads to the conclusion that no definition can be formulated which covers the entire field of equity. The function of equity is to give relief in those cases in which there is no adequate remedy in a court of law and its jurisdiction enlarges constantly to meet the needs of a growing civilization, finding new remedies as new wrongs are developed. It is and always has been an expanding jurisprudence. The fact that a question is novel is of no consequence, although equity has, of course, its own fixed principles and precedents. 

§ 2. Inadequacy of Common Law Remedies. 

The necessity which finally led to the establishment of a court of equity was due to the rigid character of the early common law. In the language of the Court, in Dalton v. 

Vanderveer, 8 Misc. 484, 486, 29 N. Y. S. 342: "The common law courts paid such deference to forms and precedents that they became slaves to them."

The original common law actions were divided into two classes, called real actions and personal actions. Real actions were those which concerned land and all estates or interests therein, and included actions for determining title to or recovering possession of land. The action of ejectment came later. Personal actions were those which concerned persons, chattels, contracts and torts and included two actions ex contractu, known as debt and covenant, and two ex delicto, known as trespass and detinue. Whatever the form of action, the only relief which could be obtained was a judgment giving to the successful suitor (1) a recovery of the possession of land, (2) a recovery of the possession of a chattel, or (3) a recovery of a sum of money. 

Even such limited and often inadequate relief was difficult to obtain because these actions were very cumbersome and because technical matters of form were exalted above the fundamental rights of the suitors. The first step in every action was a written document, called a writ, issued by a clerk, in the name of the King. Writs were prepared for the above-named actions only. The writ contained a brief summary of the facts upon which the right of action was based. The nature of the writs was fixed, so that if no writ could be found which substantially corresponded with the facts constituting the ground of complaint, the injured party could have no redress. And even if a plaintiff secured a writ, the common law judges reserved to themselves the power of determining the sufficiency of the writ and if they held that it was not a proper one for the cause of action or that its recital of facts and formulæ was imperfect or mistaken, the action was dismissed. In the reign of Edward I, Parliament passed an act authorizing the issuance of much broader writs, but the common law judges nullified this act by holding that it authorized only the issuance of writs similar to the existing writs. There was no writ for an action to enforce a trust, to enforce specific performance of a contract, to enjoin a nuisance, to set aside or reform an instrument or to afford relief in a multi-

HISTORICAL DEVELOPMENT 

3 tude of cases which did not fall within the strict rules of the common law. It is obvious that this jurisprudence was woefully insufficient to meet the needs of a rapidly growing civilization. 

§ 3. 

Development of English Court of Chancery. 

Denied of their rights by the common law courts, citizens appealed for relief to the fountain head of all justice, the King, or the King's Council, who afforded such relief not as matter of right but as matter of grace. Then began the delegation of these extraordinary powers to the Chancellor, the King's secretary. The fact that the attention of the 

King and his other high officials was primarily given to matters of State made this desirable. In the reign of Edward III, the King ordered that all such matters as were of grace should be referred to and dispatched by the Chancellor. This delegation by the King conferred a general authority to give relief in all matters, of whatsoever nature, requiring the exercise of the prerogative of grace. The gradual development of this practice into a complete system of equitable jurisprudence is well described in an address delivered by the Right Hon. Richard Burdon Haldane, Lord 

High Chancellor of Great Britain in 1913. He said: "As with the common law, so it is with equity. To know the principles of equity is to know the history of the courts in which it has been administered, and especially the history of the office which at present I chance myself to hold. Between law and equity there is no other true line of demarcation. The King was the fountain of justice. But to get justice at his hands it was necessary first of all to obtain the King's writ. As Bracton declared 'non potest quis sine brevi agere' (no one can sue without a writ). But the King could not personally look after the department where such writs were to be obtained. At the head of this, his chancery, he therefore placed a chancellor, usually a bishop, but sometimes an archbishop, and even a cardinal, for in those days the church had a grip which, to a lord chancellor of the

Twentieth Century, is unfamiliar. At first the holder of the office was not a judge. But he was keeper of the King's conscience, and his business was to see that the King's subjects had remedies when he considered that they had suffered wrongs. Consequently, he began to invent new writs, and finally to develop remedies which were not confined by the rigid precedents of the common law. Thus he soon became a judge. When he found that he could not grant a common law writ he took to summoning people before him and to searching their consciences. He inquired, for instance, as to trusts which they were said to have undertaken, and as a result of his inquiries, rights and obligations unknown to the common law were born in his court of conscience. You see at a glance how susceptible such a practice was of development into a complete system of equity. You would expect, moreover, to find that the ecclesiastical atmosphere in which my official predecessors lived would influence the forms in which they moulded their special system of jurisprudence. This did indeed happen, but even in those days the atmosphere was not merely ecclesiastical for the lord high chancellor in the household of an early English monarch was the King's domestic chaplain and as, unlike his fellow-servants in the household, the lord high steward and the lord great chamberlain, he always possessed the by no means common advantage of being able to read and write, he acted as the King's political secretary. He used, it seems, in early days to live in the palace, and he had a regular daily allowance. From one of the records it appears that his wages were five shillings, a simnel cake, two seasoned simnels, one sextary of clear wine, one sextary of household wine, one large wax candle and forty small pieces of candle. In the time of Henry II the modern treasury spirit appears to have begun to walk abroad, for in the records the allowance of five shillings appears as if subjected to reduction. If he dined away from the palace, si extra domum comederit, and was thereby forced to provide extras, then, indeed, he got his five shillings. But if he

HISTORICAL DEVELOPMENT 

5 dined at home, intra domum, he was not allowed more than three shillings and sixpence. The advantage of his position was, however, that living in the palace he was always at the King's ear. He kept the Great Seal through which all great acts of state were manifested. Indeed, it was the custody of the Great Seal that made him chancellor. Even today this is the constitutional usage. When I myself was made lord chancellor the appointment was effected not by letters patent, nor by writing under the sign manual, nor even by words spoken, but by the sovereign making a simple delivery of the Great Seal into my hands while I knelt before him at Buckingham Palace in the presence of the privy council. "The reign of Charles I saw the last of the ecclesiastical chancellors. The slight sketch of the earlier period which 

I have drawn shows that in these times there might well have developed a great divergence of equity from the common law, under the influence of the canon and Roman laws to which ecclesiastical chancellors would naturally turn. 

In the old courts of equity it was natural that a different atmosphere from that of the common law courts should be breathed. But with the gradual drawing together of the courts of law and equity under law chancellors the difference of atmosphere disappears, and we see the two systems becoming fused into one. "The moral of the whole story is the hopelessness of attempting to study Anglo-Saxon jurisprudence apart from the history of its growth and of the characters of the judges who created it. It is by no accident that among Anglo- 

Saxon lawyers the law does not assume the form of codes, but is largely judge-made. We have statutory codes for portions of the field which we have to cover. But these statutory codes come not at the beginning, but at the end. 

For the most part the law has already been made by those who practice it before the codes embody it. Such codes with us arrive only with the close of the day, after its heat

and burden have been borne, and when the journey is already near its end."

The first principles by which the early chancellors were guided were declared to be honesty, equity and conscience. 

It must be remembered that every decision of the chancellor was, of necessity, an innovation upon or disregard of common law principles and that in making their decisions the chancellors were guided by their own individual consciences, so that the decisions undoubtedly varied according to the individual capacity and sense of right and wrong of the individual who held the office. This led to the familiar sarcastic criticism of Selden: "Equity is a roguish thing. For law we have a measure and know what we trust to. Equity is according to the conscience of him that is chancellor, and as that is larger or narrower so is equity. 'Tis all one as if they should make the standard for the measure we call a foot, a Chancellor's foot. What an uncertain measure this would be. One chancellor has a long foot, another a short foot and a third an indifferent foot. "Tis the same thing in the chancellor's conscience."

But as a system of equitable principles began to be developed, this theory of a personal conscience was abandoned and the conscience that now governs is the conscience of the court-a judicial, not a personal conscience.

The system of appealing to the chancellor grew until a separate court was established to care for these cases. The constantly enlarging power of the court of chancery met much opposition, particularly with respect to the power of a court of equity to restrain the enforcement of a judgment of a common law court. This dispute was finally settled by royal decree in favor of the chancery court, and equity became a separate system of jurisprudence, operating side by side with the law courts but having its own jurisdiction and indeed its own forms and practice. This lasted in England until 1873 when the courts were consolidated into one su-

HISTORICAL DEVELOPMENT 

7 preme court of which chancery was only a division. For authorities supporting the above statements and for further discussion of the historical development of the High Court of Chancery in England, see Dalton v. Vanderveer, 8 Misc. 484, 29 N. Y. S. 342; Pomeroy's Equity Jurisprudence, 2d. ed., ch. I; Bispham's Principles of Equity, 9th ed., ch. I; Spence's Equitable Jurisdiction of The Court of Chancery, vol. I; Story's Equity Jurisprudence, 13th ed., vol. I. 

§ 4. Equity Jurisdiction in New York. 

From 1663 to the Revolution there was, except during brief intervals, a separate court of chancery in the Colony of New York. This court was reorganized by the constitution of 1787 and, with some changes made in 1821, existed until 1846 when, by the constitution of that year, its jurisdiction was transferred to the Supreme Court. Dalton v. 

Vanderveer, 8 Misc. 484, 29 N. Y. S. 342; Matter of Runk, 200 N. Y. 447, 94 N. E. 363. Section 64 of the present Civil

Practice Act provides as follows: 

"The general jurisdiction in law and equity which the supreme court of the state possesses under the provisions of the constitution includes all the jurisdiction which was possessed and exercised by the supreme court of the colony of New York at any time, and by the court of chancery in 

England on the 4th day of July, 1776; with the exceptions, additions and limitations created and imposed by the constitution and laws of the state. Subject to those exceptions and limitations the supreme court of the state has all the powers and authority of each of those courts and exercises the same in like manner."

Our courts frequently rely upon the inherent power of a court of equity to take jurisdiction. In Trotter v. Lisman, 209 N. Y. 174, 102 N. E. 575, for example, the Court of 

Appeals said, at p. 180: "The Supreme Court possesses general jurisdiction in equity, and creditors' actions to reach the personal property and things in action of their judgment debtors have always been of equitable cognizance." And

in Nat. Tradesmen's Bank v. Wetmore, 124 N. Y. 241, 250, 26 N. E. 548, the same Court said: "The subjects of fraud and trusts are peculiarly matters of equity jurisdiction, which is very comprehensive where the other tribunals cannot afford relief, and its want of it is not to be inferred from the novelty of questions presented."

From 1846 to 1848 equity was administered by the Supreme Court under the old chancery forms. In 1848 New 

York adopted a code, being the first American state thus to simplify its practice. Section 8 of our present Civil

Practice Act is a re-enactment of a provision of that first 

Code of Civil Procedure and provides as follows: 

"There is only one form of civil action. The distinction between actions at law and suits in equity and the forms of those actions and suits, have been abolished." 

Section 6 of the Civil Practice Act defines a civil action as "any action except an action prosecuted in the name of the people of the state as plaintiffs against a party charged with crime."

In construing section 8, supra, the courts have held, however, that only the distinction in procedure has been abolished. The fundamental difference between an action at law and an action in equity is as great as ever, and the Court of Appeals has gone so far as to say that the legislature cannot change it. In Gould v. Cayuga Co. Nat. Bank, 86 

N. Y. 75, the Court said, at p. 83: "It is idle to say that the distinction between legal and equitable actions has been wiped out by the modern practice. It is true that all actions must be commenced in the same way; that in every form of action the facts constituting the cause of action or defense must be truly stated; that fictions in pleadings have been abolished and that both kinds of actions are triable in the same courts. But the distinction between legal and equitable actions is as fundamental as that between actions ex contractu and ex delicto and no legislative fiat can wipe it out."

HISTORICAL DEVELOPMENT 

9 

And in Sadlier v. City of New York, 185 N. Y. 408, 78 

N. E. 272, the court said: "There is only one form of action. The distinction between actions at law and suits in equity and the forms of those actions have been abolished. 

All that a plaintiff has to do in any case is to set forth in his complaint a clear concise and unequivocal statement of the facts constituting his cause of action and a demand of the judgment to which he supposes himself entitled. "The inherent fundamental differences between actions at law and actions for equitable relief, such as determine whether a trial of the action by jury is a matter of right and otherwise affect the interest of litigants, have not been and cannot be abolished. For such reason and for the very simple reason that a person must, in his complaint, state the facts constituting his cause of action, a plaintiff who brings an action for equitable relief must establish such cause of action, or his complaint must be dismissed."

The result is that even now, when the same court administers both systems of law and equity, a party suing in equity must maintain his equitable action upon equitable grounds or fail, even though he prove a good cause of action at law upon the trial. If he fails, either the complaint is dismissed or, in some cases, the action may be sent to the proper side of the court for trial. C. P. A., sec. 111; Loeb v. 

Supreme Lodge, 198 N. Y. 180, 91 N. E. 547; Model Building & Loan Assn. v. Reeves, 236 N. Y. 331, 140 N. E. 715; 

Chadbourne v. Mayer, 207 App. Div. 754, 202 N. Y. S. 805, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 7.

The distinction between legal and equitable actions is further illustrated in the case of Di Menna v. Cooper & 

Evans, 220 N. Y. 391, 115 N. E. 993, Bachrach's Cases in

Equity, p. 12, wherein it was held that the plaintiff lost his right to trial by jury when he brought his action to foreclose a mechanic's lien with an alternative prayer for a money judgment, as permitted by statute, although the defendant, by timely demand, might preserve his right, in the event of failure of the lien, to a jury trial of the other issues.

Equitable defenses, however, can now be interposed in legal actions and it is not necessary to ask for affirmative equitable relief. Dobson v. Pearce, 12 N. Y. 156, Bachrach's

Cases in Equity, p. 2; Susquehanna S.S. Co. v. Andersen & 

Co., 239 N. Y. 285, 146 N. E. 381.

Where an equitable counterclaim is interposed, the practice is to try the counterclaim prior to the trial of the law issues. Fulton County &c Co. v. Hudson River Telephone 

Co., 200 N. Y. 287, 93 N. E. 1052; Gobran Realty Corp. v. 

James Butler's Grocery Co., 230 A. D. 661, 245 N. Y. S. 492.

CHAPTER II MAXIMS § 5. In General. 

Certain principles have become so firmly established in equity that they are now quoted as maxims. It should be borne in mind that these are underlying principles which are subject to limitations and exceptions. The principal maxims with some of their limitations will be discussed briefly in the following sections. Their application will be shown in the discussion of specific rights and remedies. 

§ 6. 

First Maxim: Equity Will Not Suffer a Wrong to 

Be Without a Remedy.

This is the application to equity of the broader maxim Ubi jus ibi remedium (whenever a legal right has been infringed a remedy will be given). The maxim refers of course, to civil, not criminal wrongs.

To this maxim may be added that when equity once takes jurisdiction of a wrong, it will make the remedy a complete one. So that if equity has jurisdiction of a cause of action and it proves impracticable or impossible at the time of the trial to grant equitable relief, a court of equity may grant a money judgment or other relief which is peculiarly within the province of a court of law. Lynch v. Met. Ry. Co., 129 

N. Y. 274, 29 N. E. 315, 15 L. R. A. 287, 26 Am. St. Rep. 

523, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 15. Sadlier v. City of 

New York, 185 N. Y. 408, 78 N. E. 272.

It has been said that a court of equity needs no other court to finish its work. 

The plaintiff, however, in order to bring himself within the rule, must have a cause of action in equity at the commencement of his action. Wappler v. Woodbury Co., 246 

N. Y. 152, 158 N. E. 56.

While the maxim under consideration is the basis of 11

equity jurisprudence, since equity was founded to give relief where other courts failed, the maxim is subject to the following important limitations: a. Equity will not usurp the legislative function. This means that equity will not take jurisdiction of a specific wrong not within its principles, but will leave the matter to the legislature to provide relief. To illustrate: Equity will not, in the exercise of its inherent jurisdiction, enforce the right of privacy, that is, the right to live one's life without comment or criticism by others, although it will enforce such a right if authorized by the legislature. Roberson v. 

Rochester Folding Box Co., 171 N. Y. 538, 64 N. E. 442, 59 L. R. A. 478, 89 Am. St. Rep. 828, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 24; Civil Rights Act, sec. 51, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 30.

A court of equity will not grant relief for a wrong which belongs properly to the tribunal of conscience. It will not restrain the foreclosure of a mortgage because the motives of the plaintiff are unworthy. A court of equity "cannot assume control over that large class of obligations called imperfect obligations, resting upon conscience and moral duty only, unconnected with legal obligations." 

Watertown, 19 Wall. 107, 22 L. Ed. 72. 

Rees v. 

In Baumann v. Baumann, 250 N. Y. 382, 165 N. E. 189, the court declined to enjoin Baumann's second wife from holding herself out as Mrs. Baumann, although they did declare that his divorce from his first wife and his subsequent remarriage were void, saying: * * * "Equity cannot by injunction restrain conduct which merely injures a person's feelings and causes mental anguish The law does not remedy all social evils or moral wrongs * * * Attempts to govern the morals of people by injunctions can only result in making ridiculous the courts which grant such decrees." b. Equity will not intervene when there is a complete and adequate remedy at law. This follows from the his-

MAXIMS 

13 torical development of equity. If the suitor could get complete and adequate relief in the law court, there was no necessity for him to appeal to the King, the application being a matter of grace and not a matter of right. 

If a party has a right which can as well be asserted in an action at law, equity will not intervene. Allerton v. Beldon, 49 N. Y. 373, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 30. However, the remedy at law must be a complete and adequate one. 

For illustrations of cases in which there is some remedy at law but not a complete and adequate one, see Wickwire v. 

Warner, 191 App. Div. 835, 182 N. Y. S. 165, Bachrach's 

Cases in Equity, p. 32, affd. 233 N. Y. 572, 135 N. E. 923; 

Bomeisler v. Forster, 154 N. Y. 229, 48 N. E. 534, 39 

L. R. A. 240, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 34; Falk v. 

Hoffman, 233 N. Y. 199, 135 N. E. 243, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 37.

In some cases there is concurrent jurisdiction in law and equity, such as an action at law by a vendee to recover a down payment and an action in equity to impress a vendee's lien. These will be explained under the specific subjects. c. Equity will not act in contravention of a positive rule of law. Equity follows the law. This means that where a court of law has established a right, a court of equity will not act contrary to the right so established. For example, equity will not aid a widow to recover her dower when her right is barred by statute. Akin v. Kellogg, 119 N. Y. 441, 23 N. E. 1046, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 40. On the same principle, the courts, as far as possible, apply to equitable titles and interests the rules of law applicable to similar legal estates and interests. For example, equitable estates are governed by the same law of descent as legal estates. d. Equity will not enjoin libel or slander. This is on the theory that there is an adequate remedy at law. As early as 1742, Lord Chancellor Hardwicke said: "Notwithstanding there should be a libel, yet unless it is a contempt of the court, I have no cognizance of it; for whether it is a libel

against the publick or private persons, the only method is to proceed at law." Huggenson's Case, 2 Atk. 469.

The limitation is now firmly established in the state courts. The leading New York case is Marlin Fire Arms 

Co. v. Shields, 171 N. Y. 384, 64 N. E. 163, Bachrach's

Cases in Equity, p. 42, in which the court refused to enjoin the future publication of malicious criticisms of a manufactured article, although the manufacturer had no remedy in an action at law for libel because of his inability to prove special damages. See also, Francis v. Flinn, 118 U. S. 385, 30 L. ed. 165, 68 Sup. Ct. Rep. 1148.

Where the words spoken are instrumentalities by which an illegal act, such as boycott or coercion, is consummated, their use may be enjoined. Gompers v. Buck's Stove & Range Co., 221 U. S. 418, 31 Sup. Ct. 492, 55 L. ed. 797; 

Allen Manufacturing Co. v. Smith, 224 App. Div. 187, 191, 229 N. Y. S. 697; American Malting Co. v. Keitel, 209 Fed. 

351; Wilner v. Bless, 243 N. Y. 544, 154 N. E. 598.

As Judge Cardozo well says in Nann v. Raimist 255 

N. Y. 307, 174 N. E. 690: * * * "Equity does not intervene to restrain the publication of words on a mere showing of their falsity It intervenes in those cases where restraint becomes essential to the preservation of a business or of other property interests threatened with impairment by illegal combinations or other tortious acts, the publication of the words being merely an instrument and incident." e. Equity will not interfere with the enforcement of the criminal law. The leading case in New York is Davis v. 

American Society, 75 N. Y. 362, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 46, in which the court refused to enjoin a threatened arrest on the ground that the rights of the accused could be adequately protected in a court of law. The Court of 

Appeals has followed this doctrine in many cases, even refusing to consider the question of constitutionality when an action was brought to restrain the prosecution of an indict-

MAXIMS 

15 Buffalo ment under an alleged unconstitutional statute. 

Gravel Co. v. Moore, 201 App. Div. 242, 194 N. Y. S. 225, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 50, affd. 234 N. Y. 542, 138 

N. E. 439; Delaney v. Flood, 183 N. Y. 323, 76 N. E. 209.

However, where property rights as distinguished from personal rights are involved, a suit will lie, where prosecution is threatened under a statute alleged to be unconstitutional. Biddles v. Enright, 239 N. Y. 354, 146 N. E. 625, 39 A. L. R. 766; Municipal Gas Co. v. Public Service Comm., 225 N. Y. 89, 121 N. E. 772, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 740. This is the federal rule, also. See authorities cited in Municipal Gas Co. v. Public Service Comm., supra.

It should be noted that the principle does not apply to cases where the act complained of may be criminal as well as tortious. Thus, in Cranford v. Tyrrell, 128 N. Y. 341, 28

N. E. 514, the maintenance of a house of ill fame was restrained at the suit of a neighbor although the proprietor was also amenable to the criminal law. 

§ 7. Second Maxim: Equity Aids the Vigilant. 

Lapse of time may bar relief in a court of equity even where no statute of limitations governs. Ellison v. Moffatt, 1 Johns. Ch. 46, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 53.

The earlier statutes of limitations were limited to actions at law but courts of equity in most cases applied the statutes by analogy. The courts now hold that where the remedies are concurrent the same statute applies. Borst v. Corey, 15 

N. Y. 505, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 59; Minion v. 

Warner, 238 N. Y. 413, 418, 144 N. E. 665, 41 A. L. R. 

1412; Keys v. Leopold, 241 N. Y. 189, 148 N. E. 828.

While the word, laches, is sometimes used as meaning lapse of time, true laches, as has been pointed out, is "not mere delay, but delay that works a disadvantage to another." (Stinness, C. J., in Chase v. Chase, 20 R. I. 202.)

Thus, a delay short of the statute of limitations may bar relief if circumstances have so changed as to make it in-

equitable for the court to apply the remedy. Groesbeck v. 

Morgan, 206 N. Y. 385, 99 N. E. 1046, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 68. 

§ 8. Third Maxim: Between Equal Equities the Law Will Prevail. 

This means that where two persons have equal equitable rights, the one who also acquires the legal title to the property has a preference over the one that has an equitable right only. Seymour v. Wilson, 19 N. Y. 417,

Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 487. There is some doubt whether this doctrine applies in New York to assignments of choses in action. Central Trust Co. v. West India Imp. 

Co., 169 N. Y. 314, 62 N. E. 387, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 490. 

§ 9. Fourth Maxim: Equality Is Equity. 

This means that where equity finds it necessary to distribute property among a class it does so equally. For example, where a donee of a power has the discretion to select one of a class to receive the property and fails to do so, equity will divide the property equally among all the members of the class. See R. P. L., sec. 160; Smith v. Floyd, 140 N. Y. 337, 35 N. E. 606. Similarly, equality is the leading principle underlying the equitable remedy of contribution. 

§ 10. Fifth Maxim: He Who Comes Into Equity Must 

Do So With Clean Hands.

This maxim has also been expressed: "He that committeth iniquity shall not have equity." One of the earliest applications of the maxim was to a bill in equity filed by one highwayman for an accounting by his co-criminal of the proceeds of their robberies.

The complainant is first judged, and if he is guilty of inequitable conduct, no relief will be granted him. Fay v. 

Lambourne, 124 App. Div. 245, 108 N. Y. S. 874, Bachrach's

MAXIMS 

17 

Cases in Equity, p. 73, affd. 196 N. Y. 575, 90 N. E. 1158.

The maxim has been applied in many classes of cases, as will be shown hereafter. See, also, for specific examples, Leonard v. Poole, 114 N. Y. 371, 21 N. E. 707, 4 L. R. A. 728, 11 Am. St. Rep. 667, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 74; 

Prince Mfg. Co. v. Prince's Metallic Paint Co., 135 N. Y. 24, 31 N. E. 990, 17 L. R. A. 129, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 76. The "unclean hands" must relate to the cause under consideration, however. In West v. Washburn, 153 App. 

Div. 460, 465, 138 N. Y. S. 230, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 82, the Court used the following language: "While equity does not employ itself in settling quarrels amongst rogues, still a rogue may have a clean cause of action and it is with reference to the cause of action which he produces that the maxim of clean hands applies." 

§ 11. 

Equity.

Sixth Maxim: He Who Seeks Equity Must Do 

Under this doctrine one suing in equity to cancel an instrument on the ground of usury must, in the absence of statute (See Gen. Bus. Law, sec. 377), offer to return the amount actually borrowed with legal interest. On the same principle, a party seeking rescission must offer to put the other party in status quo. In general, one must do equity as a prerequisite to being afforded relief. Comstock v. Johnson, 46 N. Y. 615, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 82. 

§ 12. Seventh Maxim: Equity Looks Upon That as Done 

Which Ought to Be Done.

This principle is the basis of equitable conversion. When a testator imperatively directs land to be sold and converted into money, equity treats the conversion as taking effect on the death of the testator, on the theory expressed in the maxim. Moncrief v. Ross, 50 N. Y. 431, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 566. On the same principle, when a binding contract is made for the sale of land, from that time on, equity, for its purposes, treats the vendee as the owner of

the land and the vendor as the owner of the purchase money. See, also, Sprague v. Cochran, 144 N. Y. 104, 38 

N. E. 1000, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 284. 

§ 13. Eighth Maxim: Between Equal Equities Priority of Time Will Prevail. 

Where two parties have equal equities (successive equitable assignments, for instance) the one who first acquired his title has a better right than a subsequent purchaser, even one without notice. Central Trust Co. v. West India 

Imp. Co., 169 N. Y. 314, 62 N. E. 387, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 490; Muir v. Schenck & Robinson, 3 Hill 228, 38 Am. Dec. 633, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 484. 

§ 14. 

Ninth Maxim: Equity Imputes an Intention to Fulfill an Obligation.

This means that when an obligation rests upon an individual and he does an act which may or may not be intended as a fulfillment of that obligation, equity presumes that he acted honestly and in the performance of his duty. 

For example, where a trustee buys property with trust funds and takes title in his own name, he is deemed to have acquired the property for the trust and not for himself personally, on the theory that that is what he ought to have done and, hence, equity will assume that he did it in that way. See Cromwell v. Bklyn. Fire Ins. Co., 44 N. Y. 42, 4 Am. Rep. 641, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 292. 

§ 15. 

Tenth Maxim: Equity Acts in Personam. 

In other words, equity does not divest "A" of title and confer that title on "B," but directs "A" to convey to "B”. 

Where a court has jurisdiction of the parties, therefore, it may direct a conveyance of land outside of the jurisdiction of the court. Mead v. Brockner, 82 App. Div. 480, 81 

N. Y. S. 594; Gardner v. Ogden, 22 N. Y. 327, Bachrach's 

Cases in Equity, p. 90,

MAXIMS 

19 Under the provisions of the Civil Practice Act, if the court has jurisdiction of the res, it may in certain cases acquire jurisdiction of the person of the defendant outside of the State by substituted service. Garfein v. McInnis, 248 N. Y. 261, 162 N. E. 73. 

§ 16. Eleventh Maxim: Equity Looks at the Substance 

Rather Than the Form.

The meaning of this is that equity will enforce the rights of the parties as they really are and not permit the form that the transaction takes to mislead the court. For example, a deed of real property will be construed, in equity, to be a mortgage if it was, in fact, given as security for a loan. Horn v. Keteltas, 46 N. Y. 605, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 219. 

§ 17. 

Twelfth Maxim: Equity Acts Specifically. 

The meaning of this is that equity, generally speaking, orders a specific thing to be done or enjoins its being done and does not give damages for a wrong, although it may, of course, award damages as incidental to equitable relief or even in lieu thereof, where equitable relief becomes impossible. Dr. David Kennedy Corporation v. Kennedy, 165 

N. Y. 353, 59 N. E. 133, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 95.

CHAPTER III TRUSTS § 18. Trust Defined. 

The basic principle of a trust is that one person holds the legal title to property in which the right to the beneficial interest belongs to another. The beneficiary does not take any legal estate or interest in the property but may enforce the performance of the trust in equity. R. P. L., sec. 100; Whiting v. Hudson Trust Co., 234 N. Y. 394, 407, 138 N. E. 33, 25 A. L. R. 1470.

An illustration of a trust is a devise of land to "A", in trust, to collect the rents and proceeds and pay them over to "B" for life, the property then to go to "C" absolutely.

During "B's" life "A" would hold the legal title, with, generally speaking, all the attributes of ownership except the right to retain the proceeds, while "B" not having any legal title or interest, could still, by a proceeding in equity, compel "A" to account for the proceeds. Upon "B's" death, "A's" title would automatically cease and both the legal title and the beneficial or equitable interest would be united in "C”. 

§ 19. Express and Implied Trusts Defined. 

Trusts may be either express or implied. Express trusts are those created intentionally by the direct act of the parties expressed by written or spoken words. Implied trusts are those created by operation of law. 

§ 20. Active and Passive Trusts. 

Express trusts may be either active or passive. Active trusts are those in which the trustee has active duties to perform which make it essential that the legal title shall remain in him. Passive trusts are those in which the bare legal title alone is in the trustee and the beneficiary is entitled to both the actual possession of the property and the 20

TRUSTS 

21 receipt of the proceeds. Passive trusts have been abolished in New York. R. P. L., secs. 92, 93, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 104. 

§ 21. Essentials of Express Trust. 

The Court of Appeals has stated the essentials of an express trust to be: (1) a trustee; (2) an estate devised or conveyed to him; (3) a beneficiary. To these may be added the necessity for a certain and ascertainable object. The trustee and the beneficiary should be different persons; otherwise, the legal and equitable interests would merge. 

Greene v. Greene, 125 N. Y. 506, 26 N. E. 739, 21 Am. St. 

Rep. 743, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 101; Weeks v. 

Frankel, 197 N. Y. 304, 90 N. E. 969. This does not mean that a beneficiary cannot be one of several trustees or sole trustee when he is one of several beneficiaries. Rogers v. 

Rogers, 111 N. Y. 228, 18 N. E. 636; Woodward v. James, 115 N. Y. 346, 22 N. E. 150, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 111. 

§ 22. 

Creation of Express Trust.

No particular form of words is necessary to create a trust. It is not even necessary to use the words "trust” or "trustee". Woodward v. James, 115 N. Y. 346, 22 N. E. 

150, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 111. "It is sufficient if the intention to create a trust under the statute can be fairly collected from the instrument, and what is implied from the language used is, as in other instruments, deemed to be expressed." Striker v. Daly, 223 N. Y. 468, 472, 119 

N. E. 882. 

Precatory words may be sufficient if a clear intention to create a trust can be found. Foose v. Whitmore, 82 N. Y. 

405, 37 Am. Rep. 572, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 115.

A delivery of the declaration of trust is not essential. Matter of Brown, 252 N. Y. 366, 169 N. E. 612.

§ 23. Form of Trust.. 

A trust of real property is required by the statute of frauds to be proved by a writing. The statute expressly exempts trusts arising or being extinguished by operation of law. R. P. L., sec. 242, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 

117; Hutchins v. Van Vechten, 140 N. Y. 115, 35 N. E. 446, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 117. A trust of personal property may be established by parol evidence. Matter of 

Carpenter, 131 N. Y. 86, 29 N. E. 1005, and authorities therein cited; Hirsh v. Auer, 146 N. Y. 13, 40 N. E. 397, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 120. Matter of Brown, supra. An oral declaration of trust of real property may be established if the parties, by their pleadings, raise no objection to the lack of written evidence. McKenna v. Meehan, 248 N. Y. 206, 213, 161 N. E. 472.

Testamentary trusts of both real and personal property must be established by a will duly executed as prescribed by statute. Reynolds v. Reynolds, 224 N. Y. 429, 121 

N. E. 61. 

§ 24. Express Trusts of Real Property. 

Only such active trusts of real property as are authorized by statute are permitted in New York. R. P. L., sec. 91. (Passive trusts are abolished. R. P. L., secs. 92, 93, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 104.) Those authorized by statute are four in number. R. P. L., sec. 96. They are as follows: 

(1) To sell real property for the benefit of creditors:

To sustain a trust of this kind it must appear that this was the primary or sole purpose of the grant, and the duty of the grantee to sell for the benefit of creditors must be imperative. Woerz v. Rademacher, 120 N. Y. 62, 23 N. E. 

1113. The trust is broad enough to cover the case of a solvent debtor who conveys a portion of his property to a trustee to pay a portion of his debts, with the proviso that any surplus shall be returned to him.

TRUSTS 

223 (2) To sell, mortgage or lease real property for the benefit of annuitants or other legatees, or for the purpose of satisfying any charge thereon: The direction must be imperative. Cooke v. Platt, 98 N. Y. 35. A trust in executors to sell and convert real estate into money and distribute the proceeds among certain legatees is a valid express trust under this section. Tarditi v. Tomazzoli, 107 Misc. 664, 177 N. Y. S. 127. For a discussion of the meaning of the word "lease", see Hascall v. King, 162 N. Y. 134, 56 N. E. 

515, 76 Am. St. Rep. 302. (3) To receive the rents and profits of real property, and apply them to the use of any person, during the life of that person, or for any shorter term, subject to the provisions of law relating thereto: A direction to pay over rents and profits is equivalent to a direction to apply them to the use of a beneficiary and creates a valid trust. Marx v. McGlynn, 88 N. Y. 358. There must, however, be a direction to apply the rents and profits to the use of some person; an authorization to receive rents and profits alone is not sufficient. Holly v. Hirsch, 135 N. Y. 590, 594, 32 N. E. 

709. A grantor may by deed create a trust authorizing the trustee to receive the rents and profits and apply them to the use of the grantor during his life. Blake v. Mattern, 186 

App. Div. 488, 174 N. Y. S. 556. (4) To receive the rents and profits of real property, and to accumulate the same for the purposes, and within the limits, prescribed by law: The accumulations prescribed by law are governed by section 61 of the Real Property Law, which, in general, prescribes that property may be accumulated for the benefit of one or more minors in being, in which case it must commence on the creation of the estate, out of which the rents and profits are to arise, and terminate at or before the expiration of their minority; or it may be directed to commence at a time subsequent to the creation of the estate, in which case it must commence within the time limited for the vesting of future estates and during the minority of the beneficiary and must terminate at

or before the expiration of such minority. The accumulation must be solely for the benefit of the minor. Pray v. 

Hegeman, 92 N. Y. 508. Therefore, a plan by which any part of the accumulation is added to the principal of the estate renders the trust invalid. Matter of Hoyt, 116 A. D. 

217, affd. 189 N. Y. 511, 81 N. E. 1166; Hascall v. King, 162 N. Y. 134, 56 N. E. 515, 76 Am. St. Rep. 302. 

§ 25. Rule Against Perpetuities. 

A trust must be limited to two lives in being, plus a possible minority. R. P. L., sec. 42; Central Trust Co. v. Egleston, 185 N. Y. 23, 77 N. E. 989; Matter of Horner, 237 N. Y. 

489, 143 N. E. 655; Matter of Trevor, 239 N. Y. 6, 145 

N. E. 66. 

§ 26. Powers in Trust. 

Where an express trust is created for a purpose not specified, no estate vests in the trustee; but if the trust directs or authorizes the performance of an act which may be lawfully performed under a power, it is valid as a power in trust and the property descends to the persons otherwise entitled to it, subject to the execution of the trust as a power. R. P. L., sec. 99. 

§ 27. Trusts of Personal Property. 

The New York statute governing express trusts of real property does not apply to trusts of personal property. 

Holmes v. Mead, 52 N. Y. 332. Hence, a trust in such property may be created for any purpose valid at common law. Accumulations of income are permitted on substantially the same conditions as those of the rents and profits of real property and the provisions as to perpetuities are substantially the same. Pers. Prop. Law, secs. 11, 15, 16. 

§ 28. Spendthrift Trusts. 

In New York the beneficiary of a trust under subdivision 3 of section 96 of the Real Property Law, or a similar trust

TRUSTS 

25 of personal property, cannot alienate his interest, nor can it be reached by a creditor unless there is a surplus of income over what is necessary for the education and support of the said beneficiary. R. P. L., secs. 98, 103; Pers. Prop. Law, sec. 15. If the income is over twelve dollars per week, a judgment creditor may reach ten per cent. of such income by a special form of execution. Hamilton v. Drogo, 241 

N. Y. 401, 150 N. E. 496; C. P. A., sec. 684; Justices Ct. Act, sec. 300. Or a judgment creditor may, by suit in equity, reach such surplus income as is not necessary for the support of the beneficiary and his dependents. Williams v. 

Thorne, 70 N. Y. 270. The income can also be reached for the support of the beneficiary's family. C. P. A., sec. 1171a; 

Wetmore v. Wetmore, 149 N. Y. 520, 44 N. E. 169, 33 L. R. 

A. 708, 52 Am. St. Rep. 752; Moore v. Moore, 208 N. Y. 97, 101 N. E. 711. 

§ 29. Charitable Trusts. 

Charitable trusts differ from other express trusts in that the beneficiaries need not be definite and certain. The purposes for which they may be created are generally divided into four classes: (1) for religious purposes, such as the distribution of Bibles and religious tracts or the support of home or foreign missions, (2) for educational purposes, such as the foundation of scholarships, the establishment of libraries or the endowment of colleges, (3) for eleemosynary purposes, such as the maintenance of hospitals and homes for the aged or gifts to the poor, and (4) for public purposes, such as the maintenance of parks, paving and lighting of streets, and in general anything which will lighten the burden of the taxpayer.

The struggle in New York to establish the validity of a charitable trust where the beneficiaries are indefinite and where the funds are to be held in perpetuity has been a long one.

An early New York case arose out of a bequest of property to trustees in perpetuity, one half of the income to be

used for the education of poor children until the sum amounted to $10,000 when the entire income was to be so used. Williams v. Williams, 8 N. Y. 525. The Court of

Appeals upheld the validity of the trust, stating that the law of charitable uses as it existed in England and the jurisdiction of the Court of Chancery over such trusts became the law of this State and had not been repealed. The steps by which the same court gradually reversed this ruling are traced in the opinion in Holland v. Alcock, 108 N. Y. 312, 335, 16 N. E. 305, 2 Am. St. Rep. 420, as follows: “After the decision in that case, the struggle in this court for the overthrow of charitable uses began in the case of 

Owens v. Missionary Society, 14 N. Y. 380. The opponents of such trusts had for their justification the repeal in 1788 in this State of all the British statutes which upheld such trusts in England and the substitution of a charity system maintained by our statute laws in the form of corporate charters, containing, by legislative enactment, power to receive, hold and administer charitable gifts. * * * From the case of Owens v. Missionary Society, 14 N. Y. 380, through the cases of Downey v. Marshall, 23 id. 366, Levy v. Levy, 33 id. 97, Bascom v. Albertson, 34 id. 584, Burrill v. Boardman, 43 id. 254, and Holmes v. Mead, decided in 

1873, (52 id. 332), the struggle was continued and the announcement definitely made, in the latest of those cases, that the controversy was closed by the adoption of the principles enunciated in the last mentioned case.”

The rule became firmly established, therefore, that there must be a definite beneficiary, since otherwise, there would be no one to enforce the trust, and that the trust must not offend the statute against perpetuities. Many charitable trusts were lost to the people of this State, the property going to relatives whom the testator did not desire to have enjoy his property. The matter came to a climax when a trust to found a library, created by the will of Samuel J. 

Tilden, a noted lawyer and an ex-Governor of the State, was held invalid because there was no definite beneficiary

TRUSTS 

27 at the time of his death who could enforce the trust. Tilden v. Greene, 130 N. Y. 29, 28 N. E. 880, 14 L. R. A. 33, 27

Am. St. Rep. 487. This led to the enactment of the law which is now incorporated in section 113 of the Real Property Law and provides that no charitable trust shall be held invalid on account of the indefiniteness of the beneficiary; that the title shall vest in the trustee or, in the absence of a trustee, in the Supreme Court; and that the Attorney 

General shall represent the indefinite beneficiaries and enforce the trust. It also restored the cy pres doctrine. See, also, R. P. L., secs. 114, 114a. The courts have held that this legislation restored the law as laid down in Williams v. Williams, supra, and have been very liberal in construing the provisions of the statute. Allen v. Stevens, 161 N. Y. 

122, 56 N. E. 568; Matter of Robinson, 203 N. Y. 380, 96 

N. E. 925; Matter of Cunningham, 206 N. Y. 601, 100 N. E. 

437; Matter of MacDowell, 217 N. Y. 454, 112 N. E. 177; 

Trustees v. Carmody, 211 N. Y. 286, 105 N. E. 543; Matter of Potts, 205 App. Div. 147, 199 N. Y. S. 880, affd. 236 

N. Y. 658, 142 N. E. 323. In re Durbrow's Estate, 245 

N. Y. 469, 157 N. E. 747. 

§ 30. Capacity to Act as Trustee. 

Any reasonable being may be a trustee. A corporation may act as such if authorized by its charter. 

§ 31. 

Duties of Trustee.

A trustee must not only act for the benefit of the trust estate, but must act in such a way as not to gain any personal advantage, directly or indirectly, except such as the law specifically gives him. He may not place himself in a position where his personal interest will come into conflict with that of his beneficiary. He must maintain a position where his every act is above suspicion and where the trust estate, and it alone, can receive not only his best service, but his unbiased and uninfluenced judgment. Gould v. Gould, 203 App. Div. 807, 197 N. Y. S. 515.

32. Trust Investments.

In New York, the investments which a trustee is authorized to make are prescribed by statute. Pers. Prop. Law, sec. 21; Banking Law, sec. 146. In general, these include first mortgages on real estate worth 50 per cent more than the amount loaned and investments legal for savings banks. 

Guaranteed mortgages are also authorized. 

If a trustee invests in unauthorized securities and there is a profit, the profit goes to the trust estate; if there is a loss, he must bear it personally. He may, therefore, lose, but he cannot gain. Villard v. Villard, 219 N. Y. 482, 114 N. E. 

789. If a trustee is authorized by the trust instrument to invest in other than legal trust securities, he may follow the direction without liability. But even then, in the absence of a direction to invest in a specific security, he must use care and prudence in the investment and act solely for the benefit of the trust estate. Matter of Hall, 164 N. Y. 196, 58 N. E. 11; Carrier v. Carrier, 226 N. Y. 114, 123 N. E. 

135; Gould v. Gould, 126 Misc. 54, 213 N. Y. S. 286. He must not mingle trust property with his own. Surr. Ct. 

Act, sec. 231; Gould v. Gould, supra. Trust funds may, however, for certain purposes of investment, be mingled with each other. Matter of Union Trust Co., 219 N. Y. 514, 114 N. E. 1057. 

§ 33. 

Delegation of Authority. 

A trustee cannot delegate acts which involve the exercise of judgment and discretion, but may delegate other acts. 

Gates v. Dudgeon, 173 N. Y. 426, 66 N. E. 116. 

§ 34. Responsibility for Acts of Co-Trustee. 

Where one of several trustees, without action by the others, receives and misapplies trust funds, the others will not be liable; but if the others unnecessarily turn over trust funds to one of their number and he misapplies them, all will be liable. Bruen v. Gillet, 115 N. Y. 10, 21 N. E. 676, 4 L. R. A. 529, 12 Am. St. Rep. 764. Trustees are not per-

TRUSTS 

29 mitted to sit idly by, however, and permit a co-trustee to misconduct himself, when they knew or should have known of such misconduct, and then escape liability. Gould v. 

Gould, 126 Misc. 54, 213 N. Y. S. 286. 

§ 35. Termination of Trust. 

Section 109 of the Real Property Law provides as follows: 

"When the purpose for which an express trust is created ceases, the estate of the trustee shall also cease." Upon the fulfillment of the purpose for which a trust was created, therefore, the estate of the trustee automatically ceases and no formal conveyance by the trustee is necessary to transfer the title. Watkins v. Reynolds, 123 N. Y. 211, 25 N. E. 322,

Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 108. The New York rule is that prior to that time not even all the beneficiaries acting together can terminate the trust and force the trustee to convey. Cuthbert v. Chauvet, 136 N. Y. 326, 32 N. E. 1088, 18 L. R. A. 745, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 122.

The creator of a trust in personal property may, by statute, revoke the trust in whole or in part with the written consent of all parties beneficially interested. Pers. 

Prop. Law, sec. 23. Whittemore v. Equitable Trust Co., 250 N. Y. 298, 165 N. E. 454.

An express trust may be terminated by merger; that is, by the vesting of the legal and equitable estates in the same person. Rose v. Hatch, 125 N. Y. 427, 26 N. E. 467; Weeks v. Frankel, 197 N. Y. 304, 90 N. E. 969; Greene v. Greene, 125 N. Y. 506, 26 N. E. 739, 21 Am. St. Rep. 743. 

§ 36. Implied Trusts. 

Implied trusts are of two kinds, resulting trusts and constructive trusts. Resulting trusts are those which arise by operation or presumption of law from certain acts of the parties from which an intention to create a trust is presumed to exist. Constructive trusts are those imposed by law irrespective of any actual or supposed intention that a

trust should be created, but merely to assert the rights of the parties or to frustrate fraud. 

It should be noted that the trusts implied by equity do not take the form of one of the express trusts heretofore explained. They are generally passive in form, the trustee being required to turn over the property to the beneficiary or his nominee and to account for any profit made. 

§ 37. Resulting Trusts. 

Resulting trusts may be divided into the following classes: a. Trusts resulting from the payment of the purchase price of real property where title is taken in the name of another.

Prior to statute the requisites of this trust were as follows: Payment must be made before or at the time of transfer of title; the payment must not be intended as a loan; the payment must not be intended as a gift; all or an aliquot part of the purchase price must be paid. There was a presumption of a gift when payment was made by a husband or father and title taken in the name of wife or child. 

Wiegert v. Schlesinger, 150 App. Div. 765, 135 N. Y. S. 

335, affd. 210 N. Y. 573, 104 N. E. 1143.

This trust has been abolished in New York "unless the grantee either (1) takes the same as an absolute conveyance, in his own name, without the consent or knowledge of the person paying the consideration; or, (2) in violation of some trust, purchases the property so conveyed with money or property belonging to another." R. P. L., sec. 94, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 134; Everett v. Everett, 48 

N. Y. 218, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 134; Lounsbury v. Purdy, 18 N. Y. 515, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 138; 

Fagan v. McDonnell, 115 App. Div. 89, affd. 191 N. Y. 515, 84 N. E. 1112, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 145. In cases falling within these exceptions, the above-mentioned common law requisites must also appear. To illustrate: An

TRUSTS 

31 aliquot part of the purchase money must be paid. Schierloh v. Schierloh, 148 N. Y. 103, 42 N. E. 409, Bachrach's 

Cases in Equity, p. 150; Leary v. Corvin, 181 N. Y. 222, 73 N. E. 984, 106 Am. St. Rep., 542; Weingarten v. Minskoff, 204 App. Div. 750, 198 N. Y. S. 691. The amount must not be advanced as a loan. Widmayer v. Warner, 192 

App. Div. 499, 182 N. Y. S. 629. The payment must be made at or before the transfer of title. Niver v. Crane, 98 N. Y. 40.

In considering trusts arising under the exceptions contained in the statute, it should be remembered that Sec. 94

R. P. L. relates to trusts arising solely from the payment of the purchase price. If an express agreement is relied upon, there is necessarily excluded any trust arising purely by implication of law from the payment of the purchase price by one and the taking of title in the name of another. 

Bispham's Principles of Equity, 9th ed. sec. 80. In such an event the case is governed by the Statute of Frauds, part performance, confidential relationship, or other principles. 

As the Court of Appeals said in Foreman v. Foreman, 251 N. Y. 237, 167 N. E. 428: ** * "The statute has put an end to the rule at common law that where a grant is made to one for a consideration paid by another, a trust results inevitably and always, by force merely of the payment, irrespective of the intention * The conveyance is operative according to its terms, if nothing else is proved. The statute has no effect, however, on trusts constructively imposed as a consequence not of payment alone, but of payment in combination with other or extrinsic circumstances. As to this the decisions are uniform and ample. 'It is only the common law trust for the benefit of an individual from whom the consideration for a grant issues, and resulting from the fact of payment of the consideration and having no other foundation that the statute abolishes' (Carr v. Carr, 52 N. Y. 251, 260.)”

So where a real estate operator, whose wife was insane, paid the consideration and had titlę taken in the name of

his daughter, upon her oral promise to convey to his nominee, and there was part performance, a trust was imposed. 

Jeremiah v. Pitcher, 26 App. Div. 402, affd. 163 N. Y. 574, 57 N. E. 1113, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 141. For other like cases, see Sinclair v. Purdy, 235 N. Y. 245, 139 

N. E. 255; Foreman v. Foreman, supra, McKinley v. Hessen, 202 N. Y. 24, 95 N. E. 32. Where there is not part performance, a different rule may apply. Neverman v. 

Neverman, 254 N. Y. 496, 173 N. E. 838.

The acts necessary to constitute part performance of an oral contract, so as to take it out of the Statute of Frauds, will be considered later (Sec. 141, post).

The statute only applies to real property. Hence, if the grantee sell the property, the statute is no defense to an action to have a trust imposed upon the proceeds. Robbins v. Robbins, 89 N. Y. 251, 258; Bork v. Martin, 132 N. Y. 

280, 286, 30 N. E. 584.

Since for partnership purposes, real property purchased with partnership funds is considered personalty, the statute has no application to such a purchase where title is taken in the name of one of the partners. Fairchild v. Fairchild, 64 N. Y. 471. b. Trusts resulting where the trustee or other fiduciary purchases property in his individual name with trust funds. 

To come within this rule the funds or property thus misapplied must be held in a fiduciary capacity and the property purchased must be identified in the hands of one who is not a bona fide purchaser for value without notice. 

Holmes v. Gilman, 138 N. Y. 369, 34 N. E. 205, 20 L. R. A. 

566, 34 Am. St. Rep. 463, Bachrach's Cases in Equity p. 152; 

Amer. Sugar Ref. Co. v. Fancher, 145 N. Y. 552, 40 N. E. 

206, 27 L. R. A. 757, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 160.

In Holmes v. Gilman, supra, it was held that the trustee must not only account for the property, but also for any profit that has resulted from the transaction.

In order to impose this trust, it must appear with rea-

TRUSTS 

33 sonable certainty that trust funds were used in making the purchase. A court of equity will not assume from the mere fact that the purchaser had or might have trust funds in his possession, that he used them in paying for the property. Ferris v. Van Vechten, 73 N. Y. 113; Matter of 

Hicks, 170 N. Y. 195, 63 N. E. 276.

Property on which such a trust is sought to be imposed, as well as property obtained by fraud, or stolen, may be followed and a trust impressed so long as it can be identified and is not in the hands of a bona fide purchaser for value. 

The rule applies where the property has been transformed into property of another kind. As Judge Andrews said in 

Newton v. Porter, 69, N. Y. 133: "It is immaterial in what way the change has been made, whether money has been laid out in land or land has been turned into money or how the legal title to the converted property may be placed. 

Equity only stops the pursuit when the means of ascertainment fails, or the rights of bona fide purchasers for value without notice of the trust have intervened. The relief will be adapted to the circumstances of the case so as to protect the interests and rights of the owner." 

See also, Fur & Wool Trading Co. v. Fox, 245 N. Y. 215, 156 N. E. 670; Jaffe v. Weld, 220 N. Y. 443, 116 N. E. 73.

A resulting trust will also arise where a trustee mixes trust funds with his own. In such a case, equity either compels the trustee to account and show how much of the property is his own, or impresses a lien to the extent of the property belonging to the trust estate. 

C. Trusts resulting where a trust is only partly declared or fails. To bring a case within this rule, it should be shown that property was acquired by will or through a conveyance which was without consideration and that there was expressed a clear intention that the property should be held in trust. When these are shown and the purposes of the transfer are wholly or partially undeclared or cannot be carried out in whole or in part, a trust is implied. Casey

v. Casey, 161 App. Div. 427, 146 N. Y. S. 348, Bachrach's 

Cases in Equity, p. 170. d. Trusts resulting where a conveyance is made without consideration under circumstances indicating that the grantee is not to take beneficially. See Rogers v. N. Y. & 

Tex. Land Co., 134 N. Y. 197, 32 N. E. 27, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 172; Matter of O'Hara, 95 N. Y. 403, 47 Am. 

Rep. 53, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 209. No trust results from a voluntary conveyance only. There must be other circumstances which tend to show that the grantee was not intended to take beneficially. 

§ 38. 

Constructive Trusts. "A constructive trust is the formula through which the conscience of equity finds expression. When property has been acquired in such circumstances that the holder of the legal title may not in good conscience retain the beneficial interest, equity converts him into a trustee." CARDOZO, C. J. in Beatty v. Guggenheim Exploration Co., 225 N. Y. 386, 122 N. E. 378.

There are various ways in which a constructive trust may arise, where no trust was intended to be created. Some of the most important are as follows: a. A trustee or other fiduciary cannot acquire rights antagonistic to the beneficiary or gain a personal advantage in his dealings with the trust estate. Where he has attempted to do so, any benefit which he has derived will be deemed to be held by him in trust for the beneficiary. 

One of the earliest cases is the Rumford Market case. 

Keech v. Sanford, White and Tudor's Leading Cases in

Equity, vol. I, p. 53. In that case the trustee applied for the renewal of a lease for the benefit of an infant, but the request was denied. He then renewed for himself; but it was held that he still held the lease for the benefit of the trust estate, the court suggesting that if the transaction were allowed to stand, few advantageous leases would be renewed

TRUSTS 

35 for the trust estate. Under the same principle the courts look with suspicion upon a trustee purchasing from himself even at a public sale. The reason for this is that his interest in buying at the lowest price would interfere with his duty to obtain the highest price. Davoue v. Fanning, 2 Johns. Ch. 252, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 182. The doctrine applies not only to technical trustees but to all those who stand in a fiduciary relation to others. Nichols v. Riley, 118 App. Div. 404, 103 N. Y. S. 554, Bachrach's 

Cases in Equity, p. 192, (Attorney and Client); Meinhard v. 

Salmon, 249 N. Y. 458, 164 N. E. 545 (Coadventurers); 

Seligson v. Weiss, 222 App. Div. 634, 227 N. Y. S. 338 (Partners); Beatty v. Guggenheim Exploration Co., 223 

N. Y. 294, 119 N. E. 575, id. 225 N. Y. 380, 122 N. E. 378 (Agent); Gould v. Gould, 126 Misc. 542, 13 N. Y. S. 286 (Executor); Godley v. Crandall & Godley Co., 212 N. Y. 121, 105 N. E. 818, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 436 (Director); Wendt v. Fischer, 243 N. Y. 439, 154 N. E. 303 (Broker). b. Where a person obtains property from a trustee or other fiduciary without paying value or with notice of the trust, a constructive trust will arise. This is on the theory that the legal title to the property remains in the trustee until the property reaches the possession of some person who has paid full value and is ignorant of its trust character. Whoever receives property knowing that it is part of a trust estate and has been transferred in violation of the duty or power of the trustee, takes it subject to the right not only of the beneficiary, but, also, of the trustee to reclaim possession of the specific property or to recover damages for its conversion if it has been converted. Wetmore v. Porter, 92 N. Y. 76, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 197. 

C. Constructive trusts may result from contract. To illustrate: Where a valid contract is made for the purchase and sale of real property the vendor holds the legal title in trust for the vendee. Williams v. Haddock, 145 

N. Y. 144, 39 N. E. 825, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 585.

Reife v. Osmers, 252 N. Y. 320, 169 N. E. 399. This doctrine is also applied in the elevated railroad cases where there is a conveyance of the land with a reservation of the right to future damages for the taking of the easements of light, air and access. Since these easements are appurtenant to the land and only the owner of the land can sue the elevated railroad company for the damages, equity imposes a trust on the proceeds for the benefit of the grantor. West. 

Union Tel. Co. v. Shepard, 169 N. Y. 170, 62 N. E. 154, 58 

L. R. A. 115, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 202. If the grantee refuses to sue the elevated railroad the grantor may do so, making the grantee a party, and thus the whole matter may be settled in one action. Drucker v. Manhattan 

Ry. Co., 213 N. Y. 543, 108 N. E. 74. d. Constructive trusts based on actual or presumptive fraud will be treated under the subject of Fraud, Chapter 

VIII, infra.

CHAPTER IV MORTGAGES § 39. Scope of Subject. 

We are here concerned chiefly with mortgages that are such in fact but not in form. Mortgages that are such in form are more properly within the scope of a work on Real Property. Nevertheless, in order to understand how equity deals with a deed, absolute on its face, which was, in fact, given to secure a loan, it is necessary to know something of the history of mortgages. 

§ 40. 

History of Mortgages. 

A mortgage originally was a conveyance with a defeasance clause, by which it was provided that in case the mortgagor paid the mortgagee a certain sum of money on a day named the conveyance should be void and the estate revest in the grantor, or he should be entitled to call upon the mortgagee for a reconveyance. If the payment was not made on the day named, the estate became absolute in the mortgagee. 

On the law side of the court the mortgagor was without remedy. But in equity, looking at the substance and not the form, it was considered that as the transaction was only a pledge for a debt, the mortgagor ought, by the payment of the debt, plus interest, to get his property back. This was termed the equity of redemption; i. e., the right given in equity to buy back or redeem the land. The Statute of Limitations was twenty years. Since the tenure of the mortgagee was thus made uncertain for a long time, equity now gave him a remedy by permitting him to foreclose this equity of redemption. By decree a new day was fixed on or before which the mortgagor was required to pay his debt or forfeit his estate. Later it was provided by statute that the premises should be sold and the balance over and above the debt, including interest and costs, should be returned to the mortgagor. 

37

The money lenders faced with this right of the borrower to redeem his land, sought to evade this invention of a court of equity by making the borrower agree as a condition of the loan to waive the right of redemption. The court of equity met this by holding that the mortgagor could not, either in the mortgage itself, or by any instrument executed at the time of the loan, waive or surrender his right of redemption. This has been summarized in the expression "Once a mortgage, always a mortgage," and has been firmly adhered to ever since. Macauley v. Smith, 132 N. Y. 524, 30 N. E. 997, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 231; Peugh v. 

Davis, 96 U. S. 332, at p. 336, 24 L. ed. 150. 

§ 41. 

Mortgages in New York.

In New York a mortgage merely creates a lien upon the land, the legal title remaining in the mortgagor. Trimm v. 

Marsh, 54 N. Y. 599, 13 Am. Rep. 623, Bachrach's Cases in

Equity, p. 226. The old English foreclosure of the right of redemption does not exist, therefore. Moulton v. Cornish, 138 N. Y. 133, 33 N. E. 842, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 

243; Thompson v. Lewis, 182 App. Div. 556, 169 N. Y. S. 

501. See, however, Civil Practice Act, sec. 1082. 

§ 42. Deed Given as Security for Loan. 

A deed, absolute on its face, if given as security for a loan, will be construed to be a mortgage, although at law the deed transfers the full title. Equity looks at the substance and if the transaction is in reality a transfer of property as security for a loan, equity will construe it to be a mortgage and permit the borrower to redeem his property; that is, will compel the transfer of the title back to him upon his paying the amount of the loan. Horn v. Keteltas, 46 N. Y. 

605, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 219; Mooney v. Byrne, 163 N. Y. 86, 57 N. E. 163, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 

234; Macauley v. Smith, 132 N. Y. 524, 30 N. E. 997, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 231. This is so whether the deed be executed by the borrower or a third person (Carr v.

MORTGAGES 

39 

Carr, 52 N. Y. 251, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 221) or whether the deed purports to convey the property to the lender or a third person (Thompson v. Lewis, 182 App. Div. 

556, 169 N. Y. S. 501). It will be seen that the relief afforded is analogous to the old English practice referred to in section 40, supra.

Since a mortgage, in this state, merely creates a lien, the title remaining in the mortgagor (See sec. 41, supra), equity consistently holds that, for its purposes, the title never passes to the grantees named in deeds given as security for loans but remains in the grantors. Barry v. 

Hamburg-Bremen Fire Ins. Co., 110 N. Y. 1, 17 N. E. 405;

Macauley v. Smith, supra, and cases cited. 

§ 43. Equitable Mortgages. 

Equitable mortgages will be treated under the subject of 

Liens, Chapter VI, infra.

CHAPTER V EQUITABLE ASSIGNMENTS § 44. Assignments in Law and in Equity. 

At common law assignments of things not in possession were not recognized. "It is a common learning in the law that a man cannot grant or charge that which he hath not." 

Perkins, sec. 65, quoted in Kribbs v. Alford, 120 N. Y. 519, 524, 24 N. E. 811. Equity, however, recognizes assignments not only of choses in action but, also, of contingent interests and expectations and of things which have no present actual existence but rest in possibility only. Field v. City of New York, 6 N. Y. 179, Bachrach's Cases in

Equity, p. 255. The strict rule of the common law has been modified somewhat by statute. For instance, an assignee of a chose in action may now sue at law in his own name. 

The rules of equity still apply, however, to many such cases, especially to assignments of property to be acquired in future. 

Where an assignment is forbidden by law (See Pers. 

Prop. Law, sec. 41), or is against public policy (See Bowery Bank v. Wilson, 122 N. Y. 478, 25 N. E. 855), the assignment is not recognized in equity any more than it is at law. This is on the principle that "equity follows the law." (See sec. 6c, supra.) On the same principle, a consideration is required in equity as well as at law. Tallman v. Hoey, 89 N. Y. 537, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 259. 

§ 45. Effect of Equitable Assignment. 

In the language of the Court of Appeals: "A court of 

Equity does not hold that a conveyance of that which does not exist operates as a present transfer in equity any more than it does in law. But it construes the instrument as operating by way of present contract, to give a lien which, as between the parties, takes effect and attaches to the subject 40

EQUITABLE ASSIGNMENTS 

41 of it as soon as it comes into the ownership of the party." 

Kribbs v. Alford, 120 N. Y. 519, 524, 24 N. E. 811, quoted, with approval, in Central Trust Co. v. West India Imp. Co., 169 N. Y. 314, 323, 62 N. E. 387, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 490. See, also, Fairbanks v. Sargent, 117 N. Y. 

320, 22 N. E. 1039, 6 L. R. A. 475, Bachrach's Cases in

Equity, p. 261. When the property comes into the possession of the assignor, the equitable ownership of the assignee, as between the parties, attaches, and the assignor and anyone else who, with knowledge, participates in a conversion of the property, is liable to the assignee. Hinkle Iron Co. v. Kohn, 229 N. Y. 179, 128 N. E. 113. A bona fide purchaser of a non-negotiable chose in action takes it subject to all equities existing between the parties to the instrument and also to the equities which a third party could enforce against the assignor. Central Trust Co. v. 

West India Imp. Co., supra; Stevenson Brewing Co. v. Iba, 155 N. Y. 224, 49 N. E. 677; Matter of Nunez, 226 N. Y. 

246, 123 N. E. 492. 

§ 46. 

Order on Particular Fund. 

An order to pay a sum of money out of a particular fund will operate as an equitable assignment, whether the money is then due or to become due, and the person on whom the order is drawn is bound, after notice of such assignment, to apply the fund, as it accrues, to the payment of the order and to no other purpose. However, if the order is drawn generally on the credit of the drawer, without regard to the source from which the money used for its payment is obtained, it will not operate as an equitable assignment, nor does the designation by the drawer of a fund from which the drawee is subsequently to reimburse himself for such payment, make it such. Brill v. Tuttle, 81 N. Y. 454, 37 Am. 

Rep. 515; Lauer v. Dunn, 115 N. Y. 405, 22 N. E. 270; 

Foley v. N. Y. Savings Bank, 157 App. Div. 868, 142 N. Y. S. 

822. In Hinkle Iron Co. v. Kohn, 229 N. Y. 179, 183, 128 

N. E. 113, the Court said:

"The test of an equitable assignment is the inquiry whether or not an assignment makes an appropriation of the fund so that the debtor would be justified in paying the debt or the assigned part to the person claiming to be the assignee.' 99 By statute a check does not operate as an assignment unless certified or accepted by the bank. Neg. Inst. Law, sec. 

325. Also, a bill of exchange does not operate as an assignment until it is accepted. Neg. Inst. Law, sec. 211.

CHAPTER VI EQUITABLE LIENS AND SERVITUDES § 47. Legal and Equitable Liens Distinguished. 

A common law lien connotes possession. It is the right to retain possession of personal property until a debt or claim is satisfied and is ordinarily waived by a voluntary relinquishment of possession.

An equitable lien, on the other hand, does not depend at all upon possession. It may exist even on property which is to come into being or which is to be subsequently acquired. Kribbs v. Alford, 120 N. Y. 519, 24 N. E. 811; 

McCaffery v. Woodin, 65 N. Y. 459, 22 Am. Rep. 644; 

Titusville Iron Co. v. City of New York, 207 N. Y. 203, 209, 100 N. E. 806. This has been discussed under the subject of Equitable Assignments. See sec. 45, supra.

When the property comes into being or is acquired, the possession remains with the debtor and equity forecloses the lien; i. e., directs that the property be sold and the proceeds applied to the payment of the debt. 

§ 48. Pledge of Property to Be Subsequently Acquired. 

As stated by the court, in Titusville Iron Co. v. City of 

New York, 207 N. Y. 203, 209, 100 N. E. 806: "Mortgages or contracts pledging subsequently acquired property, though void at law, will nevertheless be enforced in equity as agreements to give liens and also as against purchasers with notice."

Where the rights of creditors intervene, however, equity will prefer their claim to that of the lienor. Zartman v. 

First Nat. Bank, 189 N. Y. 267, 82 N. E. 127, 12 L. R. A. (N. S.) 1083, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 276, and authorities therein cited.

Where the intent is to give a lien, but through some defect the lien is not established, equity, treating that as done 43

which ought to be done, will enforce it. Nat. Bank of Deposit v. Rogers, 166 N. Y. 380, 59 N. E. 922, Bachrach's 

Cases in Equity, p. 271; Payne v. Wilson, 74 N. Y. 348, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 280; Sprague v. Cochran, 144 N. Y. 104, 38 N. E. 1000, Bachrach's Cases in Equity p. 284. The dictum in the case last cited as to the effect of an oral agreement to give a mortgage should be disregarded. 

Sleeth v. Sampson, 237 N. Y. 69, 142 N. E. 355. 

§ 49. Assignments Required to Be Filed. 

Certain assignments are required, by statute, to be filed. 

Lien Law, sec. 15 et seq. This requirement has been held 

Van Kannel Revolving Door Co. 

104 N. Y. S. 653. to apply to equitable liens. v. Astor, 119 App. Div. 214, 

§ 50. 

Vendor's Lien. 

Where a vendor delivers title to an estate in land to a vendee without receiving the entire purchase price, equity creates a lien on the land, in the hands of the purchaser or any one not a bona fide purchaser for value, to the extent of the unpaid purchase price. Hubbell v. Hendrickson, 175
