N. Y. 175, 67 N. E. 302, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 296.

Our Court of Appeals has stated that there is no satisfactory theory upon which the lien is based, although it holds that the fundamental principle underlying the lien is that it would be unconscionable for the vendee to hold the land and not pay for it. Zeiser v. Cohn, 207 N. Y. 407, 414, 101 N. E. 184. In the case last cited, the court granted a lien to a creditor against land in the hands of a third person, where a debt to the creditor was part of the consideration for the conveyance by his debtor to such third person and, also, held that the remedies at law and in equity were concurrent.

The doctrine has also been applied in favor of a vendor who discharged a collateral mortgage. Bach v. Kidansky, 186 N. Y. 368, 78 N. E. 1088.

EQUITABLE LIENS AND SERVITUDES 

45 The law may be considered settled in this state, therefore, that the lien exists not only in favor of vendors but also in favor of third persons and creditors whose debts enter into the consideration of the transfer, where no intervening rights of other creditors are involved. 

§ 51. Waiver of Vendor's Lien. 

While a vendor's lien may be reserved by express contract, it is generally implied. The presumption is in favor of a lien, but this presumption may be rebutted by showing that the vendor accepted other security, such as the note or other obligation of a third party, a mortgage upon the same or other property or a commodity in lieu of money. Vail v. 

Foster, 4 N. Y. 312; Hazeltine v. Moore, 21 Hun 355. The lien is not waived by taking the vendee's own note, bond or covenant. Maroney v. Boyle, 141 N. Y. 462, 36 N. E. 511, 38 Am. St. Rep. 821; Hubbell v. Henrickson, 175 N. Y. 175, 67 N. E. 302, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 296. Since the remedies at law and in equity are concurrent, the obtaining of a judgment at law does not operate as either an election of remedies or a waiver of the lien. Flickinger v. Glass, 222 N. Y. 404, 118 N. E. 712; Giarratano v. McIlwain, 215 

App. Div. 644, 214 N. Y. S. 582. 

§ 52. 

Vendee's Lien. 

On the default, before conveyance, of the vendor, a vendee has a lien for the amount paid under a contract for the purchase of land, even in the absence of other equities, such as taking possession, making improvements, etc. Elterman v. Hyman, 192 N. Y. 113, 84 N. E. 937, 127 Am. St. Rep. 

862, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 299. There is considerable difference of opinion as to the theory on which this lien is based, but in the case last cited, the Court stated that in equity part payment creates partial ownership and that, therefore, the vendee has an interest in the land itself to the extent of payments made.

The lien applies not only where there is a breach of the contract by failure to convey, but, also, to any other breach by the vendor. Feldblum v. Laurelton Land Co., 151 App. 

Div. 24, 135 N. Y. S. 349, affd. 210 N. Y. 594, 104 N. E. 

1129; Soderbery v. Laurelton Land Co., 189 App. Div. 910, 178 N. Y. S. 920.

Where personal property and real property are covered by the same contract, the lien has been held to apply to the personal property in the hands of the vendee. v. McIlwain, 215 App. Div. 644, 214 N. Y. S. 582. 

§ 53. Waiver of Vendee's Lien. 

Giarratano

The lien is based on the contract. Therefore, if the vendee rescind or sue for rescission he waives the lien. 

Davis v. Rosenweig Realty Corp., 192 N. Y. 128, 84 N. E. 

943, 20 L. R. A. (N. S.) 175, 127 Am. St. Rep. 190, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 306. 

§ 53a. Permanent Improvements on Another's Property. 

Where in good faith one made permanent improvement on another's property, in the honestly mistaken belief that he was the owner, equity may impose a lien for the value that the improvement has added to the property. The essentials of an action to enforce such a lien are: Good faith on the part of the plaintiff in making the improvements; Innocent mistake as to ownership, and; An action in equity seeking relief. Thomas v. Evans, 105 N. Y. 601, 12 N. E. 571; Lyons Nat. Bank v. Shuler, 199 N. Y. 405, 410, 92 N. E. 800; Satterlee v. Kobbe, 173 N. Y. 91, 99, 65 N. E. 952; O'Marr v. McLean, 228 App. Div. 19, 238 

N. Y. S. 443. $ 54. Other Equitable Liens.

The doctrine of equitable liens has been applied to unmined coal (Hurley v. A. T. & S. F. Ry., 213 U. S. 126, 53 L. ed. 729, 29 Sup. Ct. Rep. 466); to a judgment re-

EQUITABLE LIENS AND SERVITUDES 

47 covered in this state for negligence where the law of the state where the accident happened provided that the amount paid by way of compensation insurance in that state should be deducted from the amount of any recovery (Hartford 

Accident & Indemnity Co. v. Chartrand, 239 N. Y. 36, 145 

N. E. 274); to a case where a party has collected insurance money which, by contract, belongs to another (Cromwell v. Bklyn. Fire Ins. Co., 44 N. Y. 42, 4 Am. Rep. 641, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 292); to the assets of a corporation in favor of its creditors (Cole v. Millerton Iron 

Co., 133 N. Y. 164, 30 N. E. 847, 28 Am. St. Rep. 615; 

Hurd v. N. Y. Steam Laundry Co., 167 N. Y. 89, 60 N. E. 

327); to bonds set aside in the debtor's own safe deposit vault to be security for a debt (Sexton v. Kessler, 225 

U. S. 90, 56 L. ed. 995, 32 Sup. Ct. Rep. 657) and many others. § 55. Equitable Servitudes.

Restrictions on the use of land are examples of equitable servitudes. Although these are more properly within the scope of a work on Real Property, they will be given brief consideration under the subject of Injunctions. The restrictions do not necessarily run with the land. Other examples are set back restrictions (Tallmadge v. East River 

Savings Bank, 26 N. Y. 105); a personal agreement not to build an apartment house in a certain neighborhood (Lewis v. Gollner, 129 N. Y. 227, 29 N. E. 81, 26 Am. St. Rep. 

516, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 313); an agreement not to sell sand from land conveyed (Hodge v. Sloan, 107 N. Y. 

244, 17 N. E. 335, 1 Am. St. Rep. 816); and in general, agreements limiting the use of land purchased, particularly so as not to interfere with the business of the grantor. 

R. L. Association v. Kellogg, 141 N. Y. 348, 36 N. E. 326.

CHAPTER VII  § 56. Relief from Consequences of Accident. 

Most of the text book writers agree that it is difficult, if not impossible, to fully define accident. But, generally speaking, it may be said that equity will afford relief where by reason of an unforeseen occurrence, a party's legal rights are injured and he has no adequate remedy at law. 

The rule has been stated that equity will not grant relief to a party when the accident is the result of his own negligence. Bispham's Principles of Equity, 9th ed., sec. 175; 

Penny v. Martin, 4 Johns. Ch. 566, 569. 

§ 57. Lost Instruments. 

The advantage of seeking relief in a court of equity is marked in cases where instruments which are needed to prove title are lost. Equity can decree the re-execution of an instrument or the issuance of a new stock certificate or direct that the production of an instrument be dispensed with, and at the same time direct that the plaintiff give such security as is necessary to protect the defendant against any claim under the original instrument. The doctrine has been applied where an unrecorded deed was lost (Kent v. 

Church of St. Michael, 136 N. Y. 10, 32 N. E. 704, 18

L. R. A. 331, 32 Am. St. Rep. 693, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 319); where an insurance policy was lost, the surrender of which was a prerequisite to the issuance of a paid up policy (Wilcox v. Equitable Life Ins. Co., 173 N. Y. 

50, 65 N. E. 857, 93 Am. St. Rep. 579); and where bonds were lost (Switzerland Gen. Ins. Co. v. N. Y. C. & H. R. R. 

Co., 152 App. Div. 70, 136 N. Y. S. 726). The case last cited furnishes a good illustration of the way in which equity protects the party issuing the security against any subsequent claim arising under the lost instrument, for the 48

court refused to order the issuance of new bonds which might have caused an overissue of bonds authorized by the mortgage, but directed the issuance of a certificate of indebtedness instead. 

Where a summary remedy for the loss of stock certificates has been authorized by statute (Stock Corp. Law, secs. 75, 76), the remedy is still an equitable one. Kinnan v. Fortysecond St. Ry. Co., 140 N. Y. 183, 35 N. E. 498, Bachrach's 

Cases in Equity, p. 38. § 58.

Penalties. 

The principle underlying the relief afforded in equity against penalties for breach of contract is that where ascertainable damages will make a party whole, he ought not to require or receive more. Where an agreement provides that on the non-payment on a day certain of a sum of money, a larger sum shall be paid, or that on the non-performance of an obligation a sum of money shall be paid which will more than compensate the injured party, equity looks upon the provision as a penalty and will not enforce it. Under modern practice the defense may be set up at law without the necessity for a separate recourse to equity. This applies to equitable defenses in general. Susquehanna S. S. Co. v. 

Andersen & Co., 239 N. Y. 285, 146 N. E. 381.

The rule against the enforcement of penalties does not apply to so-called "acceleration clauses," which provide that on the non-payment of an installment the whole amount shall become due. Noyes v. Anderson, 124 N. Y. 175, 180, 26 N. E. 316, 21 Am. St. Rep. 657, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 327, 329; Bennett v. Stevenson, 53 N. Y. 508; 

Malcolm v. Allen, 49 N. Y. 448.

Since equity looks at the substance and not the form, it is of no importance that the parties themselves agree that a specified sum shall be considered liquidated damages and not a penalty. The test is whether the actual damages were in their nature uncertain and unascertainable with exactness

at the time the agreement was made, and whether the amount specified is out of all proportion to the probable loss. 

Curtis v. Van Bergh, 161 N. Y. 47, 55 N. E. 398, Bachrach's 

Cases in Equity, p. 323; Caesar v. Rubinson, 174 N. Y. 492, 67 N. E. 58; Mosler Safe Co. v. Maiden Lane S. D. Co., 199 

N. Y. 479, 93 N. E. 81, 37 L. R. A. (N. S.) 363. 

Judge Andrews in the leading case of Seidlitz v. Auerbach, 230 N. Y. 167, 129 N. E. 461, has stated the rule as follows: 

"Whether a sum is to be treated as liquidated damages or as a penalty depends upon the intent of the parties to a contract, as disclosed by the situation and by the terms of the instrument (Caesar v. Rubinson, 174 N. Y. 492, 67 

N. E. 58). Generally, whenever the damages flowing from a breach of a contract can be easily established, or where the damages fixed are plainly disproportionate to the injury, the stipulated sum will be treated as a penalty. So where a strict construction would result in an absurdity, there must be some attempt to proportion these damages to the actual loss. The parties must not lose sight of the principle of compensation.' 

A party will not be permitted to escape specific performance of a contract by the payment of a penalty if the primary intent was that the contract should be performed. 

Phoenix Ins. Co. v. Cont. Ins. Co., 87 N. Y. 400; Diamond 

Match Co. v. Roeber, 106 N. Y. 473, 486, 13 N. E. 419, 60 

Am. Rep. 464. 

§ 59. 

Forfeitures.

Where the contract provides that a defaulting party shall lose an estate or right on default, and it appears that the default was without fault or negligence on the part of the party committing it, equity either relieves against the forfeiture or refuses its aid to enforce the forfeiture. Noyes v. Anderson, 124 N. Y. 175, 26 N. E. 1107, 21 Am. St. Rep. 

657, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 327; Schieck v. Donohue, 92 App. Div. 330, 87 N. Y. S. 206; Giles v. Austin, 62

N. Y. 486; Cohen v. N. Y. Mut. Life Ins. Co., 50 N. Y. 610, 10 Am. Rep. 522.

The case of Graf v. Hope Building Corporation, 254 

N. Y. 1, 171 N. E. 884, a four-to-three decision of the Court of Appeals, has aroused considerable discussion and many are inclined to agree with the vigorous dissenting opinion of Chief Judge Cardozo. But Judge O'Brien, in his characteristically able prevailing opinion, puts the decision squarely on the ground that the negligence of the mortgagor induced the default which resulted in the forfeiture. 

In that view, the decision would seem to be clearly right. 

Both opinions should be carefully studied. 

§ 60. 

Mistake Defined. "A mistake exists when a person under some erroneous conviction of law or fact does or omits to do some act which, but for the erroneous conviction, he would not have done or omitted." Bispham's Principles of Equity, 9th ed., sec. 185.

Mistakes are classified as mistakes of fact and mistakes of law. 

§ 61. Mistakes of Fact. 

Mistakes of fact may be divided into the following classes: (a) Mistakes which prevent any agreement from being formed; (b) Mistakes which do not prevent the agreement from being formed; and (c) Mistakes in expressing the agreement. a. Mistakes of fact which prevent any agreement from being formed. When the consent of one or both parties is given under a material mistake of fact, there is no meeting of the minds and, hence, no agreement. Richardson's 

Outline of Contracts, 3d ed., sec. 43. Generally recourse to equity is unnecessary in these cases for the reason that the injured party has an adequate remedy at law. Wilcox

v. Am. Tel. & Tel. Co., 176 N. Y. 115, 68 N. E. 153, 98 

Am. St. Rep. 650; Whipple v. Brown Bros. Co., 225 N. Y. 

237, 121 N. E. 748. Equity is resorted to only when there is need for a decree of rescission or cancellation, as where there is an instrument outstanding which may create a cloud on a title or in any other situation in which the remedy at law is inadequate. A type of action illustrating relief afforded in cases of unilateral mistake is found in 

City of New York v. Dowd Lumber Co., 140 App. Div. 358, 125 N. Y. S. 394, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 336, where an offer which was accepted contained a mistake patent upon its face, and the court granted relief by refusing to allow a recovery on the contract, the defense being set up in an action at law. This followed a similar case in the 

United States Supreme Court (Moffet & Co. v. Rochester, 

178 U. S. 373, 44 L. ed. 1108, 20 Sup. Ct. Rep. 957) where the mistake was not patent on the face of the offer and yet the court granted rescission but refused reformation, holding that since the minds of the parties had not met there was no contract and hence nothing to be reformed. The 

State courts have since followed the ruling of the United 

States Supreme Court. See Harper, Inc., v. City of Newburgh, 159 App. Div. 695, 145 N. Y. S. 59, affd. 222 N. Y. 

670, 119 N. E. 1047; Martens & Co., Inc., v. City of Syracuse, 183 App. Div. 622, 171 N. Y. S. 87; Metzger v. Aetna 

Ins. Co., 227 N. Y. 411, 417, 125 N. E. 814. b. Mistakes of fact which do not prevent the agreement from being formed. A mistake of this class is illustrated in the case of Mills v. Kampfe, 202 N. Y. 46, 94 N. E. 1072, where an agreement for the purchase and sale of farm land was entered into under the mistaken belief of both parties that the farm contained about 250 acres, whereas, in fact, it contained only a little more than 233 acres. Under these circumstances the court reformed the contract and allowed a recovery for the deficiency at the average price paid per acre; and this notwithstanding the fact that the purchaser had been on the land and observed the boundaries and in

that manner had an opportunity to know of its extent. See, also, Paine v. Upton, 87 N. Y. 327, 41 Am. Rep. 371; Bush v. Hicks, 60 N. Y. 298; Thomas v. Harmon, 122 N. Y. 84, 25 N. E. 257.

C. Mistakes in expressing the agreement. It must be borne in mind that an agreement is made when the minds of the parties meet. The written instrument is intended to express that agreement. When, therefore, the written instrument does not express the agreement actually made, equity will reform the instrument to make it do so. Knobloch v. Kracke, 151 App. Div. 19, 135 N. Y. S. 381, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 359; Born v. Schrenkeisen, 110 

N. Y. 55, 17 N. E. 339; Jamaica Savings Bank v. Taylor, 72 App. Div. 567, 76 N. Y. S. 790; MacDonald v. Crissey, 215 N. Y. 609, 109 N. E. 609; Metzger v. Aetna Ins. 

Co., 227 N. Y. 411, 125 N. E. 814. 

A memorandum of an oral contract cannot be reformed to correct a mistake where the Statute of Frauds is involved, for unless the writing evidences the contract it is unenforceable and the court has no power to substitute a writing which never existed. Friedman & Co. v. Newman, 255 N. Y. 340, 174 N. E. 703. 

§ 62. Mutuality of Mistake. 

Where the action is to reform the agreement itself, it must be shown that the mistake was mutual. Otherwise, the court would be making a new agreement for the parties. 

Christopher St. R. Co. v. Twenty-third St. R. Co., 149 N. Y. 

51, 43 N. E. 538, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 662; Drachler v. Foster, 88 App. Div. 270, 84 N. Y. S. 977, Bachrach's 

Cases in Equity, p. 646; Baird v. Erie Railroad Co., 210 

N. Y. 225, 104 N. E. 614, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 

154; Born v. Schrenkeisen, 110 N. Y. 55, 17 N. E. 339; 

Curtis v. Albee, 167 N. Y. 360, 60 N. E. 660; Avery v. 

Equitable Life Assur. Soc., 117 N. Y. 451, 23 N. E. 3; 

Solomon v. North British & Merc. Ins. Co., 215 N. Y. 214, 109 N. E. 121, L. R. A. 1917 C 106; Isaacs v. Schmuck,

245 N. Y. 77, 156 N. E. 621. But where there is no mistake as to the terms of the agreement, but, through mistake of the scrivener in reducing it to writing, the instrument does not express the agreement actually made, it is not necessary to allege or prove that the mistake was mutual. That is necessary only in an action to reform the agreement itself. MacDonald v. Crissey, 215 N. Y. 609, 109 N. E. 609. A unilateral mistake may be ground for rescission but not for reformation. City of New York v. 

Dowd Lumber Co., 140 App. Div. 358, 125 N. Y. S. 394,

Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 336, and other authorities cited in sec. 61, subd. a, supra. 

§ 63. Mistakes of Law. 

The cases are not altogether in accord as to the effect of a mistake of law, running all the way from a statement that relief will not be given for a mere mistake of law to a suggestion that there is no longer any distinction between mistakes of law and mistakes of fact as a basis for equitable relief. See Greene v. Smith, 160 N. Y. 533, 55 N. E. 210; 

Millspaugh v. Cassidy, 191 App. Div. 221, 181 N. Y. S. 276,

Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 366. The better rule seems to be that where there is a mistake in expressing the agreement, the same relief will be afforded in equity whether the mistake be one of law or of fact. In either case the court will make the written instrument conform to the agreement actually made. Pitcher v. Hennessey, 48 N. Y. 415, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 361; Baird v. Erie Railroad Co., 210 N. Y. 225, 104 N. E. 614, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 654; Millspaugh v. Cassidy, supra; Maher v. Hibernia 

Ins. Co., 67 N. Y. 283. See, also, authorities cited in sec. 

61, subd. c, supra. Where, on the other hand, the instrument expresses the agreement of the parties but they are mistaken as to its legal effect, no relief will be given. This is on the theory that equity will not make a new contract for the parties. Hunt v. Rousmanier, 8 Wheat. 174; id., 1 Peters 1, 7 L. ed. 27; Lanning v. Carpenter, 48 N. Y. 408;

Farmers Loan & Trust Co. v. Winthrop, 238 N. Y. 477, 487, 144 N. E. 686, and authorities cited therein; Miller v. Carpenter, 68 App. Div. 346, 74 N. Y. S. 231.

In Pitcher v. Hennessey, supra, the Court said, at p. 424: "Sometimes it happens that parties agree * * to carry out their agreement by an instrument which, by their mistake of the law, will not effectuate their intention. In such a case equity will not reform the instrument, or substitute another instrument which will, in law, give effect to their intention, because they adopted and agreed upon the particular instrument, and equity will not compel them to execute an agreement which they never agreed to execute, and thus make an agreement for them." 

Where an act is done under an erroneous conception of the law, even though it be by the advice of counsel, equity will not relieve. Weed v. Weed, 94 N. Y. 243; Knickerbocker 

Trust Co. v. Oneonta, etc., Ry. Co., 201 N. Y. 379, 94 N. E. 

871; Treadwell v. Clark, 124 App. Div. 260, 108 N. Y. S. 

733. An attorney, however, is not permitted, by misrepresentation of law, to take advantage of his client in an agreement between them. Hungerford Brass & C. Co. v. Brigham, 47 Misc. 240, 95 N. Y. S. 867. This is an illustration of the rule that where there is a mistake of law on one side and fraud or inequitable conduct on the other, equity will give relief. Haviland v. Willets, 141 N. Y. 35, 35 N. E. 958, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 350. 

§ 64. Recovery of Money Paid Under Mistake of Law or 

Fact.

The rule is that money paid under a mistake of law cannot be recovered back at law or in equity. Newburgh Sav. 

Bank v. Town of Woodbury, 173 N. Y. 55, 65 N. E. 858,

Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 346. An extreme case is 

Doll v. Earle, 59 N. Y. 638, where a payment of money was made on the faith of a decision of the Supreme Court of the United States, holding that a payment must be made in

gold and could not be made in legal tender since the contract was made before the passage of the Legal Tender Acts. By a subsequent decision the court reversed its ruling, but it was held that the difference could not be recovered back, the payment being made through a mistake of law. An exception to this rule seems to have been established in cases of payments of public moneys (Supervisors v. Ellis, 59 N. Y. 620), and payments to an officer of the court (Gillig v. Grant, 23 App. Div. 596, 49 N. Y. S. 78).

Money paid under a mistake of fact may be recovered back if the payee was not entitled to receive the money and his circumstances have not so changed through its receipt as to render it unjust to compel him to refund. Hathaway v. County of Delaware, 185 N. Y. 368, 78 N. E. 153, 113 

Am. St. Rep. 909; Irving Bank v. Wetherald, 36 N. Y. 335; 

Mt. Morris Bank v. Twenty-third Ward Bank, 172 N. Y. 

244, 64 N. E. 810; Continental Bank v. Tradesmen's Nat. 

Bank, 173 N. Y. 272, 65 N. E. 1108; Allen v. Fourth Nat. 

Bank, 59 N. Y. 12. On the other hand, if the mistake was brought about by the fraud of a third person and the payee receives the money in good faith in the regular course of business and for a valuable consideration, it cannot be recovered back. Ball v. Shepard, 202 N. Y. 247, 95 N. E. 719. 

§ 65. Compromise. 

Where disputed claims are compromised, the compromise will be upheld even though subsequent events turn out otherwise than the parties expected. Sears v. Grand Lodge, 163 N. Y. 374, 57 N. E. 618, 50 L. R. A. 204, Bachrach's 

Cases in Equity, p. 343; Miles v. N. Y. C. R. R. Co., 195 

App. Div. 748, 185 N. Y. S. 941.

CHAPTER VIII FRAUD § 66. Definition of Fraud. 

All the text book writers agree that there is no definition which covers the entire field of fraud and most of them quote Lord Chancellor Hardwicke's statement made in 1745:

See "The court very wisely hath never laid down any general rule beyond which it will not go, lest other means for avoiding the equity of the courts should be found out." 

Richardson's Outline of Contracts, 3d ed., sec. 67.

Probably the best considered definition is that given by 

Pomeroy in his Equity Jurisprudence, 2d ed., sec. 873: "Fraud in equity includes all willful or intentional acts, omissions and concealments which involve a breach of either legal or equitable duty, trust or confidence and are injurious to another or by which an undue or unconscientious advantage over another is obtained." 

§ 67. 

Classification of Frauds.

The classification of frauds usually given is that stated by Lord Hardwicke, in Earl of Chesterfield v. Janssen, White & Tudor's Leading Cases in Equity, 4th Am. ed., vol. 1, p. 773: (1) Frauds which are actual, arising from facts and circumstances of imposition. (2) Frauds apparent from the intrinsic nature and subject of the bargain itself, such as no man in his senses and not under delusion would make on the one hand, and, as no honest and fair man would accept on the other. (3) Fraud presumed from the circumstances and condition of the parties contracting. This goes further than the rule of law, which is that fraud must be proved, not presumed, and is established to prevent taking surreptitious advantage of the weakness or necessity of another. 57

(4) Fraud on third persons not parties to the transaction.

The first division is classified as actual fraud; the second and third as constructive fraud. 

§ 68. Misrepresentations Not Amounting to Actual Fraud. 

Before considering actual fraud, attention should be called to a class of cases in which rescission will be allowed although the requirements of an action for deceit are not met. Relief will be given although misstatements and misrepresentations were honestly made with no intent to deceive. In other words it is not necessary to prove knowledge of the falsity of the statements, or scienter. The grounds for equitable relief in such cases have been variously stated. Some writers have expressed the view that these are, in reality, cases of mutual mistake. Others claim that they fall under the head of constructive fraud, arising from the relation of the parties. But whatever the reason, the rule is firmly established that an innocent misrepresentation may be sufficient ground for a suit in equity for rescission. Hammond v. Pennock, 61 N. Y. 145, Bachrach's

Cases in Equity, p. 381. In the language of the Court, in 

Bloomquist v. Farson, 222 N. Y. 375, 380, 118 N. E. 855: "An action may be maintained in equity to rescind a transaction which has been consummated through misrepresentation of material facts not amounting to fraud. Unlike an action at law for damages, intentional misstatements need not be proved."

Equity acts on the principle that it is inequitable to permit a person to keep property which he has acquired through misrepresentation even though made with no intent to deceive, especially where the parties are not on an equal footing. 

In Leary v. Geller, 224 N. Y. 56, 58, 120 N. E. 31, the 

Court said: "Equity will, in a proper case, avoid and set aside a trans- 1

FRAUD 

59 action induced or procured through material misrepresentations and false statements, although the statements and representations were honestly made with no intent to deceive." * * * 

And, in Carr v. Watertown Nat. Bank, 167 N. Y. 375, 379, 60 N. E. 649, the Court used the following language: "It is quite immaterial that there may have been no intention to actually defraud. 

In such cases equity will, upon the seasonable application of a party, avoid the transaction and this right is conceded, without reference to any actual fraud." 

In Seneca Wire & Mfg. Co. v. Leach, 247 N. Y. 1, 159

N. E. 700, the court applied the same doctrine to an action at law, holding that an innocent misrepresentation is sufficient to avoid a transaction at law. 

This makes simple the rule as to rescission at law and in equity. If it is desired solely to recover back money paid as a result of an innocent misrepresentation, the action must be at law; if something else is needed, such as the reconveyance of real property parted with, the action must be in equity.

The essentials of the action under consideration may be stated to be: (a) Misrepresentation of a material fact. (b) Reliance upon the misrepresentation to the party's damage. (c) Offer to return what has been received promptly upon the discovery of the falsity of the representation. (d) No material change in the situation of the parties which would make it inequitable to grant relief. 

§ 69. Elements of Actual Fraud. 

The elements which must be proved in an action at law for deceit are: (1) The representation must be of a material fact and not a matter of opinion;

(2) must be false; (3) must be known to be false or made with reckless disregard of whether it is true or false; (4) must be made with intent that it be acted upon; (5) must be relied upon to the party's injury. 

Richardson's Outline of Contracts, 3d ed., sec. 68. 

§ 70. 

Misrepresentation of Material Fact. 

A material fact is one which induces the making of the contract. 

Opinion as distinguished from fact embraces expressions of expectation, intention, prediction or belief and promises. 

Richardson's Outline of Contracts, 3d ed., sec. 71. The rule is the same at law and in equity. property is matter of 

Meritas Realty Co. v.

A statement as to the value of opinion; as to cost, matter of fact. 

Farley, 166 App. Div. 420, 151 N. Y. S. 1052, affd. 224 N. Y. 

671, 121 N. E. 879, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 373. A statement that stock is fully paid is a statement of fact; that it is of great value and stockholders will receive large dividends are expressions of opinion. Van Slochem v. Villard, 207 N. Y. 587, 101 N. E. 467; Hubbell v. Meigs, 50 

N. Y. 480.

A misstatement of present intention is a misrepresentation of fact. Adams v. Gillig, 199 N. Y. 314, 92 N. E. 670, 32 L. R. A. (N. S.) 127; Ritzwoller v. Lurie, 225 N. Y. 464, 122 N. E. 634. Such a misstatement binds the principal of an agent acting within the scope of his authority. Moore v. Abbey, 213 App. Div. 787, 210 N. Y. S. 766. 

§ 71. Falsity of Representation. 

The false representation may consist of:

(a) 

Actual false representation. (b) Active or artful concealment. (c) 

Suppression of the truth amounting to falsehood;

FRAUD 

61 i. e., withholding certain information, thereby making that which is stated absolutely false.

The rules are the same at law and in equity. For illustrations, see authorities cited in Richardson's Outline of 

Contracts, 3d ed., sec. 70.

Neither at law nor in equity will the courts permit a partial statement of the truth or a statement which is misleading, although literally true, to be used to deceive when the parties are not on an equal footing. Von Au v. Magenheimer, 126 App. Div. 257, 110 N. Y. S. 629, affd. 196 N. Y. 

510, 89 N. E. 1114. 

§ 72. Knowledge of Falsity by Party Making Statement. 

The representation must be made with knowledge of its falsity or with reckless disregard as to whether it be true or false. Richardson's Outline of Contracts, 3d ed., sec. 73, and authorities therein cited.

As shown in section 68, supra, in an action for rescission it is not essential to show knowledge of falsity but equity will, a fortiori, act in cases of actual fraud where it is shown. 

§ 73. Intent To Deceive. 

As shown in section 68, supra, it is not always necessary to show intent to deceive, but when it exists, in addition to the other requisites, equity will, of course, act. 

§ 74. Reliance upon Representation to Party's Injury. 

The rule is the same at law and in equity. 

If the party does not rely upon the misrepresentation he cannot be deceived. While the party deceived must be injured, it is not necessary that the party making the representation profit thereby. Laska v. Harris, 215 N. Y. 554, 109 N. E. 599.

The representation need not necessarily be made to the party injured. It has been held, for example, that where

a prospectus was issued to the public generally, any one into whose hands it came was entitled to rely on it. Lehman- 

Charley v. Bartlett, 135 App. Div. 674, 120 N. Y. S. 501, affd. 202 N. Y. 524, 95 N. E. 1125. And equity has even gone so far as to compel a party to make good representations made to the plaintiff's mother before the plaintiff was born and which induced the plaintiff's mother to marry the plaintiff's father. Piper v. Hoard, 107 N. Y. 73, 13 N. E. 

626, 1 Am. St. Rep. 789, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p 404. 

§ 75. 

Remedies-Election. 

The remedies for fraud have been classified by the Court of Appeals as follows: 

"(1) an action for deceit in tort: (2) in proper cases, an informal rescission of the contract at law and a recovery of what has been parted with thereunder; (3) in proper cases, a formal decree of rescission or cancellation in equity and a recovery of what has been parted with thereunder; (4) a defense against the enforcement of the executory promise induced by the fraud.

The election of one of these remedies is a waiver of the others. * * * If rescission is the remedy selected, it must be in whole and not in part. If there be an affirmance, it must be of all the terms and conditions of the transaction." Merry Realty Co. v. Shamokin & Hollis R. E. Co., 230 N. Y. 316, 323, 130 N. E. 306, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 636.

The bringing of an action at law which was barred by the statute of limitations has been held not to be an election barring equitable relief. Schenck v. State Line Telephone 

Co., 238 N. Y. 308, 144 N. E. 592, 35 A. L. R. 1149. This is an illustration of the rule that if the remedy chosen be insufficient or inadequate or useless, the plaintiff is not barred from taking other timely methods to obtain his rights. Clark v. Kenny, 243 N. Y. 295.

FRAUD 

63 The injured party must act promptly and offer to restore what he has received; otherwise, he will be held to have affirmed the contract. Hammond v. Pennock, 61 N. Y. 145, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 381; Baker v. Lever, 67 N. Y. 

304, 23 Am. Rep. 117, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 393.

But the retention of property for six months has been held not to amount to an affirmance of the contract which barred rescission in equity. Weigel v. Cook, 237 N. Y. 136, 142 

N. E. 444. The receipt and retention of dividends on stock after rescission of a contract for the purchase of such stock has been held to be a ratification of the sale and waiver of the rescission. Brennan v. National Equitable Investment 

Co., 247 N. Y. 486, 160 N. E. 924, rearg. den. 248 N. Y. 560, 162 N. E. 524. 

§ 76. 

Trusts Ex Maleficio.

Where a person through fraud attempts to keep the sole ownership of property which in equity and good conscience belongs to another, equity affords a remedy for the fraud by enforcing a trust, the person holding the title to the property being called a trustee ex maleficio. One case illustrating this kind of trust has already been cited, in which the owner of property who had obtained the same through a false representation to the plaintiff's mother that the property would belong to the plaintiff, was compelled to make good the representation. Piper v. Hoard, 107 N. Y. 

73, 13 N. E. 626, 1 Am. St. Rep. 789, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 404. Another illustration is Matter of O'Hara, 95 

N. Y. 403, 47 Am. Rep. 53, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 

209, in which it appeared that the testatrix left property to her doctor, lawyer and priest absolutely, pursuant to an agreement that they would devote it to certain uses which would be illegal if a trust had been established. The court held that to allow them to so use the property would be permitting that to be done indirectly which could not legally be done directly. It also held that for these men to retain the property for themselves would be a fraud upon the testa-

wwww trix, who had no such intention. The court, therefore, imposed a trust for the benefit of the heirs at law and next of kin. For further illustrations, see Goldsmith v. Goldsmith, 145 N. Y. 313, 39 N. E. 1067, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 410; Ryan v. Dox, 34 N. Y. 307, 90 Am. Dec. 696, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 412; Wheeler v. Reynolds, 66 

N. Y. 227, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 421; Falk v. 

Hoffman, 233 N. Y. 199, 135 N. E. 243.

So equity will impose a trust to compel restitution to the owner of stolen property. Fur & Wool Trading Co. v. Fox, 245 N. Y. 215, 156 N. E. 670; Newton v. Porter 69 N. Y. 

133. The same rule applies to money obtained by forgery. 

Jaffe v. Weld, 220 N. Y. 443, 116 N. E. 73. 

Before leaving the subject of actual fraud, attention should be called to Quaid v. Ratkowsky, 183 App. Div. 428, 170 N. Y. S. 812, affd. 224 N. Y. 624, 121 N. E. 887, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 400, in which the court refused to allow a separate corporate entity to cloak fraud; and David 

Gilmour Door Co. v. Shea, 217 N. Y. 697, 112 N. E. 1057, decided on the dissenting opinion of Mr. Justice Laughlin, reported in 150 App. Div. 239, 242, 134 N. Y. S. 919,

Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 397, in which the court intervened to remove an obstruction to legal process in the enforcement of a judgment created by a fraudulent conspiracy. § 77.

Classification of Constructive Frauds. 

As shown in section 67, supra, constructive frauds are generally divided into two classes. Under the first class; viz., fraud apparent from the intrinsic nature and subject of the bargain itself, we shall consider the effect of inadequacy of consideration and of illegality of subject matter. Under the second class; viz., fraud presumed from the circumstances and conditions of the parties contracting, we shall consider transactions with persons under disability or wholly or partially mentally incapacitated and transactions between persons in fiduciary relationships.

FRAUD 

65 

§ 78. Inadequacy of Consideration. 

The general rule is that mere inadequacy of price, unless it is so great as to "shock the conscience and confound the judgment of any man of common sense," is not of itself proof of fraud, though it will be considered in connection with other matters when the parties do not stand on an equal footing or there is a fiduciary relationship. Osgood v. Franklin, 2 Johns. Ch. 1, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 

427. A court of equity will not, therefore, ordinarily set aside a transaction on the ground of inadequacy of consideration alone. Parmelee v. Cameron, 41 N. Y. 392. It may, however, in the exercise of its discretion, refuse to compel specific performance of such a contract. Margraf v. Muir, 57 N. Y. 155, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 683. 

§ 79. Illegality of Subject Matter. 

When a contract is made, the purpose of which is prohibited by statute or against public policy, equity will intervene where the parties are not in pari delicto and the contract is not malum in se and aid the injured party by setting aside the contract and restoring him to his original position. Where contracts are malum in se equity leaves the parties where it finds them. Duval v. Wellman, 124 N. Y. 156, 26 N. E. 343, Bachrach's cases in Equity, p. 429. In the case cited, the rule was applied to a marriage brokerage contract.

The general subject of contracts which are void by reason of the illegality of their subject matter belongs to the subject of Contracts. See Richardson's Outline of

Contracts, 3d ed., chapters ix and x and authorities therein cited. It is within the scope of this outline only to state a few of the principles applicable to the equitable relief which may be had in such cases.

Equity will not interfere where there is a complete and adequate remedy at law. If, therefore, the invalidity can as well be interposed as a defense to an action on the contract, equity will not interfere.

Section 373 of the General Business Law declares that all usurious contracts are void and states that upon proof that they are usurious the court "shall declare the same to be void and enjoin any prosecution thereon and order the same to be surrendered and cancelled." The courts have held that this did not change the rule stated above that relief in equity would not be given where there was an adequate remedy at law. Thus, the court refused to adjudge a past due note illegal and void on the ground of usury. Reiner v. Galinger, 151 App. Div. 711, 136 N. Y.

S. 205. On the other hand, a mortgagor, owning the fee of land covered by a usurious mortgage, may maintain an equitable action to have the mortgage surrendered and the record cancelled as a cloud on title. Myers v. Wheeler, 24 App. Div. 327, 48 N. Y. S. 611, affd. 161 N. Y. 637, 57 N. E. 1118. And an action in equity will lie to cancel a usurious chattel mortgage where the mortgagee has taken possession of the property and thus avoided bringing the instrument before the court, thereby depriving the mortgagor of both his property and an opportunity to establish at law the illegality of the transaction. Hager v. 

Arland, 81 Misc. 421, 143 N. Y. S. 388.

The formation of a corporation for the express purpose of having a borrower which could not plead usury was held to be proper in Jenkins v. Moyse, 254 N. Y. 419, 172 N. E. 

521, the court holding that this was not an evasion, but a strict following of the usury law.

Under the maxim that he who seeks equity must do equity, one bringing a suit in equity to cancel a usurious instrument was compelled to repay or offer to repay the amount actually loaned. The borrower is now exempt by statute. Gen. Bus. Law, sec. 377. Anyone else is still subject to the application of the principle. Vilas v. Jones, 1 N. Y. 274; Matter of Fishel, 198 Fed. Rep. 464, 117 C.

C. A. 224. The courts have given a strict construction to the word "borrower" and have held that it does not include his heirs, devisees, personal representatives (Bucking

FRAUD 

67 ham v. Corning, 91 N. Y. 525), his surety (Allerton v. 

Belden, 49 N. Y. 373) or a receiver in bankruptcy (Rice v. 

Schenck, 189 App. Div. 877, 179 N. Y. S. 335, affd. 228 N. 

Y. 561, 127 N. E. 920). In Matter of Fishel, supra, the 

Court said, at p. 467: "Although the borrower and his privies can attack usurious agreements, only the borrower can do so free from the condition of repaying the amount actually loaned with legal interest. This is the difference made between defending and attacking on the ground of usury. The 

Court of Appeals of New York has strictly confined the privilege of attacking without repaying the amount of the loan with legal interest to the actual borrower." 

Equity is also used to enforce the United States statutes prohibiting monopolies or undue restraint of trade. Under the Sherman Act, sec. 4, the United States courts are authorized to enjoin violations of the law, and under other acts the Federal Trade Commission may apply to a court of equity to enforce its orders. These cases are merely noted here in passing as they are somewhat beyond the scope of this outline. 

§ 80. Transactions with Persons under Disability or Men tally Incapacitated. 

Transactions with persons under disability may be illustrated by contracts with infants. Such contracts are voidable both at law and in equity. In some states married women are without capacity to bind themselves by contract although their contracts are sometimes enforced when dealing with their separate property. 

Transactions with persons wholly or partially mentally incapacitated include transactions with persons insane, intoxicated, senile or under actual duress. 

In these cases, the rules are the same at law and in equity and belong to the subject of Contracts.

Equity will also grant relief where a person is ignorant of his own rights and acts without professional advice,

and advantage is taken of this ignorance to exact an unfair contract. This is illustrated in the case of Ryon v. Wanamaker, 116 Misc. 91, 190 N. Y. S. 250, affd. 202 App. Div. 

848, 194 N. Y. S. 977, affd. 235 N. Y. 545, 139 N. E. 728. In that case, the plaintiff's wife had incurred large indebtedness at the defendant's store just before she deserted the plaintiff. The plaintiff had a conference with defendant at which defendant's employees and lawyers advised him that he was responsible for all debts contracted by his wife, whether reasonable for her support or not. He executed certain assignments of mortgages to secure the wife's indebtedness. In an action to set aside these assignments, the court granted judgment for the plaintiff, stating that unfair advantage had been taken of him. While the court has based its decision on the doctrine of misrepresentations not amounting to fraud, it would seem that the case falls under the principle above stated. 

In the appropriately named case of Allcard v. Skinner, 36 

Ch. Div. (Eng. 1887) 145, it appeared that Miss Allcard joined a Protestant institution known as the "Sisters of the Poor" which was under the control of a lady superior. The rules provided that no external advice should be received without the consent of the lady superior. The lady superior then induced Miss Allcard to turn over to her large sums of money and stocks. After Miss Allcard left the institution, she brought suit to recover. The court held that she had a good cause of action but that it was barred by laches. 

See, also, Watson v. Holmes, 80 Misc. 48, 140 N. Y. S. 

727; Barnard v. Gantz, 140 N. Y. 249, 35 N. E. 430. 

§ 81. Transactions between Persons in Fiduciary Rela tionships. 

The Court of Appeals, in Cowee v. Cornell, 75 N. Y. 91, 99, 31 Am. Rep. 428, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 449, has admirably stated the rule applicable to transactions between persons in a fiduciary relationship as follows: "It may be stated as universally true that fraud vitiates

FRAUD 

69 all contracts, but as a general thing, it is not presumed but must be proved by the party seeking to relieve himself from an obligation on that ground. Whenever, however, the relations between the contracting parties appear to be of such a character as to render it certain that they do not deal on terms of equality but that either on the one side from superior knowledge of the matter derived from a fiduciary relation, or from overmastering influence, or on the other from weakness, dependence, or trust justifiably reposed, unfair advantage in a transaction is rendered probable, there the burden is shifted, the transaction is presumed void, and it is incumbent upon the stronger party to show affirmatively that no deception was practiced, no undue influence was used, and that all was fair, open, voluntary and well understood *. The law presumes in the case of guardian and ward, trustee and cestui que trust, attorney and client, and perhaps, physician and patient, from the relation of the parties itself that their situation is unequal and of the character I have defined; and that relation appearing itself throws the burden upon the trustee, guardian or attorney of showing the fairness of his dealings. But while the doctrine is without doubt to be extended to many other relations of trust, confidence or inequality, the trust and confidence, or the superiority on one side and weakness on the other must be proved in each of these cases; the law does not presume them from the fact, for instance, that one party is a grandfather and old and the other a grandson and young, or that one is an employer and the other an employee. The question as to parties so situated is a question of fact dependent upon the circumstances in each case. There is no presumption of inequality either way from these relations merely." 

The doctrine has been very strictly applied in cases of guardian and ward, attorney and client and trustee and beneficiary. Illustrations of transactions between trustee and beneficiary have already been given. See sec. 38a, supra.

Transactions between guardian and ward are illustrated by the case of Shiverick v. Bonsall, 185 App. Div. 338, 173

N. Y. S. 90, in which it appeared that plaintiff's stepfather and guardian induced plaintiff to invest $400,000 in a manufacturing enterprise shortly after she became of age. The court stated that if an action had been seasonably brought the transaction might have been set aside but that the right had been lost through repeated ratifications. 

The principle was reiterated in another action brought by a second stepdaughter against the same defendant. 

Kineon v. Bonsall, 194 App. Div. 110, at p. 118, 185 N. Y. 

S. 694, affd. 232 N. Y. 624, 134 N. E. 598.

The court applies the rule strictly to the relation of attorney and client where the attorney uses information secured in his confidential capacity for his own advantage. 

Bingham v. Sheldon, 101 App. Div. 48, 91 N. Y. S. 917; 

Nesbit v. Lockman, 34 N. Y. 167; Moller v. Pickard, 232 

N. Y. 271, 133 N. E. 887.

The rule as to gifts by will appears to be less strict, although it has been held that the relationship of the parties, the age, mental and physical condition of the decedent and other circumstances, taken together, cast upon the proponent of a will the burden of satisfying the court that the will was the free, untrammeled and intelligent expression of the wishes and intention of the testatrix. 

Matter of Smith, 95 N. Y. 516, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 453.

In respect to transactions between parent and child, the weight of authority supports the rule that the mere relationship alone is not sufficient to throw the burden on the party receiving the property to justify the transaction but that additional circumstances such as the extreme youth and inexperience of the child or weakness of intellect, feebleness or old age on the part of the parent may be enough to raise the presumption of fraud. Hunter v. 

McCammon, 119 App. Div. 326, 104 N. Y. S. 402, Bachrach's 

Cases in Equity, p. 445; Leary v. Corvin, 181 N. Y. 222,

FRAUD 

71 

73 N. E. 984, 106 Am. St. Rep. 542; Wood v. Rabe, 96 N. Y. 

414, 48 Am. Rep. 640; Goldsmith v. Goldsmith, 145 N. Y. 

313, 39 N. E. 1067; Allen v. La Vaud, 213 N. Y. 322, 107 

N. E. 570, Harrington v. Schiller, 231 N. Y. 278, 132 

N. E. 89.

Somewhat the same rule applies to the relation of husband and wife. Where the court finds that the wife's property rights are injured through oppressive methods, even though the procedure be according to the letter of the law, equity will give relief. Two late cases strikingly illustrate this doctrine. In Byrnes v. Owen, 243 N. Y. 211, 153 N. E. 

51, equity set aside a foreclosure sale conducted in accordance with the rules of law because it found that the sale was part of a conspiracy to deprive the wife of her dower right. 

In Scheinberg v. Scheinberg, 249 N. Y. 277, 164 N. E. 98, equity refused to enforce an agreement entered into upon the advice of the wife's own attorneys because it found unfair methods.

It is impossible to formulate any general rule as to what must be shown in addition to the relationship in order to have equity intervene, since each case is decided upon its own facts. But through them all, runs evidence of oppression or unfair dealing with inequality of position. 

Another class of cases are those where property is parted with on the faith of an oral promise induced by the confidential relationship. In these cases equity will not allow the 

Statute of Frauds to be used as an instrument of fraud. 

Thus in Jeremiah v. Pitcher, 26 App. Div. 402, affd. 163 

N. Y. 574, 57 N. E. 1113, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 141, a vendor conveyed property to his daughter on an oral agreement that she should convey to his nominee. He collected the rents, paid the taxes, water rates, etc., and it was held the combination of the confidential relationship, the oral agreement and the acts evidencing it were sufficient to compel her to keep her promise. 

In Foreman v. Foreman, 251 N. Y. 237, 167 N. E. 428, a husband paid the consideration and had title taken in his inTas

wife's name under her oral agreement to convey to him. 

The husband collected the rents, paid the taxes, insurance, interest and cost of improvements and the court held that the oral agreement must be kept, as otherwise a fraud would be perpetrated. See also, Sinclair v. Purdy, 235 N. Y. 

245, 253, 139 N. E. 255; Gallagher v. Gallagher, 135, App. 

Div. 457, affd. 202 N. Y. 572, 96 N. E. 1115. In these cases, "it is not the promise alone nor the breach alone, but unjust enrichment under cover of the relation of confidence which puts the court in motion." Cardozo, J., in Sinclair v. Purdy, supra.

The relation of principal and agent is analogous to that of trustee and beneficiary, and the agent is prohibited from acquiring any personal advantage. Gardner v. Ogden, 22 

N. Y. 327, 78 Am. Dec. 192; Heckscher v. Edenborn, 203 

N. Y. 210, 96 N. E. 441; United States v. Carter, 217 U. S. 

286, 54 L. ed. 769, 30 Sup. Ct. Rep. 515. The same rule applies to the relation of director and stockholder, Godley v. 

Crandall-Godley Co., 212 N. Y. 121, 105 N. E. 818, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 436; to that of partners, Butler v. Prentiss, 158 N. Y. 49, 52 N. E. 652; to that of coadventurers, Meinhard v. Salmon, 249 N. Y. 458, 164 N. E. 545, and other relationships. (See Sec. 38a ante.) § 82. 

Fraud on Third Parties.

The cases which will be discussed in the following sections, under the classification of fraud on third parties, differ from those previously discussed in that, as a general rule, neither party to the transaction, as against the other, is permitted to set the transaction aside. That right belongs to some third party whose rights are affected. 

§ 83. Transfers in Fraud of Creditors. 

Where property is transferred with intent to defraud creditors, a creditor may have the transfer set aside and the property applied in satisfaction of his claim. While such transfers were voidable at common law, the relief

FRAUD 

73 afforded at the present time rests principally, if not entirely, upon statute. It should be borne in mind that the remedies at law and in equity are concurrent. The creditor may levy execution directly upon the land, as was done in the case of Smith v. Reid, 134 N. Y. 568, 31 N. E. 1082,

Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 457, or may bring his action in equity to set the transaction aside. As the Court said, in Hillyer v. LeRoy, 179 N. Y. 369, 375, 72 N. E. 237, 103 Am. St. Rep. 919: "The property of a debtor, which has been transferred by him in fraud of creditors, still remains, as to them, the debtor's property and the lien of the creditors' judgment attaches to the real estate. The judgment creditor may enforce his judgment by a sale of the land under execution; or he may bring an action to remove the obstruction caused by the debtor's fraudulent act and proceed to enforce his judgment by a sale of the land, unembarrassed by the cloud of the transfer." 

And in Kulp & Sons Co. v. Irr, 210 App. Div. 587, 588, 206 N. Y. S. 700, the Court used the following language: "A conveyance by a debtor in fraud of his creditors is subject to attack both at law and in equity. After a sale of the debtor's property under execution, the purchaser, who is not in possession, may maintain a possessory action. 

The purchaser may also maintain an action in equity to set aside the fraudulent conveyance."

In 1925 there was enacted in New York State the Uni form Fraudulent Conveyances Act. Debtor & Creditor Law, 

Art. 10. By that law, every conveyance made, or obligation incurred, by a person who is or will be thereby rendered insolvent is fraudulent as to creditors, present and future, if made without a fair consideration as defined by the act. 

For example, a conveyance by one engaged or about to engage in business which leaves him with an unreasonably small capital or by one about to incur debts when he intends or believes that he will incur debts beyond his ability to pay as they mature, if made without a fair consideration

is fraudulent. There is an analogous provision as to the conveyance of partnership property. Debtor & Creditor 

Law, secs. 273-277. A creditor whose claim has matured may, as against everyone except a purchaser for a fair consideration or one claiming under him, (a) have the conveyance set aside or obligation annulled to the extent necessary to satisfy his claim; or (b) disregard the conveyance and attach or levy execution upon the property conveyed. 

A person who, without actual fraudulent intent, has given less than a fair consideration for the conveyance or obligation may retain the property or obligation as security for repayment. Debtor & Creditor Law, sec. 278.

A creditor whose claims have not matured may proceed in a court of competent jurisdiction against any person against whom he could have proceeded had his claim matured and the court may, (a) restrain the defendant from disposing of his property; (b) (c) appoint a receiver to take charge of his property; set aside the conveyance or annul the obligation; (d) make any order which the circumstances of the case may require. Debtor & Creditor Law, sec. 279.

The law also provides that every conveyance made and obligation incurred with actual intent, as distinguished from intent presumed by law, to hinder, delay or defraud either present or future creditors is fraudulent as to those creditors.

There have been few authoritative decisions in New York construing this law. 

In American Surety Co. v. Conner, 251 N. Y. 1, 166 N. E. 

783, the court held that the statute had abrogated the old equity rule that a creditor must exhaust his remedies at law before resorting to equity. See also, Brody v. Pecoraro, 250 N. Y. 56, 164 N. E. 741.

FRAUD 

75 Prior to the enactment of this law, it was held that a voluntary conveyance by a person indebted was presumptively fraudulent as to existing creditors. Kerker v. Levy, 206 

N. Y. 109, 99 N. E. 181. This presumption could be rebutted by showing that the debtor retained sufficient property to pay his debts. The burden was on the defendant to show this. Ga Nun v. Palmer, 216 N. Y. 603, 111 N. E. 

223. Where there was actual intent to defraud, the fact that assets were retained was immaterial. Garstairs v. 

Spear, 201 App. Div. 418, 421, 194 N. Y. S. 134, and authorities therein cited. Where a valuable consideration was paid, proof was required not only of a fraudulent intent on the part of the vendor, but also of a fraudulent intent on the part of the vendee or that he had notice of the vendor's fraudulent intent. Starin v. Kelly, 88 N. Y. 418; Frank v. 

Von Bayer, 236 N. Y. 473, 141 N. E. 920; Billings v. Russell, 101 N. Y. 226, 4 N. E. 531, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 463; Block v. Amsden, 108 Misc. 318, 323, 177 N. Y. 

S. 604, modified and affd. 196 App. Div. 915, 187 N. Y. S. 

927, affd. 232 N. Y. 620, 134 N. E. 596. This need not be shown by direct proof but circumstances may be shown and proper and legitimate inferences drawn from the whole transaction. Starin v. Kelly, supra. A conveyance with intent to defraud creditors places the burden upon the grantee to show that he paid the consideration without knowledge of the fraudulent intent. Emmi v. Patane, 128 

Misc. 901, 220 N. Y. S. 495.

By section 34 of the Personal Property Law, a transfer of personal property made in trust for the use of the person making it is void as against the existing or subsequent creditors of such person. Under this section, it has been held that a bill of sale given for the future support of the vendor is void as to his creditors. Cullin v. Ryder, 44 Misc. 

485, 89 N. Y. S. 465, affd. 188 N. Y. 574, 80 N. E. 1107.

It has been held that such a transfer is void as against creditors irrespective of any intent to defraud. Young v. Hermans, 66 N. Y. 374.

Under section 94 of the Real Property Law, a grant of real property to one person, the consideration being paid by another, is presumed fraudulent as against the existing creditors of the person paying the consideration and, unless a fraudulent intent is disproved, a trust results in favor of such creditors to an extent necessary to satisfy their just demands. The right secured to creditors by this section is enforceable only in equity. Ocean Nat. Bank v. Olcott, 46 

N. Y. 12, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 469. The presumption that the transfer is fraudulent may be overcome by showing that the one paying the consideration retained ample property to pay his debts and that an inability to meet his obligations was not, and could not reasonably have been supposed to have been in his mind. Dunlap v. Hawkins, 59 N. Y. 342. The creditors' remedy is not to sue in equity to set aside the conveyance, but to sue to enforce the resulting trust. Flower City Brewing Co. v. Edwards, 190 

App. Div. 203, 179 N. Y. S. 887. 

Under section 44 of the Personal Property Law, a sale in bulk of stocks of merchandise and fixtures is void as against creditors of the assignor unless certain steps are taken. This law has been held to be constitutional. Klein v. Maravelas, 219 N. Y. 383, 114 N. E. 809, L. R. A. 1917 E 549.

Under sections 37 of the Personal Property Law and 265 

of the Real Property Law, the question of existence of fraudulent intent in cases arising under those laws is a question of fact and not of law.

Under section 39 of the Personal Property Law, a transfer void against creditors is also void against the heirs, successors, personal representatives or assignees of such creditors. A similar provision of the Real Property Law (sec. 264) was repealed in 1925. 

§ 84. Fraud in Relation to Composition Agreements. 

Where a debtor makes a composition agreement with his creditors, a secret agreement by the debtor with one or more of his creditors, by which they secure more than the others

FRAUD 

77 as a condition of their joining in the composition agreement, is void. If the secret agreement is executory, it cannot be enforced against the debtor in law or in equity. If executed, it avoids the composition agreement as to the innocent creditors. Klaw v. Famous Players-Lasky Corp., 207 App. 

Div. 211, 201 N. Y. S. 691, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 

474, affd. 239 N. Y. 592, 147 N. E. 209; Glens Falls Nat. 

Bank v. Van Nostrand, 41 Misc. 526, 85 N. Y. S. 50, affd. 

103 App. Div. 598, 92 N. Y. S. 1125; White v. Kuntz, 107 

N. Y. 518, 14 N. E. 423, 1 Am. St. Rep. 886; Hanover Nat. 

Bank v. Blake, 142 N. Y. 404, 37 N. E. 519, 27 L. R. A. 33. 

§ 85. Fraud on Marital Rights. 

The leading case on fraud on marital rights is Youngs v. 

Carter, 10 Hun 194, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 479, in which it appeared that the prospective bridegroom conveyed nearly all his real estate to his two daughters by a prior marriage, four days before his marriage to plaintiff. It was held that this was a fraud on her expectation of an inchoate right of dower. See, also, Graham v. Graham, 143 N. Y. 

573, 38 N. E. 722.

CHAPTER IX PRIORITIES § 86. Conflicting Equities. 

In considering which of two or more adverse equitable interests in the same subject matter has priority, effect must be given to two equitable maxims: (1) Between equal equities priority of time will prevail. (2) Between equal equities the law will prevail. 

§ 87. 

Priority of Time. 

There has been some criticism of the form of the first maxim stated in the preceding section and the tendency has been to adopt the language of the English Court, in Rice v. 

Rice, 2 Drew 73. In that case there was a conveyance of land without the receipt of the purchase money. Nevertheless, the deeds were delivered to the grantee and receipt of the money was indorsed on the deeds. The grantee afterwards borrowed money on the security of the property, depositing the title deeds with the lender and thus creating an equitable mortgage. The court held that by reason of the fact that the receipt of the money by the grantor had been indorsed on the title deeds which had been delivered to the grantee, the equitable mortgage was superior to the vendor's lien, as the two equities were not equal. In the course of the opinion, the Court said: "What is the rule of a court of equity for determining the preference as between persons having adverse equitable interests? The rule is sometimes expressed in this form: As between persons having only equitable interests qui prior est tempore potior est jure. This is an incorrect statement of the rule, for that proposition is far from being invariably true. * * * Another form of stating the rule is this:

As between persons having only equitable interests, if their equities are equal, qui prior est tempore potior est jure. 78

PRIORITIES 

79 This form of stating the rule is not so obviously incorrect as the former. And yet even this enunciation of the rule when accurately considered seems to me to involve a contradiction. For when we talk of two persons having equal or unequal equities in what sense do we use the word "equity"?

For example when we say that A has a better equity than B, what is meant by that? It means only that according to those principles of right and justice which a court of equity recognizes and acts upon, it will prefer A to B and will interfere to enforce the rights of A as against B; and therefore it is impossible (strictly speaking) that two persons should have equal equities except in a case in which a court of equity would altogether refuse to lend its assistance to either party as against the other. If the court will interfere to enforce the right of one against the other on any ground whatever, say on the ground of priority of time, how can it be said that the equities of the two are equal? i. e., in other words, how can it be said that one has no better right to call for the interference of a court of equity than the other? 

To lay down the rule, therefore, with perfect accuracy I think it should be stated in some such form as this: As between persons having only equitable interests, if their interests are in all other respects equal, priority of time gives the better equity; or qui prior est tempore, potior est jure and I think the meaning is this: that in a contest between persons having only equitable interests, priority of time is the ground of preference last resorted to; i. e., that a court of equity will not prefer the one to the other on the mere ground of priority of time, until it finds, upon an examination of their relative merits, that there is no other sufficient ground of preference between them or in other words, that their equities are in all respects equal; and that if the one has on other grounds a better equity than the other, priority of time is immaterial.” * * * The view thus expressed has been adopted by many authors of learned treatises. See Pomeroy's Equity Jurisprudence, 2d ed., sec. 414; 21 C. J., p. 209.

The law of this state is that in equity a subsequent assignee takes only what his assignor had, a right subject to the prior assignment, and notice to the debtor is not necessary to secure priority to the claim of the first assignee, although the debtor is, of course, protected in making payments until he receive such notice. This is illustrated by the cases of Muir v. Schenck & Robinson, 3 Hill 228, 38 Am. 

Dec. 633, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 484, and Central 

Trust Co. v. West India Imp. Co., 169 N. Y. 314, 62 N. E. 

387, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 490. In the former case a bond and mortgage were executed to A. A assigned the bond and mortgage to B as security for the payment of a note. B, at that time, gave no notice of this assignment to the mortgagor. A later assigned the bond and mortgage to 

C who immediately gave notice to the mortgagor. The mortgagor paid one installment to C. Before any more payments were made B gave notice to the mortgagor of his assignment and forbade any further payments to C. Nevertheless the mortgagor paid the balance to C. The court held that after notice of B's prior assignment, the mortgagor should not have made the final payment to C, although he was protected by lack of notice in his prior payments, on the ground that, both having equal equities, B's, being prior in time, was prior in right. In the latter case, the West 

India Improvement Co. transferred to the Central Trust 

Co., as security for a loan, certain property in Jamaica, West Indies, and also all other property which it should acquire by reason of a railroad it was constructing in Jamaica. It later executed another mortgage to the Manhattan Trust Co., covering certain property which had come into existence since the execution of the first mortgage but had been included in that mortgage. Holding that the first assignee had priority, the Court said, at p. 323: "It is further the settled law of this state, though a different rule prevails not only in England but in the Federal courts and in some of the states, that a bona fide purchaser for value of a chose in action takes it subject not only to

PRIORITIES 

81 equities between the parties, but also to latent equities in favor of third persons, and that to secure his superiority it is not necessary that the earlier assignee should give any notice of his assignment to the debtor or trustee." 

The doctrine would seem to be settled, therefore, that as between different assignees of a chose in action from the same person, the one whose assignment is prior in time will be protected although he has given no notice to either subsequent assignees or to the debtor. Williams v. Ingersoll, 89 

N. Y. 508; Fortunato v. Patten, 147 N. Y. 277, 41 N. E. 572.

Nor is a judgment creditor on a judgment obtained subsequent to an assignment in any better position than if he were a subsequent assignee. Niles v. Mathusa, 162 N. Y. 

546, 57 N. E. 184.

It has been held, also, that where payments have been made to a second assignee, the first assignee can recover them from the second in an action to impress a trust and for an accounting in equity. Superior Brassiere Co. v. 

Zimetbaum, 214 App. Div. 525, 212 N. Y. S. 473.

Under the familiar doctrine that equity looks at the substance and not the form, it has been held in York Mortgage Corp. v. Clotar Construction Corp., 254 N. Y. 128, 172

N. E. 265, that where a first mortgage contains a clause that it will be subordinate to a building loan mortgage thereafter to be obtained, the first mortgage will nevertheless be prior to the building loan mortgage where the latter is not in fact such, but was so denominated solely for the purpose of subordinating the prior mortgage. 

Certain preferences are given by statute to the one who first files or records his instrument. Lien Law, Arts. 2, 10; 

Recording Act, sec. 291. With these preferences equity does not interfere, since equity follows the law. 

§ 88. Effect of Subsequently Acquiring Legal Title. 

Between equal equities the law will prevail. This means that where two people have equal equities and one acquires the legal title, the one having both the equitable interest and

legal title will prevail over the one having the equitable interest only. Pomeroy, in his Equitable Jurisprudence, 2d ed., sec. 727, recognizes this rule but states that it is subject to certain exceptions, the principal one being that where there are successive equitable mortgages, the legal estate remaining in the mortgagor, the mortgagor cannot himself give priority to the holder of a subsequent encumbrance by conveying the legal estate to him. This was referred to in 

Central Trust Co. v. West India Improvement Co., 169 N. Y. 

314, 62 N. E. 387, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 490, where the court stated that there was some doubt whether the rule was the law of this State as applied to choses in action but held that the facts in that case came within the exception stated above. 

In Warren v. Wilder, 114 N. Y. 209, 21 N. E. 159, one of two creditors acquired the legal title to some of the debtor's property and it was held that as both were general creditors with equal equities the one with the legal title should prevail. The same situation is referred to in Seymour v. Wilson, 19 N. Y. 417, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 487.

There have been no other authoritative cases applying the maxim except cases in which the rights of a bona fide purchaser for value are also considered. 

The rule does not apply, as stated above, where preferences are given by the Recording Act or other statute. 

§ 89. Rights of Bona Fide Purchasers for Value. 

Where an owner of an equitable interest attempts to enforce it against one who has acquired the legal title to the property for a valuable consideration and without notice, equity will not aid. For example, a vendor's lien cannot be enforced against property which has come into the ownership of a bona fide purchaser for value. Maroney v. Boyle, 141 N. Y. 462, 36 N. E. 511, 36 Am. St. Rep. 821. On the same principle, if property transferred in fraud of creditors comes into the hands of a bona fide purchaser for value, it cannot be reached. Zoeller v, Riley, 100 N. Y. 102, 2 N.

PRIORITIES 

83 

E. 388, 53 Am. Rep. 157. Where a sale of property is induced by fraud and title has passed to the vendee, it may not be reclaimed from a bona fide purchaser for value. 

Stevens v. Brennan, 79 N. Y. 254; Barnard v. Campbell, 58 

N. Y. 73, 17 Am. Rep. 208, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 497. In such cases, however, equity, being unable to reach the property itself, will impress a lien on the proceeds. Am. Sugar Refining Co. v. Fancher, 145 N. Y. 552, 40 N. E. 206, 27 L. R. A. 757, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 160; Hammond v. Pennock, 61 N. Y. 145, Bachrach's 

Cases in Equity, p. 381.

CHAPTER X § 90. Notice Defined. 

NOTICE

Bispham, in his Principles of Equity, 9th ed., sec. 263, defines notice as "the legal cognizance of a fact," and states further: "Notice therefore in the sense here used may be said to be the definite legal cognizance, either actual or presumptive of a right or title; and the doctrine of Notice in 

Equity may be described to be this; viz., that where such cognizance is shown to exist either by proof as a fact or by presumption of law, the right or title thus proven to be known will be capable of assertion in Equity as against a party who might otherwise and at law have disregarded it.” 

Pomeroy, in his Equity Jurisprudence, 2d ed., sec. 594, criticizes this definition as giving the effect of notice rather than describing it and himself defines notice as "the information concerning a fact actually communicated to a party by an authorized person or actually derived by him from a proper source or else presumed by law to have been acquired by him which information is regarded as equivalent in its legal effects to full knowledge of the fact and to which the law attributes the same consequences as would be imputed to knowledge."

Both of these eminent authors agree that there may be notice without knowledge (under the Recording Act, for instance) and knowledge without notice (such as the examination of a defective deed on the record).

Notice may be either actual or constructive. 

§ 91. 

Actual Notice Defined. 

Notice is actual when the purchaser is aware of the adverse claim or title or has such information as would lead to knowledge. See White & Tudor's Leading Cases in 

Equity, vol. 2, p. 144. 84

NOTICE 

§ 92. Constructive Notice Defined. 

Constructive notice is notice which is presumed by law, as, for example, under the Recording Act. 

§ 93. Facts Constituting Notice. 

It is unnecessary to treat here of cases of actual knowledge or of the effect of the Recording Act or other similar statutes, which are properly treated in works on Real Property. We need only to point out in what other ways equity construes notice to be given. 

In the leading case of Williamson v. Brown, 15 N. Y. 354,

Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 501, it appeared that Brown conveyed land to one Earl taking back a purchase money mortgage which was not recorded. Later Earl conveyed to the plaintiff by a deed which was recorded. After that the mortgage was recorded. The defendant commenced proceedings to foreclose the mortgage and the plaintiff in this action sued to restrain the foreclosure. The referee found that the plaintiff did not, at the time he purchased the property, have actual notice of the existence of the mortgage but that he did have sufficient information to put him upon inquiry, that he pursued such inquiry to the extent of his information and did not find that the mortgage existed. On the one side it was contended that knowledge sufficient to put the purchaser upon inquiry is only presumptive evidence of actual notice and may be repelled by showing that the party did inquire with reasonable diligence but failed to discover the unrecorded conveyance; on the other, it was insisted that notice, which makes it the duty to inquire, amounts to constructive notice of the prior conveyance, the law presuming that inquiry will necessarily lead to discovery. The Court, at p. 362, stated the law to be as follows: 

"Where a purchaser has knowledge of any fact, sufficient to put him on inquiry as to the existence of some right or title in conflict with that he is about to purchase, he is presumed either to have made the inquiry and ascertained

the extent of such prior right, or to have been guilty of a degree of negligence equally fatal to his claim, to be considered as a bona fide purchaser. This presumption however, is a mere inference of fact and may be repelled by proof that the purchaser failed to discover the prior right, notwithstanding the exercise of proper diligence on his part."

The rule thus laid down has been followed in many cases dealing with real and personal property, one of the latest of which is Fidelity & Deposit Co. v. Queens Co. Trust Co., 226 

N. Y. 225, 123 N. E. 370.

Knowledge acquired by an agent engaged in his principal's business, which should have put him on inquiry, is notice to the principal of facts which the agent would have discovered had he inquired. Bennett v. Buchan, 76 N. Y. 386. 

The courts have held that actual, visible occupation of premises is sufficient notice of the existence of title in the person in possession, as against one who proposes to acquire an interest in the land, whether by conveyance or mortgage. 

Phelan v. Brady, 119 N. Y. 587, 23 N. E. 1109, Bachrach's

Cases in Equity p. 508. Constructive possession is not such notice. Brown v. Volkening, 64 N. Y. 76, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 511. But unless possession be so inconsistent with the existence of a mortgage lien as to suggest a prudent person a claim of title adverse to the mortgagee, it would not constitute notice. Gibson v. Thomas, 180 N. Y. 483, 73 

N. E. 484.

The rule as to the effect of recitals in recorded instruments as notice is stated in Acker v. Westcott, 46 N. Y. 

384, 7 Am. Rep. 355, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 515, to be that a purchaser is bound by recitals of defects which appear in his chain of title but not otherwise. The rule was applied in Western Union Tel. Co. v. Shepard, 169 

N. Y. 170, 62 N. E. 154, 58 L. R. A. 115, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 202, and has been followed in many cases in-

NOTICE volving restrictive covenants. Hodge v. Sloan, 107 N. Y. 

244, 17 N. E. 335, 1 Am. St. Rep. 816; Goldstein v. Rosenberg, 191 App. Div. 492, 181 N. Y. S. 559, affd. 232 N. Y. 

535, 134 N. E. 560, and authorities therein cited. See also, as to the effect of recording, Greene v. Warnich, 64 N. Y. 

220, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 520; Decker v. Boice, 83 N. Y. 215.

CHAPTER XI EQUITABLE ESTOPPEL § 94. Equitable Estoppel Defined. 

Equitable estoppel has been defined as follows: "When a party, either by his declarations or conduct, has induced a third person to act in a particular manner, he will not afterwards be permitted to deny the truth of the admission if the consequence would be to work an injury to such third person or to some one claiming under him." Trustees v. Smith, 118 N. Y. 634, 641, 23 N. E. 

1002, 7 L. R. A. 755.

And again, in Continental Nat. Bank v. Nat. Bank of the Commonwealth, 50 N. Y. 575, at p. 581, Bachrach's
