Cases in Equity, p. 525, the Court of Appeals said: "It is agreed that there must have been some act or declaration of the plaintiff or of its agent to the defendant's assignors, which so affected the conduct of the latter to their injury, as that it would be unjust now to permit the plaintiff to set up the truth of the case to the contrary of its mistaken act or declaration."

Equitable estoppel need not rest upon consideration or agreement or legal obligation. As the Supreme Court of the United States said, in Dickerson v. Colgrove, 100 U. S. 

578, 580, 25 L. ed. 618: "The vital principle is that he who by his language or conduct leads another to do what he would not otherwise have done, shall not subject such person to loss or injury by disappointing the expectations upon which he acted." 

And in Kirk v. Hamilton, 102 U. S. 68, 76, 26 L. ed. 79, the same Court said: "What I induce my neighbor to regard as true is the truth as between us, if he has been misled by my asseveration." 88

EQUITABLE ESTOPPEL 

89 

§ 95. 

Classification of Estoppel. 

Estoppel may arise either by matter of record, of deed or in pais. It is only with the latter class, sometimes called estoppel by misrepresentation, that we have to deal. 

Bispham's Principles of Equity, 9th ed., sec. 281. The doctrine of estoppel in pais is, however, applicable in law as well as in equity. Williamsburg Savings Bank v. Town of 

Solon, 136 N. Y. 465, 32 N. E. 1058.

Under estoppel in pais we have (a) Estoppel by statement. (b) Estoppel by act. (c) Estoppel by silence where there is a duty to speak. 

§ 96. Estoppel by Statement. 

An illustration of this class of estoppel is found in Miller v. Zeimer, 111 N. Y. 441, 18 N. E. 716, where, upon the assignment of an obligation by the holder, the debtor represented that the obligation was free from usury and the assignee purchased the obligation in reliance upon this statement. The debtor was held to be estopped thereafter to set up the usury. In Weyh v. Boylan, 85 N. Y. 394, 39 

Am. Rep. 669, the same rule was applied even though the debtor did not know to whom the obligation was to be assigned.

On the same principle, if one is induced to purchase land by the acts or representations of another, designed to influence his conduct and creating a reasonable belief on his 'part, under which he acts, that he is thereby acquiring a valid title to the same, the party who has thus influenced him is estopped from setting up his own title, existing at the time of the purchase, against that of the purchaser. 

Trustees v. Smith, 118 N. Y. 634, 23 N. E. 1002, 7 L. R. A. 

755. Where remaindermen represented to a mortgagee that their mother owned land, they were held to be estopped to deny the validity of a mortgage she executed on the land

པངས་་ even though she owned only a life estate. Dickinson v. 

Blake, 116 App. Div. 545, 101 N. Y. S. 709.

While a party bound to perform a contract has still time and opportunity for so doing, if something be said or done by the other party by which the former is induced to believe that a condition is waived or that strict compliance will not be insisted upon, the latter is estopped from claiming non-performance of the condition; but an estoppel cannot be founded on facts occurring after forfeiture of the contract because of non-performance. Underwood v. Farmers' Joint Stock Ins. Co., 57 N. Y. 500; Thomson v. Poor, 147 N. Y. 402, 42 N. E. 13, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 536; Imperator Realty Co. v. Tull, 228 N. Y. 447, 127

N. E. 263. For example, where a mortgage provided for payment of interest and installment “quarter-annually” and the mortgagee represented to the mortgagor before the date of one of these payments that it would be extended for a period of approximately two months, he was estopped to assert thereafter that the whole mortgage became due because of the failure of the mortgagor to make the payments on the original date. Firestone v. Miroth Cons. Co., 215 App. Div. 564, 214 N. Y. S. 239.

It should be noted that no question of consideration for the promise is involved. Some cases treat the doctrine as estoppel based on waiver, but the better doctrine would seem to be that it is another illustration of the tendency of courts of equity to enforce a promise where something resembling a fraud would otherwise be perpetrated. 

While a contract under seal cannot be modified by an executory parol agreement (Cammack v. Slattery, 241 N. Y. 

39, 148 N. E. 781), where the parol agreement has been acted upon, the parties are, to that extent, excused from the performance of the original contract, because it would be inequitable to hold them to its terms. Harris v. Shorall, 230 N. Y. 343, 136 N. E. 572; Nat. Cash Register Co. v. 

Remington Arms Co., 242 N. Y. 99, 151 N. E. 144.

EQUITABLE ESTOPPEL 

91 

Where plaintiff sold improved property to defendant orally representing that there was a right of way over other property of plaintiff's, he was estopped to deny the existence of the right of way, even though it was not mentioned in the deed. Mattes v. Frankel, 157 N. Y. 603, 52 N. E. 585.

A bank is estopped to deny the genuineness of a certification of a check which its cashier has declared to be good even though the certification prove to be a forgery, where the party to whom the statement was made relied on it to his injury. Continental Nat. Bank v. Nat. Bank of the Commonwealth, 50 N. Y. 575, Bachrach's Cases in

Equity, p. 525. This case is also authority for the proposition that to constitute equitable estoppel, intention to mislead need not be shown.

The statement relied on to constitute estoppel must be a statement of fact, except where it relates to the abandonment of an existing right. Akin v. Kellogg, 119 N. Y. 

441, 23 N. E. 1046, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 40; 

Witherell v. Kelly, 195 App. Div. 227, 187 N. Y. S. 43. In the latter case, Kelly was induced to appear personally in a foreclosure proceeding in Connecticut on the statement that no deficiency judgment would be taken against him. 

A deficiency judgment was nevertheless taken against him but the court held that the plaintiff was estopped to enforce it. Matter of opinion cannot be the basis of estoppel. 

McAleenan v. Mass. Bonding Co., 232 N. Y. 199, 133 

N. E. 444.

The contract of a person under disability cannot be made good by estoppel. For example, a representation by an infant, at the time of making a contract, that he is of age does not estop him from pleading his infancy. Int. Text 

Book Co. v. Connelly, 206 N. Y. 188, 99 N. E. 722, 42 

L. R. A. (N. S.) 1115. An infant may be estopped from affirmatively asserting a right of property, the same as an adult. Pomeroy's Equity Jurisprudence, 2d ed., sec. 815. 

And where intentional fraud is shown, an infant may be estopped. Spencer v. Carr, 45 N. Y. 406, 6 Am. Rep. 112.

www The representation must be external to the contract. In other words, an estoppel cannot be based upon anything contained in an instrument under attack as to its validity. In 

Bridger v. Goldsmith, 143 N. Y. 424, 38 N. E. 458, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 547, the contract contained a clause stating, in effect, that there had been no false representations inducing the contract. The court held that the party signing it was not estopped to attack the validity of the agreement on the ground that such false representations had been made. The same doctrine was laid down in 

Brick v. Campbell, 122 N. Y. 337, 25 N. E. 493. It has been held, however, that estoppel may be based upon representations contained in a separate instrument even though the latter be executed and delivered at the same time as the principal obligation. Weyh v. Boylan, 85 N. Y. 394, 39 

Am. Rep. 669. 

§ 97. Estoppel by Act. 

An illustration of estoppel by act is found in Rothschild v. Title Guar. & Trust Co., 204 N. Y. 458, 97 N. E. 879,

Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 533, in which it appeared that one Baldwin Strauss forged his mother's name to a mortgage and the mother, with knowledge of this, made payments of interest on the mortgage. The court held that, while the mother would not have been estopped by remaining silent, the payment of interest prevented the defendant from availing itself of its right to recover against Baldwin (who disappeared) as well as to prefer criminal charges against him, and hence she was estopped, as were her executors after her death, from denying the validity of the mortgage. To the same effect, see Elmhorst v. Maziroff, 176 App. Div. 145, 161 N. Y. S. 1029, affd. 223 N. Y. 649, 119 N. E. 1041.

Where the true owner of property holds out another as the owner, he is estopped to deny the ownership in the other as to third persons who act upon such apparent ownership to their damage. 21 C. J., p. 1172; Moore v. Metropolitan

EQUITABLE ESTOPPEL 

93 

Bank, 55 N. Y. 41, 14 Am. Rep. 173; Barnard v. Campbell, 58 N. Y. 73, 17 Am. Rep. 208, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 497; Feinberg v. Allen, 118 App. Div. 497, 103 N. Y. S. 

339; id., 143 App. Div. 866, 128 N. Y. S. 906, affd. 208 

N. Y. 215, 101 N. E. 893; McNeill v. Tenth Nat. Bank, 46 

N. Y. 325, 7 Am. Rep. 341. 

Where a married woman indorsed a note in New Jersey, the note being dated, made payable and discounted in New 

York, she was held estopped from claiming the advantage of exemption from liability under New Jersey laws, since by her act she induced the bank at which the note was discounted to believe that the indorsement was made in New 

York. Chem. Nat. Bank v. Kellogg, 183 N. Y. 92, 75 N. E. 

1103, 2 L. R. A. (N. S.) 299, 111 Am. St. Rep. 717. 

Where a Gas Company obtained the consent of a Town

Board to lay its pipes in the street, it was estopped, while retaining possession of the highway, from insisting that the

Town Board had no authority to give the consent, and hence that it was not bound by its terms. Farnsworth v. 

Boro Oil & Gas Co., 216 N. Y. 40, 109 N. E. 860. 

§ 98. Estoppel by Silence. 

A party may be estopped by silence only where there is a duty to speak. N. Y. Rubber Co. v. Rothery, 107 N. Y. 

310, 14 N. E. 269, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 541; 

Trenton Banking Co. v. Duncan, 86 N. Y. 221, Bachrach's

Cases in Equity, p. 550. In the language of the Court of 

Appeals: 

"A fraudulent purpose or a fraudulent result lies at the basis of the doctrine of equitable estoppel through silence or inaction. Actual or intended fraud is not an element essential to it. Neither affirmative acts or words nor silence maintained with the fraudulent intention of deceiving are indispensable elements of it. But it arises only when, relatively to the party invoking it or his privies, the omission to speak is an actual or constructive fraud. Its existence

requires that the party against whom it acts remained silent when he had the opportunity of speaking and when he knew or ought to have known that his silence would be relied upon, and that action would be taken or omitted which his statement of the truth would prevent, and that injury of some nature or in some degree would result." 

Rothschild v. Title Guar. & Trust Co., 204 N. Y. 458, 97 

N. E. 879, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 533.

The United States Supreme Court said, in Wiser v. Law ler, 189 U. S. 260, 271, 47 L. ed. 802, 23 Sup. Ct. Rep. 624: "The authorities also recognize a distinction between mere silence and a deceptive silence accompanied by an intention to defraud which amounts to a positive beguilement. . . (p. 272). It was said by the Court of Appeals of New York, in Viele v. Judson, 82 N. Y. 32, 40, of the cases holding a party to be estopped by his silence: 'In all of them the silence operated as a fraud and actually itself misled. In all of them there was both the specific opportunity and apparent duty to speak. And in all the party maintaining silence knew that some one else was relying upon that silence and either acting or about to act as he would not have done had the truth been told. These elements are essential to create a duty to speak.'' 

Where one remains silent under a mistaken impression of his own rights he will not be estopped. Bispham's Principles of Equity, 9th ed., sec. 288; Pomeroy's Equity Jurisprudence, 2d ed., sec. 809; Laventy v. Moore, 33 N. Y. 658.

Where an attorney consents to a stipulation that a certain judgment shall be a lien upon a judgment against "A" without informing the court or parties that he claims a prior lien on the judgment for his services as attorney, he is estopped from asserting his lien. McClare v. Lockard, 121 N. Y. 308, 24 N. E. 453. Where a man made a subscription towards the erection of a factory, which was not completed at the time specified, and stood by and saw it erected at a later period without attempting to cancel his

EQUITABLE ESTOPPEL 

95 subscription, he was held estopped from claiming that his subscription was invalid on that ground. Horton v. Erie 

Preserving Co., 90 App. Div. 255, 85 N. Y. S. 503, affd. 

181 N. Y. 535, 73 N. E. 1125.

On the other hand, a holder of a mortgage has been held not to be estopped from enforcing it by keeping silent after knowledge of a fraudulent discharge of his mortgage. 

Viele v. Judson, 82 N. Y. 32. This leading case has been followed by many others; e. g., N. Y. Rubber Co. v. Rothery, 107 N. Y. 310, 14 N. E. 269, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 541, holding that the failure by a riparian owner to object to a proposed diversion of water by the owner upon the opposite side did not bar an action to recover for such diversion; Thompson v. Simpson, 128 N. Y. 270, 289, 28 N. E. 627, holding that the owner of property which another had attempted to convey without authority was not precluded by mere silence from subsequently claiming it; 

Rothschild v. Title Guar. & Trust Co., 204 N. Y. 458, 97 

N. E. 879, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 533, referred to above, holding that mere silence after knowledge of a forged mortgage would not estop the person whose name was forged from setting up the forgery.

These cases are illustrative of the rule that no duty to speak arises from the mere fact that a man is aware that another may take action prejudicial to himself if the real facts are not disclosed. It must be a deceptive silence, accompanied by an intention to defraud. Lord Const. Co. v. 

Edison Portland Cement Co., 234 N. Y. 411, 138 N. E. 39.

A declaration which might not amount to an estoppel at the time it was made may become such by ratification or acquiescence. Trustees v. Smith, 118 N. Y. 634, 23 N. E. 

1002, 7 L. R. A. 755. Waiver by silence may also be made the basis of estoppel. Lord Const. Co. v. Edison Portland 

Cement Co., 234 N. Y. 411, 138 N. E. 39.

§ 99. Essentials of Estoppel-In General. 

It may be stated, therefore, that in order to create an equitable estoppel, the misrepresentation, whether arising from statements, acts or silence where there is a duty to speak, must be of a material fact, except where it relates to the abandonment of an existing right, or acquiescence in a state of facts relied upon by the other party to his injury. 

Vohmann v. Michel, 185 N. Y. 420, 78 N. E. 156, 113 Am. 

St. Rep. 921.

It is not essential that the party sought to be estopped should design to mislead except in cases of estoppel by silence. If his act was voluntary and calculated to mislead and actually has misled another acting in good faith, that is enough. Trustees v. Smith, 118 N. Y. 634, 23 N. E. 

1002, 7 L. R. A. 755; Manufacturers, etc., Bank v. Hazard, 30 N. Y. 226. The misrepresentation should be of such a character, however, that a sensible person would believe it was meant to be acted upon. Continental Nat. Bank v. Nat. 

Bank of the Commonwealth, 50 N. Y. 575, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 525; 21 C. J., p. 1121; Witherell v. Kelly, 195 

App. Div. 227, 187 N. Y. S. 43. The party alleging the estoppel must have been misled. Trustees v. Smith, supra; 

Merwin v. Romanelli, 141 App. Div. 711, 126 N. Y. S. 549; 

Wilcox v. Howell, 44 N. Y. 398, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 543; Card v. Moore, 68 App. Div. 327, 336, 74 N. Y. S. 

18, affd. 173 N. Y. 598, 66 N. E. 1105, and authorities therein cited; Catskill Nat. Bank v. Lasher, 165 App. 

Div. 548, 151 N. Y. S. 191, affd. 221 N. Y. 551, 116 N. E. 

1039, to his injury. Continental Nat. Bank v. Nat. Bank of the Commonwealth, supra; Rothschild v. Title Guar. & 

Trust Co., 204 N. Y. 458, 97 N. E. 879, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 533. It is not necessary to show actual damage other than the loss of valuable rights. Weil v. City of New 

York, 179 App. Div. 80, 166 N. Y. S. 221, affd. 223 N. Y. 

599, 119 N. E. 1085.

It will be seen, therefore, that the essentials of estoppel

EQUITABLE ESTOPPEL 

97 closely approximate some of the essentials of fraud. Indeed, it has been suggested that estoppel would be treated under fraud were it not for the importance of the subject. See 

Bispham's Principles of Equity, 9th ed., sec. 282. 

§ 100. 

Election.

The doctrine of election is explained in Havens v. Sackett, 15 N. Y. 365, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 560, as follows: "One who accepts a benefit under a deed or will must adopt the whole contents of the instrument, conforming to all its provisions and renouncing every right inconsistent with it. For example, if a testator has affected to dispose of property not his own and has given a benefit to the person to whom that property belongs, the legatee or devisee accepting the benefit so given to him must make good the testator's attempted disposition. If he insist upon retaining his own property which the testator has given to another person, equity will appropriate the gift made to him for the purpose of making satisfaction out of it to the person whom he has disappointed by the assertion of his rights. * The party whose property has been given to another will be put to his election, either to take what is offered to him in the instrument, yielding up to the party who would otherwise be disappointed, his own property, or to keep what was his own, abandoning the provision made for him in the instrument." * * As Pomeroy aptly states, in his Equity Jurisprudence, 2d ed., sec. 463, this is a practical application of the maxim "He who seeks equity must do equity." 

The doctrine has been applied in Beetson v. Stoops, 186 

N. Y. 456, 79 N. E. 731, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 556, holding that one who accepts a devise under a will cannot maintain an action for partition against another devisee to whom the testator undertook to devise real property in which he had only a life interest but which was owned by the two devisees as tenants in common; Matter of Peaslee,

73 Hun 113, 25 N. Y. S. 940, holding that a legatee who has accepted a benefit under a will cannot apply for its revocation until he puts other parties in interest in a position where they would lose nothing by the acceptance; 

Shanley v. Shanley, 22 App. Div. 375, 48 N. Y. S. 32, holding that a widow could not retain benefits under her husband's will and refuse to make a conveyance under an agreement between her and her husband; and other cases.

Where a provision is made by will in lieu of dower a widow is put to an election whether she will accept the provision or retain her dower and this election must be made within six months or she will be deemed to have accepted the provision in lieu of dower. R. P. L., secs. 200, 201.

Dower is now abolished as to real property acquired since 

August 31, 1930. A right of election under wills made since that time is now given to the surviving spouse, provided the amount left by the will is less than the intestate share of the survivor. Such election must also be made within six months. Dec. Est. Law, sec. 18.

§ 101. 

CHAPTER XII EQUITABLE CONVERSION 

Equitable Conversion Defined.

By the operation of the doctrine of equitable conversion real property is considered in equity as personalty or personalty as realty. It is based on the application of the maxim: "Equity looks on that as done which ought to be done."

Conversion may arise under a contract or under a will. 

Where a contract for the sale of land is made, equity, from the time a binding contract is entered into, regards the purchaser as the owner of the land and the seller as the owner of the money. On the same principle, where land is directed by will to be sold and converted into money, or money is directed to be invested in land, equity treats the property, from the time the direction becomes effective, as the kind of property into which it is directed to be converted. Williams v. Haddock, 145 N. Y. 144, 39 N. E. 825,

Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 585; Moncrief v. Ross, 50 

N. Y. 431, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 566. At law, under the same circumstances, the purchaser would not own the land until a deed was actually delivered or the money until it was actually paid. 

The doctrine is purely equitable and is applied for equitable purposes only. 13 C. J., p. 854; Wilder v. Ranney, 97 

N. Y. 7, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 578; Chamberlain v. Taylor, 105 N. Y. 185, 11 N. E. 625; Matter of Tienken, 131 N. Y. 391, 30 N. E. 109; Ray v. Fowler, 200 App. Div. 

155, 192 N. Y. S. 859. 

§ 102. 

Equitable Conversion Under Contracts. 

In the language of the Court of Appeals: "The general rule in regard to contracts for the sale of land is that the owner of real estate from the time of the 99

execution of a valid contract for such sale is to be treated as the equitable owner of the purchase money, and the purchaser of the land is to be treated as the equitable owner thereof. The vendor is deemed in equity to stand seized of the land for the benefit of the purchaser, and the latter, even before the conveyance to him, can devise the same and it descends to his heir; and the land which was agreed to be sold has been turned into money belonging to the vendor. Courts of equity regard that as done which ought to be done; they look at the substance of things and not at the mere form of agreements, to which they give the precise effect which the parties intended. 

If the vendor die prior to the completion of the bargain, provided there have been no default, the heir of the vendor may be compelled to convey and the proceeds of the land will go to the executors as personal property." Williams v. 

Haddock, 145 N. Y. 144, 150, 39 N. E. 825, Bachrach's 

Cases in Equity, p. 585. * * * The same case is authority for the proposition that the conversion takes effect from the date of a valid binding contract. Where the vendee has an option to purchase, the conversion takes effect as of the date of the exercise of the option. Rockland-Rockport Lime Co. v. Leary, 203 

N. Y. 469, 97 N. E. 43. The rule that on the exercise of the option the conversion relates back to the date of the contract (see Bispham's Principles of Equity, 9th ed., sec. 

321, and cases cited) is not followed in New York.

By statute, an agreement for the sale of property devised or bequeathed in a will already made is not deemed a revocation of such previous devise or bequest. Dec. Est. Law, sec. 37. The devisee, however, takes the property in its converted form. Van Tassel v. Burger, 119 App. Div. 509, 104 N. Y. S. 273.

Upon the theory that the vendee under a contract of sale is, in equity, the owner of the property, the courts have held that where an unsubstantial part of the land is taken by the exercise of the right of eminent domain, pending the

EQUITABLE CONVERSION 

101 transfer of title, the vendee is entitled to the award. Reife v. Osmers, 252 N. Y. 320, 189 N. E. 299; Clarke v. Long 

Island Realty Co., 126 App. Div. 282, 110 N. Y. S. 697.

On the same theory, the courts have held that, in the absence of any provision in the contract, if buildings on the property are destroyed by fire without fault of the vendor, the loss falls on the vendee. Sewell v. Underhill, 197 N. Y. 168, 90 N. E. 430, 27 L. R. A. (N.S.) 233, 134

Am. St. Rep. 863, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 592. The application of these principles may be varied by express contract between the parties. Brownell v. Board of Education, 239 N. Y. 369, 146 N. E. 630, 37 A. L. R. 1319.

Under the doctrine of equitable conversion, a contract vendee is entitled to any benefit or improvements to the land after the date of the contract. Clinton v. Hope Ins. 

Co., 45 N. Y. 454, at p. 465; Goldman v. Rosenberg, 116 

N. Y. 78, at p. 85, 22 N. E. 259. § 103. 

Equitable Conversion by Will.

The importance of this subject has been diminished by the recent abolition of the difference between heirs at law and next of kin. It is of some importance in its relation to property acquired or wills made before September 1, 1930.

In determining whether there has been equitable conversion of property under a will, the court attempts to find the intention of the testator. This intention must be clearly expressed and where there is doubt there is no conversion. 

Scholle v. Scholle, 113 N. Y. 261, 21 N. E. 84. The intention may be found in an imperative direction to sell, as in 

Moncrief v. Ross, 50 N. Y. 431, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 566, and Matter of Caldwell, 188 N. Y. 115, 80 N. E. 663, or in such a disposition of the property that a sale is necessary to carry out the intention of the testator, as in 

Salisbury v. Slade, 160 N. Y. 278, 54 N. E. 741, Bachrach's 

Cases in Equity, p. 569. See, also, Lent v. Howard, 89

N. Y. 169. The test has been stated to be whether it is

the duty of, and obligatory upon, the trustee to sell. White v. Howard, 46 N. Y. 144, at p. 162.

By statute, every trust power, unless its execution or non-execution is made expressly to depend on the will of the grantee, is imperative. R. P. L., sec. 57.

A discretionary power in the trustee to sell is not sufficient to constitute conversion. White v. Howard, 46 N. Y. 

144; Chamberlain v. Taylor, 105 N. Y. 185, 11 N. E. 625; 

Clift v. Moses, 116 N. Y. 144, 22 N. E. 303; Coann v. Culver, 188 N. Y. 9, 80 N. E. 362; Newell v. Nichols, 75 N. Y. 78, 31 Am. Rep. 424.

A discretion as to the time or manner of sale, however, does not make the direction any the less imperative. Mott v. Ackerman, 92 N. Y. 539; Union Trust Co. v. Cole, 198 

App. Div. 534, 190 N. Y. S. 855; Stagg v. Jackson, 1 N. Y. 

206; Doane v. Mercantile Trust Co., 160 N. Y. 494, 55 

N. E. 296. 

In Matter of Earnshaw, 196 N. Y. 330, 89 N. E. 825, a direction to the trustees to convert two-fifths of the residuary estate into cash "as soon as they can conveniently do so without loss" was held to result in conversion. In 

Phoenix v. Trustees of Columbia College, 87 App. Div. 438, 84 N. Y. S. 897, affd. 179 N. Y. 592, 72 N. E. 1149, the words, "I authorize and empower my executors and trustees at any time to sell, mortgage, lease or convey any portion of my real estate" were held to confer only a discretionary power from which no conversion resulted. 

§ 104. 

Time of Taking Effect of Conversion under Will. 

As stated by the Court of Appeals: "Whether the conversion shall be deemed to take effect at the death of the testator or at some later period depends upon his intention as manifested by the provisions of his will. If it provides in terms that a sale shall be made at some specified future time or creates a trust with direction to sell only on the happening of a designated event which

EQUITABLE CONVERSION 

103 in might or might not happen, then the conversion would only take place on its occurrence. Otherwise, the general rule is that real estate will be deemed to be converted into personalty as of the date of the death of a testator." Underwood v. Curtis, 127 N. Y. 523, 28 N. E. 585. 

In Moncrief v. Ross, 50 N. Y. 431, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 566, and Savage v. Burnham, 17 N. Y. 561, the sale was directed to be made at the termination of a life estate and the court held that there was no conversion until that time. Where there is a discretion in the trustees as to the time of sale, but the direction is imperative, the conversion takes place as of the testator's death. Underwood v. Curtis, supra; Robert v. Corning, 89 N. Y. 225; 

Hope v. Brewer, 136 N. Y. 126, 32 N. E. 558, 18 L. R. A. 

458; Fisher v. Banta, 66 N. Y. 468; Matter of Tienken, 131 

N. Y. 391, 30 N. E. 109. 

§ 105. 

version. 

Miscellaneous Applications of Doctrine of Con- 

By statute, the proceeds of a sale of real property of an infant or incompetent are deemed property of the same nature as the property sold until the infant arrives of age or the incompetency is removed. C. P. A., sec. 1402; Forman v. Marsh, 11 N. Y. 544;; Horton v. McCoy, 47 N. Y. 21. 

The money descends as realty. Ford v. Livingston, 140 

N. Y. 162, 35 N. E. 437. Similarly, where there is an unauthorized investment in real estate of personal property of an infant by his guardian, the property is considered personalty. Matter of Bolton, 159 N. Y. 129, 53 N. E. 756.

Where executors, not being authorized by the will to acquire realty, bought land in order to protect the interests of the estate, it was held that the land should be deemed personalty. Archer v. Archer, 147 App. Div. 44. 131 

N. Y. S. 66.

§ 106. Failure of Purpose of Conversion. 

Since equity attempts to carry out the intention of the testator, where there is a total failure of the purpose of the testator in directing conversion, there is no conversion. 

Read v. Williams, 125 N. Y. 560, 26 N. E. 730, 21 Am. St. 

Rep. 748. In Parker v. Linden, 113 N. Y. 28, 20 N. E. 858, a conversion was directed in order that an alien brother and sister of the testator might take under the will, aliens being prohibited at that time from owning realty. The brother and sister died before the testator. The court held that there was no conversion.

Where there is a partial failure of the purpose of the testator, while the doctrine of conversion applies, equity will impress a resulting trust in favor of the heirs to the extent that the intention of the testator fails, so that, in effect, there is no conversion. Chamberlain v. Chamberlain, 43 N. Y. 424; Chamberlain v. Taylor, 105 N. Y. 185, 11 

N. E. 625; Matter of Seymour, 209 App. Div. 655, 205 

N. Y. S. 327, modified and affd. 239 N. Y. 259, 146 N. E. 372. 

In Jones v. Kelly, 170 N. Y. 401, 63 N. E. 443, Bachrach's 

Cases in Equity, p. 575, it appeared that the testator left more than one-half of his estate, consisting of real property, to his executors in trust to sell and give to charity, in violation of law (Dec. Est. Law, sec. 17), and the court held that the half so attempted to be disposed of illegally should be treated as land and go to the heirs at law rather than the next of kin. 

§ 107. 

Reconversion.

Where there has been a conversion directed for the purposes of distribution, the parties entitled to take may, if they so elect, receive the property in its original form. 

Prentice v. Janssen, 79 N.. Y. 478, Bachrach's Cases in

Equity, p. 580. There may also be reconversion by operation of law. For example, where, under a contract of sale, there has been an equitable conversion of personalty into

EQUITABLE CONVERSION 

105 real property and the property comes into the possession of a person who is entitled to it both as heir and executor, it becomes in equity what it is in fact; that is, personal property. It is discharged from the fictitious impression which had been given to it. Forman v. Marsh, 11 N. Y. 544; 

Matter of Rathjen, 115 App. Div. 644, 101 N. Y. S. 289.

CHAPTER XIII ADJUSTMENT INCLUDING SET-OFF, CONTRIBUTION, EXONER- ATION, SUBROGATION, MARSHALLING AND ACCOUNTING. 

§ 108. Set-off Defined. 

Set-off means the right of the defendant to offset against the claim of the plaintiff, a claim which he (the defendant) has against the plaintiff, in order to diminish or defeat the plaintiff's claim. As the courts have said, the underlying principle is that the balance, in case of mutual debts, is the real sum owing by or to the party. Hughitt v. Hayes, 136 

N. Y. 163, 32 N. E. 706, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 595. 

§ 109. 

Set-off at Law and in Equity.

Set-off did not exist at common law, according to which mutual debts were distinct, and inextinguishable except by actual payment or release, and a defendant who had a demand against the plaintiff was compelled to resort either to his cross action or to a bill in Chancery. 34 Cyc., p. 626.

The modern practice has removed this defect by allowing the defendant to set up such defenses or counterclaims as he has in the action at law. C. P. A., sec. 262. In order to justify a recourse to equity at the present time, therefore, there must be some special equity, such as insolvency of one of the parties. Spofford v. Rowan, 124 N. Y. 108, 26 N. E. 350; Richards v. La Tourette, 119 N. Y. 54, 23 

N. E. 531; Newton v. Lee, 139 N. Y. 332, 34 N. E. 905.

In a case falling within equitable jurisdiction, however, general equitable principles are applied. Thus, claims will be regarded as due, notwithstanding the absence of a technical demand, when equitable considerations demand that they be applied each to the other and the fact that the debt from the party asserting the set-off is not yet due is of no importance. Richards v. La Tourette, supra; Hughitt v. Hayes, 136 N. Y. 163, 32 N. E. 706, Bachrach's 106

ADJUSTMENT-INCLUDING SET-OFF, ETC. 

107 

Cases in Equity, p. 595; Matter of Hatch, 155 N. Y. 401, 50 N. E. 49, 40 L. R. A. 664; Gerseta Corp. v. Equitable 

Trust Co., 241 N. Y. 418, 150 N. E. 501. In Hughitt v.

Hayes, supra, it appeared that the plaintiff accumulated funds in a bank for the purpose of paying for some land which he had a contract with the bank to buy. The bank failed and the question arose, in an action for specific performance of the contract, whether the plaintiff should be treated as a general creditor of the bank, receiving only the dividends received by them, or should be allowed to offset his entire claim against the amount owing on the contract. The court held that he was entitled to offset his entire claim and that the fact that he had not made a technical demand for the money owing him made no difference in equity.

The rule that the debt due to the one asserting the set-off must be due but that the debt from him need not be, is stated in Gerseta Corp. v. Equitable Trust Co., supra, at p. 423, as follows: "The law of this State, when unmodified by statute, is that in insolvency, a distinction exists between a debt due to the one who claims the set-off and a debt due from him. If the debt is due to the one who claims the set-off and it has not matured, he is not at liberty to offset it against the debt which he owes. If, on the other hand, the debt due to him has matured and the question is whether he is to be allowed to claim a dividend, not only on the debt due to him, but to increase the dividend by offsetting the debt which is due from him, the fact that the debt due from him is not yet due will not defeat the set-off, but he may prove the credit. The principle upon which the rule rests is that in case of mutual debts it is only the balance which is the real or just sum owing by or to the insolvent. 

If the claim against the insolvent has matured at the time of an assignment for the benefit of creditors the assignee gets no greater right than the assignor had. The assignee takes the balance subsequently accruing after deducting

the matured claim. But if the debt due from the insolvent subsequently matures, the equities of other creditors will intervene to prevent the depletion of the assets in the hands of the trustee by extinguishing a good debt due to the estate by a bad one due to the creditor from the estate." 

A different rule prevails in the federal courts, where claims from the insolvent may be offset though not yet due. 

See Frank v. Mercantile Nat. Bank, 182 N. Y. 264, 74 N. E. 

841, 108 Am. St. Rep. 805. A similar rule exists in assignments for the benefit of creditors under the Debtor & Creditor Law, Sec. 151; Appleton v. Nat. Park Bank, 241 N. Y. 

561, 150 N. E. 555. At law, on the other hand, both claims must have matured in order to allow an offset. Jordan v. Nat. Shoe & Leather Bank, 74 N. Y. 467, 30 Am. 

Rep. 319; Appleton v. Nat. Park Bank, 122 Misc. 248, 202 

N. Y. S. 516, affd. 211 App. Div. 708, 208 N. Y. S. 228, affd. 

241 N. Y. 561, 150 N. E. 555. 

In Building & Engineering Co. v. Northern Bank, 206 

N. Y. 400, 99 N. E. 1044, an indorser of notes not yet due, made by accommodation makers, was allowed to offset his liability on the notes against his deposit in the bank and the court points out that while at law the makers of the notes must arbitrarily be treated as primarily, and the indorser as secondarily liable, the rights in equity are enforced as they exist as matter of fact, and as matter of fact the indorser was liable for the debt and hence entitled to the set-off. 

In Curtis v. Davidson, 215 N. Y. 395, 109 N. E. 481, an indorser was allowed to offset a deposit against his liability as indorser of notes of different makers on the ground that, when sued, an indorser is absolutely liable and hence may offset any claim he has against the holder. The Court suggests that where the indorser sues for a set-off it may be that a court of equity would require some satisfactory assurance that he will not be indemnified by the maker, but not where he is sued by the holder. 

In Burns v. Lopez, 256 N. Y. 123, 175 N. E. 537, the

ADJUSTMENT INCLUDING SET-OFF, ETC. 

109 court seems to have extended the rule to a partnership debt due from the insolvent, as against the individual debt of one of partners due to the insolvent, where the partners consent to its application. 

The date of adjudication of insolvency, not the date of insolvency in fact, is the one taken into consideration in determining whether there is necessity for recourse to equity. While the debtor is a going concern, even though insolvent in fact, the rule of legal set-off applies. Gerseta 

Corp. v. Equitable Trust Co., supra. 

§ 110. 

Contribution. 

The theory upon which the equitable right of contribution is based is explained, in Asylum of St. Vincent de Paul v. 

McGuire, 239 N. Y. 375, 382, 146 N. E. 632, 38 A. L. R. 

1214, as follows: "The right to contribution here invoked is not dependent on contract or joint action or original relationship between the parties. It is based on principles of fundamental justice and equity (citing cases). And of these principles no one is more explicit and outstanding than the one that where the situation of parties is equal and one has borne more than his just share of the common burden, he is entitled to contribution from others who have been dealt with more fortunately (citing cases). This is simply the statement in another form of the principle that equality amongst those similarly situated is equity."

While the right originated in equity, courts of law sometimes take jurisdiction on the ground of an implied contract arising from the equitable obligation. 13 C. J., p. 832; 

Rindge v. Baker, 57 N. Y. 209, 15 Am. Rep. 475. The remedy in equity has many advantages. See Bispham's 

Principles of Equity, 9th ed., sec. 329; 13 C. J., p. 833; 

Asylum of St. Vincent de Paul v. McGuire, supra; Armitage v. Pulver, 37 N. Y. 494, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 597.

The doctrine is most frequently applied in cases of co-

sureties or co-guarantors. Where one co-surety pays more than his moiety he may recover the excess from his cosureties. Morgan v. Smith, 70 N. Y. 537. The fact that the obligors are bound by separate instruments or for different amounts is immaterial so long as they are bound for a common debt. Armitage v. Pulver, supra; Yawger v. Am. 

Surety Co., 212 N. Y. 292, 106 N. E. 64. The right to compel contribution does not arise until payment. Hence, contribution may be enforced against a co-guarantor even though, by delay in prosecuting, the creditor has lost his right to enforce the debt against the co-guarantor. However, the creditor must not affirmatively do any act, such as releasing one co-guarantor, which would destroy or impair the rights of the sureties as between themselves. If he does, to that extent he diminishes his recovery against them. Hard v. Mingle, 206 N. Y. 179, 99 N. E. 542, 42 L. 

R. A. (N. S.) 1131. The payment must be compulsory. A mere volunteer acquires no right of contribution by payment. Aspinwall v. Sacchi, 57 N. Y. 31; Yawger v. American Surety Co., supra.

The application of the principle of contribution to cases other than those involving co-sureties is well illustrated by Asylum of St. Vincent de Paul v. McGuire, supra, in which the owner of securities, criminally but effectively pledged by a depository for his own debt, was held liable to contribute to another owner of securities similarly pledged but which had been sold to pay the common debt. In Aspinwall v. Sacchi, supra, a stockholder who had been compelled to pay a debt of the corporation because of the failure of its officers to comply with the law was held entitled to contribution from his co-stockholders. See, also, Cheesebrough v. Millard, 1 Johns Ch. 409, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 602.

By statute, there is now contribution between joint tort feasors where a judgment has been recovered against both. 

C. P. A., sec. 211-a. In the absence of statute, there is

no such contribution. Price v. Ryan, 255 N. Y. 16, 173 

N. E. 907. 

§ 111. Exoneration. 

Exoneration is the right of one secondarily liable to recover what he has paid from the primary debtor. While this doctrine was of equitable origin, a legal remedy now exists. 

See Bispham's Principles of Equity, 9th ed., sec. 331; Pomeroy's Equity Jurisprudence, 2nd ed., sec. 1417; Konitzky v. 

Meyer, 49 N. Y. 571. Before the surety has paid the debt he may call upon the creditor to proceed against the principal debtor and may also bring a suit in equity for that purpose upon showing a special equity. If the creditor does not do so the surety may be discharged. Pain v. Packard, 13 Johns. 174, 7 Am. Dec. 369; Remsen v. Beekman, 25 

N. Y. 552; Colgrove v. Tallman, 67 N. Y. 95, 23 Am. Rep. 

90; 32 Cyc., pp. 234, 248. For cases in which the equities were not sufficient, see Newcomb v. Hale, 90 N. Y. 326, 43 

Am. Rep. 173; Wells v. Mann, 45 N. Y. 327, 6 Am. Rep. 93.

Where a mortgage covers several parcels of land that are separately and at different dates sold by the mortgagor they are each liable to sale in satisfaction of the mortgage as against the purchasers from the mortgagor in the inverse order of alienation. Where the mortgagee with notice of a conveyance releases a part of the land subject to be sold first that fact generally constitutes a defense to the earlier purchaser to the extent of the value of the land released. 

Trustees v. Wheeler, 61 N. Y. 88; Kendall v. Woodruff, 87 

N. Y. 1; Libby v. Tufts, 121 N. Y. 172, 24 N. E. 12, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 606. Where the vendees assume payment, the reverse is true and the last purchaser is entitled to exoneration from the earlier purchasers. pham's Principles of Equity, 9th ed., sec. 333. 

§ 112. Subrogation Defined. 

Bis- The doctrine of subrogation has been defined as "the principle by which a person is placed in the position of a

creditor, so that the person in whose favor the doctrine is exercised succeeds to the rights of the creditor as they exist in relation to the debt." Editorial, Sept. 14, 1923, N. Y. 

Law J'l, vol. 69, p. 1806. To quote Sheldon on Subrogation, 2d ed., sec. 1: "It is treated as the creature of equity and is so administered as to secure real and substantial justice without regard to form, independent of any contractual relations between the parties."

In the language of the Court, in Pittsburgh-Westmoreland Coal Co. v. Kerr, 220 N. Y. 137, 144, 115 N. E. 465, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 609: "The remedy includes . . . so wide a range of subjects that it has been called the 'mode which equity adopts to compel the ultimate payment of a debt by one who in justice, equity and good conscience ought to pay it.' 999 

In Arnold v. Green, 116 N. Y. 566, 572, 23 N. E. 1, the 

Court said: "Under such circumstances the debt, although paid and satisfied in form, is regarded in equity as neither paid nor satisfied in fact, but by operation of law the former holder ceases to be the creditor, while the person paying takes his place as owner of the debt and security unimpaired." 

And in Pease v. Egan, 131 N. Y. 262, 272, 30 N. E. 102, the Court used the following language: "No contract is necessary upon which to base the right, for it is founded upon principles of equity and benevolence and may be decreed where no contract exists * * * * It was said by Chief Justice Marshall that equity would clothe the party thus paying with the legal garb with which the contract he has discharged was invested, and it would substitute the party paying to every equitable interest and purpose, in the place of the creditor whose debt he has discharged."

§ 113. 

Application of Doctrine of Subrogation. 

A judgment or mortgage though paid may be considered in equity to be in full force for the benefit of the party paying. Union Trust Co. v. Monticello & P. J. Ry. Co., 63 N. Y. 311, 314, 20 Am. Rep. 541. Where a party is entitled to be subrogated to a legal right of action, a court of law may treat the plaintiff, who is entitled in equity to subrogation, as an assignee and allow him to maintain an action of a legal nature. Dunlop v. James, 174 N. Y. 411, 415, 67 N. E. 60.

The right to subrogation arises only on payment of the whole debt. There is no subrogation on payment of a part of a debt. Thus it was held that a surety, who had paid a portion of a debt for which a mortgage was held as collateral, could not claim a portion thereof. Hanlon v. Union 

Bank, 247 N. Y. 389, 160 N. E. 650. 

Am. Surety Co. v. 

One who is only a

Nor is it created by liability to pay. 

Palmer, 240 N. Y. 63, 147 N. E. 359. volunteer cannot invoke the aid of subrogation, for such a person can establish no equity. Gans v. Thieme, 93 N. Y. 

225, 232; Koehler v. Hughes, 148 N. Y. 507, 42 N. E. 

1051. He must have paid upon request or as surety or under some compulsion made necessary by the adequate protection of his own rights. Acer v. Hotchkiss, 97 N. Y. 395.

An excellent illustration of the application of the doctrine is found in Pittsburgh-Westmoreland Coal Co. v. Kerr, 220 

N. Y. 137, 115 N. E. 465, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 

609, in which it appears that a bank was the holder of a note of one John K. Kerr, indorsed by his uncle. Kerr misappropriated money of the plaintiff and with it paid the note, which he destroyed. It was held that the plaintiff was entitled to be subrogated to the rights of the bank before the note was paid; in other words, that the note, with the liabilities of the maker and indorser unimpaired, should

be deemed in equity to be kept alive for the benefit of the plaintiff.

A surety who pays the debt is entitled to all the property and collateral securities of the debtor held by the creditor even though he had no knowledge of such securities when he made his contract or they came into possession of the creditor after the execution of the contract. Havens v. 

Willis, 100 N. Y. 482, 3 N. E. 313. If the creditor returns the securities to the principal debtor he will be liable to the surety. State Bank v. Smith, 155 N. Y. 185, 49 N. E. 680.

A surety who pays a judgment is entitled to an assignment of the judgment. Townsend v. Whitney, 75 N. Y. 425. A creditor is entitled to the benefit of any obligation or collateral securities which the principal debtor has given to the surety. Catskill Nat. Bank v. Dumary, 206 N. Y. 550, 100 N. E. 422.

The doctrine has been extended to one who paid off a mortgage in order to protect his rights in the property covered by the mortgage (Durante v. Eannaco, 65 App. 

Div. 435, 72 N. Y. S. 1048; Gans v. Thieme, 93 N. Y. 225; 

Arnold v. Green, 116 N. Y. 566, 23 N. E. 1); to a railroad company which settled an injury claim with the injured person with notice of an attorney's lien thereon (Morehouse v. Bklyn. Hts. R. R. Co., 185 N. Y. 520, 78 N. E. 178); to an heir who paid a judgment which was a lien on land which he inherited (Cole v. Malcolm, 66 N. Y. 363); to a bank which by mistake paid a forged check, given to pay off certain assessments against land, and which was thereafter subrogated to the lien of the assessments (Title Guar. & 

Trust Co. v. Haven, 196 N. Y. 487, 89 N. E. 1082, 25 L. R. A. (N. S.) 1308, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 615); to a partner who paid a firm debt after dissolution (Schuyler v. 

Booth, 76 App. Div. 619, 79 N. Y. S. 1146) or after retiring from the partnership with the agreement that the remaining partners would assume the debts (Merrill v. Green, 55

N. Y. 270); to executors who paid a mortgage in order to protect the estate (Lilianthal v. Lesser, 102 App. Div. 500,

92 N. Y. S. 619, affd. 185 N. Y. 557, 77 N. E. 1190); to a mortgagor who, after selling the land to one who assumed the mortgage, was compelled to pay the debt (Johnson v. 

Zink, 51 N. Y. 333); and many others.

Being an equitable doctrine, it can never be involved against one whose equities are equal or superior to those of the party seeking to be subrogated. Bell v. Greenwood, 229 App. Div. 550, 242 N. Y. S. 149. 

§ 114. Marshalling. 

Marshalling means that where a creditor has a lien on properties "A" & "B" and a second creditor has a lien only on property "B," equity requires the first creditor to satisfy as much of his demand as possible out of property "A." As stated by Pomeroy, in his Equity Jurisprudence, 2d ed., sec. 396: "The holder of the security on two funds is compelled to shape his own remedy so as to preserve, if possible, the equity of the one whose lien extends to but one fund." 

The principles governing the application of this doctrine are stated in Cheesebrough v. Millard, 1 Johns. Ch. 409, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 602, as follows: "I admit as a principle of equity that if a creditor has a lien on two different parcels of land and another creditor has a lien of a younger date on one of those parcels only and the prior creditor elects to take his whole demand out of the land on which the junior creditor has a lien, the latter will be entitled to have the prior creditor thrown upon the other land or to have the prior lien assigned to him and to receive all the aid it can afford him. This is a rule founded in natural justice and I believe it is recognized in every cultivated system of jurisprudence. In the English law it is an ordinary case that if a party has two funds he shall not by his election disappoint another who has one fund only, but the latter shall stand in the place of the former or compel the former to resort to that fund which can be affected by him only."

In Breed v. National Bank, 57 App. Div. 468, 68 N. Y. S. 

68, affd. 171 N. Y. 648, 63 N. E. 1115, it appeared that the defendant was secured by two funds, one of which was the plaintiff's sole security, and the rule was stated that the defendant must first resort to the fund on which the plaintiff did not have a lien. See also, Bowery Savings Bank v. 

Nosmo Realty Corp., 218 App. Div. 160, 218 N. Y. S. 48.

If a creditor who has a lien on two funds, with notice of the facts, release one of them to the prejudice of another creditor whose lien extends to the other, it will amount to a satisfaction pro tanto as to such other creditor. Ingalls v. 

Morgan, 10 N. Y. 178.

The rule in equity requiring parcels of land incumbered by a judgment or mortgage to be subjected to sale in the inverse order of their alienation by the debtor, referred to in section 111, supra, under the subject of Exoneration, is a branch of this rule. Ingalls v. Morgan, supra; Libby v. 

Tufts, 121 N. Y. 172, 24 N. E. 12. 

To make the rule applicable, the properties must belong to the same debtor. Continental Ins. Co. v. Reeve, 149 App. 

Div. 835, 134 N. Y. S. 78. 

§ 115. Accounting in Equity. 

Actions for accounting had their origin in the common law, but, the remedy being inadequate in many cases, equity frequently assumed jurisdiction. 1 C. J., p. 612; Pomeroy's 

Equity Jurisprudence, 2d ed., sec. 2353. Under modern practice in New York, practically the only ground for recourse to equity is the existence of a fiduciary or trust relation. In Marvin v. Brooks, 94 N. Y. 71, at p. 80, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 618, the Court said: "The best considered review of the authorities puts the equitable jurisdiction upon three grounds, viz: The complicated character of the accounts; the need of a discovery, and the existence of a fiduciary or trust relation. (1 Story's 

Eq. Jur., sec. 459, and note 5.) The necessity for a resort

to equity for the first two reasons is now very slight, if it can be said to exist at all, since a court of law can send to a referee a long account, too complicated for the handling of a jury, and furnishes by an examination of the adverse party before trial, and the production and deposit of books and papers, almost as complete a means of discovery as could be furnished by a court of equity. But the jurisdiction of the latter court over trusts and those fiduciary relations which partake of that character remains, and in such cases the right to an accounting seems well established."

Accountings by trustees and the supervision of trusts have always been under the peculiar jurisdiction of equity. 

Husted v. Thompson, 158 N. Y. 328, 53 N. E. 20. This applies not only to express trusts but to other cases in which equity recognizes the existence of a fiduciary or trust relationship. To illustrate: An accounting has been allowed in equity against the directors of a corporation (Bosworth v. 

Allen, 168 N. Y. 157, 61 N. E. 163, 55 L. R. A. 751, 85 Am. 

St. Rep. 667; Godley v. Crandall-Godley Co., 212 N. Y. 121, 105 N. E. 818, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 436); against a tenant in common who took possession of mineral lands which were the subject of the tenancy, extracted the ore therefrom and sold it for his exclusive benefit (Abbey v. 

Wheeler, 170 N. Y. 122, 62 N. E. 1074); in the case of a joint venture, a quasi partnership and a partnership (Forman v. Lumm, 214 App. Div. 579, 212 N. Y. S. 487, and authorities therein cited); and in the case of an agency involving trust and confidence, although a bare agency is not enough (Marvin v. Brooks, supra; China & Japan

Trading Co. v. Provand, 155 App. Div. 171, 140 N. Y. S. 

79, and authorities therein cited). In a proper case, equity will decree an accounting between partners even though dissolution is not asked. Sanger v. French, 157 N. Y. 213, 51 

N. E. 979. See, also, discussion in Lord v. Hull, 178 

N. Y. 9, 70 N. E. 69, 102 Am. St. Rep. 484. Equity will, also, compel an accounting where a party has collected a sum of money for the benefit of a number of others; e. g.,

where money has been paid to a common carrier under an insurance policy taken out by the carrier "for account of whom it may concern." Symmers v. Carroll, 207 N. Y. 632, 101 N. E. 698, 47 L. R. A. (N. S.) 196, Ann. Cas. 1914

C 685. An accounting has also been allowed where property has been purchased from a thief and a portion resold. Fur & Wool Trading Co. v. Fox, 245 N. Y. 215, 156 N. E. 670; and to a case where the relationship of broker and customer was concerned. Haight v. Haight & Freese Co., 112 

App. Div. 475, affd. 190 N. Y. 540, 83 N. E. 1126.

The application has been denied where it was sought to invoke the aid of equity for an accounting between partners as a mere incident in the settlement of one solitary matter (Lord v. Hull, supra); where it was sought to have an accounting of royalties (Ehrlich v. Mills, 215 App. Div. 116, 214 N. Y. S. 830, and authorities therein cited) and where it was sought to recover compensation measured by the profits of a business even though an accounting was necessary (Freeman v. Miller, 157 App. Div. 715, 142 N. Y. S. 797). § 116. Burden of Proof in Legal and Equitable Accountings.

In legal accountings, the burden is always on the plaintiff to prove how much is due. In equity, proof of the fiduciary relationship, followed by proof of money or property entrusted to the defendant, puts upon the defendant the burden of rendering an account. In Marvin v. Brooks, 94 N. Y. 71, 76, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 618, the 

Court said: "The equitable jurisdiction has always rested largely upon such relation of confidence, involving the need of discovery and the duty of explanation, and hence the burden of such explanation and the proof of its truth fell, in such cases, upon the defendant whose conduct was questioned, whenever an accounting was decreed, and required of him the extreme of good faith."

ADJUSTMENT INCLUDING SET-OFF, ETC. 

119 

§ 117. Accounting Incidental to Other Equitable Relief. 

In many cases an accounting is necessary as an aid to other equitable relief. In such cases the question of jurisdiction is based upon the demand for the other relief asked as well as on the necessity for the intervention of equity for the accounting. A leading case is Falk v. Hoffman, 233 

N. Y. 199, 135 N. E. 243, which was a suit for the rescission of a contract alleged to have been procured by fraud and an accounting for the proceeds and ensuing profits. The court, conceding that there was some remedy at law, held that it was not so complete or effective as the remedy in equity. 

The same rule applies to other actions, of which the following are examples: An action to recover stock and for an accounting McKenzie v. Wappler Electric Co., 215 App. 

Div. 336, 214 N. Y. S. 875; Treadwell v. Clark, 190 N. Y. 51, 82 N. E. 505; an action for dissolution of a partnership with an accounting Brady v. Powers, 112 App. Div. 845, 98 N. Y. S. 237, modified and affd. 188 N. Y. 626, 81 N. E. 

1160; an action to recover the proceeds of stolen bonds with an accounting Newton v. Porter, 69 N. Y. 133, 25 Am. 

Rep. 152; and stolen furs. Fur & Wool Trading Co. v. Fox, 245 N. Y. 215, 156 N. E. 670.

CHAPTER XIV RE-EXECUTION, REFORMATION, RESCISSION AND CANCELLATION. 

§ 118. Re-execution. 

As the name implies, this remedy is used to secure a new execution of deeds, contracts or other papers which have been lost or destroyed. The subject has been treated under 

Accident. See sec. 57, supra. As there stated, when a paper needed to prove title has been lost or accidentally destroyed, equity will decree the execution of a new paper. A court of equity is peculiarly fitted to give this relief since it can at the same time protect the original signers of the document from the possibility of the original appearing in the hands of a bona fide purchaser for value. For example, where an indemnity bond would not fully protect a company issuing a negotiable bond which has been lost, the court directed that the company issue a simple certificate of indebtedness. Switzerland Gen. Ins. Co. v. N. Y. C. & H. 

R. R. Co., 152 App. Div. 70, 136 N. Y. S. 726. See, also, Kent v. Church of St. Michael, 136 N. Y. 10, 32 N. E. 704, 18 L. R. A. 331, 32 Am. St. Rep. 693, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 319; Kinnan v. Forty-second St. Ry. Co., 140 

N. Y. 183, 35 N. E. 498, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 38. 

§ 119. Reformation Defined. 

Reformation is the mode adopted by equity to make a written instrument express the actual agreement of the parties. At law, a court can only enforce an agreement as written; in equity, the written instrument can be corrected to express the actual agreement of the parties and enforced as reformed. 

§ 120. Mistake as Ground for Reformation. 

Reformation is granted in cases of mutual mistake, except where the mistake is of such a nature as to prevent the mak- 120

RE-EXECUTION, ETC. 

121 ing of any agreement; that is, where there has been no actual meeting of the minds; e.g., where the parties were unaware of the non-existence of the subject matter of their agreement. See secs. 61-63, supra. 

A deed or other instrument may be reformed not only where there is a mistake in the omission or insertion of words contrary to the intention of the parties, but also where the parties, understanding the language used in the description, believe it to correspond with the actual boundaries but are mistaken. Bush v. Hicks, 60 N. Y. 298; Thomas v. Harmon, 122 N. Y. 84, 25 N. E. 257; Le Gendre v. Scottish Union & Nat. Ins. Co., 95 App. Div. 562, 88 N. Y. S. 1012.

A memorandum, relied upon to prove a contract within the Statute of Frauds, will not be reformed. Friedman & 

Co., Inc. v. Newman, 255 N. Y. 340, 174 N. E. 763.

Where a husband secures a gratuitous conveyance from his wife, the existence of the confidential relationship casts the burden upon him of showing that the conveyance was free from fraud or mistake. Boyd v. De La Montagnie, 73 

N. Y. 498, 29 Am. Rep. 197.

A deed or contract may be reformed by inserting a different name as grantee or vendee. Haack v. Weicken, 118 

N. Y. 67, 23 N. E. 133; MacDonald v. Crissey, 215 N. Y. 

609, 109 N. E. 609. For other illustrations, see sec. 61, supra. 

§ 121. Mistake Induced by Fraud. 

Where the mistake of one party was induced by fraud on the part of the other, equity will decree reformation or rescission. For example, in Welles v. Yates, 44 N. Y. 525, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 649, a reservation of timber was omitted from a deed by mistake of the plaintiff with fraudulent conduct on the part of the defendant. The court not only decreed reformation of the deed, but also directed the defendant to account for the timber removed. See, also,

Maher v. Hibernia Ins. Co., 67 N. Y. 283; Kilmer v. Smith, 77 N. Y. 226, 33 Am. Rep. 613;; Gotthelf v. Shapiro, 136 

App. Div. 1, 120 N. Y. S. 210; Albany City Sav. Inst. v. 

Burdick, 87 N. Y. 40, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 390. 

$122. Proof of Mistake in Expressing Agreement. 

Parol evidence is admitted in equity, not to contradict the agreement, but to show that the written instrument does not express the actual agreement of the parties. Gillespie v. Moon, 2 Johns. Ch. 585, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 

641. Many writers and courts have adopted the observation of Lord Hardwicke, in Baker v. Paine, 1 Vesey 456: "How can a mistake in an agreement be proved but by parol evidence? It is not read to contradict the face of the instrument, but to prove a mistake therein.” 

See, also, Gordon Malting Co. v. Bartels Brewing Co., 206 N. Y. 528, 100 N. E. 457; MacDonald v. Crissey, 215 

N. Y. 609, 109 N. E. 609; Ruppert v. Singhi, 243 N. Y. 156.

The courts require clear and convincing proof of the mistake. In the language of the Court, in Drachler v. Foote, 88 App. Div. 270, 273, 84 N. Y. S. 977, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 646: "A written instrument, carefully and deliberately prepared and executed, is evidence of the highest character and will be presumed to express the intention of the parties to it until the contrary appears by clear, positive and unequivocal evidence."

The courts do not, however, require proof beyond a reasonable doubt. Southard v. Curley, 134 N. Y. 148, 31 

N. E. 330, 16 L. R. A. 561, 30 Am. St. Rep. 642. The authorities as to the degree of proof required are collated in the case last cited. See, also, Jamaica Sav. Bank v. Taylor, 72 App. Div. 567, 76 N. Y. S. 790.

RE-EXECUTION, ETC. 

123 

§ 123. Negligence of Party Seeking Reformation. 

The fact that the party seeking reformation omitted to read the paper and understand its terms is no defense in equity. Albany City Sav. Inst. v. Burdick, 87 N. Y. 40, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 390; Jamaica Sav. Bank v. 

Taylor, 72 App. Div. 567, 76 N. Y. S. 790. 

§ 124. Jurisdiction of Court in Action for Reformation. 

Where reformation of a contract is sought, the jurisdiction of the court must appear from well recognized equitable principles and cannot be conferred by agreement. 

Stoddard v. Stoddard, 227 N. Y. 13, 124 N. E. 91, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 662. 

§ 125. 

Rescission.

As previously explained, a defrauded party, if he has not waived the right by electing an inconsistent remedy, has the right to bring a suit in equity for rescission or at law to recover the down payment even though he is unable to prove knowledge of falsity, or scienter. See sec. 68, supra,

In equity the same remedy may be had where there is a unilateral mistake or mistake on one side and fraud on the other. See secs. 62, 121, supra. The remedy is also available for failure of consideration, inability to perform a contract, repudiation of the contract, or an essential part thereof, and for such a breach as substantially defeats its purpose. As stated by the court, in Callanan v. Keeseville, etc., R. R. Co., 199 N. Y. 268, 284, 92 N. E. 747, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 625: "It is not permitted for a slight, casual or technical breach, but, as a general rule, only for such as are material and willful or, if not willful, so substantial and fundamental as to strongly tend to defeat the object of the parties in making the contract. Failure to perform in every respect is not essential, but a failure which leaves the subject of the contract substantially different from what was contracted for is sufficient.

"If the party who seeks rescission has an adequate remedy at law, ordinarily he is not entitled to rescind, but in case of repudiation or of a breach going to the root of the contract, unless the damages can be ascertained with reasonable certainty, rescission is a matter of right, with restitution instead of compensation."

The case cited is an excellent example of the way in which equity does complete justice in one action. While it was held that the defendants had no right to proceed with work under the contract after notice of rescission, still under the maxim "He who seeks equity must do equity" the plaintiff was compelled to allow for permanent benefits to the property made by the defendant. 

In order to rescind a contract, the party seeking the remedy must restore or offer to restore what he has received. If, however, the acts of the defendant have made it impossible to restore him to his original position, he has but himself to blame. Hammond v. Pennock, 61 N. Y. 

145, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 381; Baker v. Lever, 67 N. Y. 304, 23 Am. Rep. 117; Moore v. Mutual Reserve 

Assn., 121 App. Div. 335, 106 N. Y. S. 255. “In exacting the return of benefits as a condition of rescission it (equity) proportions the exaction to the justice of the case before it." Marr v. Tumulty, 256 N. Y. 15, 175 N. E. 356. 

Where the contract has been executed, equity will not entertain an action for rescission unless the decree is essential to the plaintiff's protection. Schanck v. Schuchman, 212 N. Y. 352, 106 N. E. 127.

Where the property has come into the hands of a bona fide purchaser for value under such circumstances that he acquires a good title, equity does complete justice by enforcing a trust and rendering a money judgment for the value of the property against the guilty party. Valentine v. Richardt, 126 N. Y. 272, 27 N. E. 255, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 634; Slayback v. Raymond, 93 App. Div. 326, 87 N. Y. S. 931.

RE-EXECUTION, ETC. 

125 In order to be entitled to sue for rescission, plaintiff must be a party or a privy to the contract. A mere volunteer has no right to bring the action. Insurance Co. of Penn. v. Park & Pollard Co., 190 App. Div. 388, 393, 180 N. Y. S. 

143, affd. 229 N. Y. 631, 129 N. E. 936. Rescission must be in toto or it will not be allowed at all. There cannot be a part affirmance and part disaffirmance. Yeomans v. Bell, 151 N. Y. 230, 234, 45 N. E. 552; Merry Realty Co. v. 

Shamokin & Hollis R. E. Co., 230 N. Y. 316, 130 N. E. 306, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 636; Hammond v. Pennock, supra; Goins v. Atwood, 204 App. Div. 439, 197 N. Y. S. 781. 

Subsequent acceptance of benefits of the contract nullifies rescission. Brennan v. National Equitable Investment Co., 247 N. Y. 486, 160 N. E. 924, rearg. den. 162 N. E. 524, 248 N. Y. 560. 

§ 126. Cancellation. 

Cancellation of an instrument is usually decreed in connection with a decree rescinding a contract. Where a contract is rescinded and there is outstanding a note or mortgage given in pursuance of the contract, for example, equity will direct that the instrument be delivered up and cancelled, especially in a case in which there is danger that the instrument may get into the hands of a bona fide purchaser for value. To illustrate: In Wickwire v. Warner, 191 App. Div. 835, 182 N. Y. S. 165, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 32, affd. 233 N. Y. 572, 135 N. E. 923, the court cancelled a note given in pursuance of a subscription to stock which was rescinded.

Cancellation may be decreed independently of rescission, however. For example, where one Rogers orally promised a charitable institution that he would give them certain land free and clear, and the institution entered upon the land and made large improvements, the court decreed a cancellation of a mortgage which he had given to his son, without consideration, after the improvements were made

and just before he conveyed the property to the institution. 

Messiah Home for Children v. Rogers, 212 N. Y. 315, 106 

N. E. 59. Similarly, bonds of a municipality which are illegally issued may be directed to be delivered up and cancelled. Calhoun v. Millard, 121 N. Y. 69, 121 N. E. 69, 8 L. R. A. 248, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 62. A court of equity will refuse to cancel an obligation tainted with usury when the rights of the maker can be adequately protected at law. Reiner v. Galinger, 151 App. Div. 711, 136 

N. Y. S. 205. Cancellation will be decreed, however, where the lender has taken possession of the property pledged as security, thus depriving the borrower of his legal remedy. 

Berry v. Arland, 153 App. Div. 940, 138 N. Y. S. 1107, rev. on other grounds, 217 N. Y. 709, 112 N. E. 439. A usurious mortgage on real property will be cancelled. 

Myers v. Wheeler, 24 App. Div. 327, 48 N. Y. S. 611, affd. 

161 N. Y. 637, 57 N. E. 1118. See, also, Ryon v. Wanamaker, 116 Misc. 91, 190 N. Y. S. 250, affd. 202 App. Div. 

848, 194 N. Y. S. 977, affd. 235 N. Y. 545, 139 N. E. 728.

CHAPTER XV SPECIFIC PERFORMANCE § 127. Specific Performance Defined. 

Specific performance has been defined as "an equitable remedy by which a party to a contract is compelled to do or omit the very acts which he has undertaken to do or omit." 

36 Cyc., p. 543; Pomeroy's Specific Performance of Contracts, 3d ed., sec. 3. It is a remedy enforceable in equity only. At law, in an action on a contract, the plaintiff can recover a sum of money, either the debt itself or damages for the breach of the contract, and nothing more. In equity he recovers the specific thing agreed to be delivered. As stated by the Court in Rindge v. Baker, 57 N. Y. 209, 214, 15 Am. Rep. 475: "The doctrine of specific performance is nothing more nor less than a means of compelling a party to do what he ought to have done without being coerced by a court." 

§ 128. Classes of Contracts to Which Remedy Is Ap plicable.

As has been heretofore many times stated, equity will not interfere where there is a complete and adequate remedy at law. Where a contract provides for the payment of a sum of money only or where money damages for its breach will be adequate, there is no necessity for recourse to equity, since the law furnishes a complete remedy. It should be noted, however, that where the vendor is obligated to do some act in return for the payment of money equity modifies this principle somewhat by holding that, since the vendee has the right to compel specific performance by the vendor, the remedy should be mutual. For instance, in a contract for the sale of land the vendee ordinarily agrees to pay a sum of money on receipt of a deed conveying the title to the land, but since he can compel the vendor to deliver the deed, 127

equity gives the right to the vendor to compel the acceptance of the deed and the payment of the money. Woodruff v. Germansky, 233 N. Y. 365, 135 N. E. 601; Baumann v. Pinckney, 118 N. Y. 604, 23 N. E. 916. The rules governing the extent to which the remedy of specific performance is applicable to specific classes of contracts will be discussed in the following sections. 

§ 129. Contracts Concerning Land. 

The right of the vendor or vendee to a decree for the specific performance of a contract concerning land is firmly established. As stated by the Court in Losee v. Morey, 57 

Barb. 561, at p. 564, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 668: "When all is fair, and the parties deal on equal terms, it is a universal rule, in equity, to enforce contracts for the sale of lands specifically, at the demand of either the vendor or vendee; and in such case it is as much the duty of the court to decree specific performance of the contract as it is to give damages for its breach.

The basis of this rule has been the subject of much discussion. The weight of opinion supports the theory that, as no two parcels of land are alike, money damages would be inadequate, since the vendee, for instance, could not with the money purchase a like parcel. As stated by Vice Chancellor Sandford, in Best v. Stow, 2 Sandf. Ch. 298, at p. 301, "the peculiar locality, soil, vicinage, advantage of markets and the like conveniences of the estate contracted for cannot be replaced by other land of equal value." See Losee v. 

Morey, supra; Baumann v. Pinckney, 118 N. Y. 604, 23 

N. E. 916; Jones v. Barnes, 105 App. Div. 287, 94 N. Y. S. 

695; Hamilton College v. Roberts, 223 N. Y. 56, 119 N. E. 

97. Bispham advances the theory that, since, under a contract for the sale of land, equity regards the vendee as the owner of the land and the vendor as the owner of the money, the parties should have the aid of the Chancellor in securing the legal title to property of which they are the equitable owners. Bispham's Principles of Equity, 9th ed., sec. 364.

SPECIFIC PERFORMANCE 

129 Pomeroy states that while the explanation first given is almost universally adopted, its historical accuracy may be doubted and suggests that the reason for the rule may lie in the higher importance accorded to land in the early 

English jurisprudence. Pomeroy's Specific Performance of Contracts, 3d ed., sec. 9; 36 Cyc., p. 552. However, as stated by the same author, "the rule having once been established is now universal. The actual motives and design of the purchaser are never inquired into, for it is assumed in every instance that damages are an inadequate relief for the breach of a land contract." Pomeroy's 

Specific Performance of Contracts, 3d ed., sec. 10.

Since equity acts in personam, specific performance may be decreed even though the real property lie without the jurisdiction. The leading case in this country is 

Massie v. Watts, 6 Cranch 148, 3 L. ed. 181, in which the 

Supreme Court of the United States ruled that the Circuit Court of Kentucky had jurisdiction to compel a conveyance of land situated in Ohio. An equally famous case is Penn v. Lord Baltimore, 1 Ves. Sen. 441, 27 Eng.

Rep. (Reprint) 1132, in which Lord Chancellor Hardwicke decreed specific performance of an agreement with respect to the boundaries between the colonies of Pennsylvania and Maryland. The rules thus laid down in these cases have been followed in this state. Gardner v. 

Ogden, 22 N. Y. 327, 78 Am. Dec. 192, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 90; Bailey v. Ryder, 10 N. Y. 363; Newton v. Bronson, 13 N. Y. 587, 67 Am. Dec. 89.

Where the defendant is outside the State and the property within, the court may nevertheless acquire jurisdiction of the defendant through constructive service and compel specific performance of a contract, the sheriff conveying in case the defendant is beyond the jurisdiction of the court. 

Garfein v. McInnis, 248 N. Y. 261, 162 N. E. 73.

Many contracts concerning land other than those for the sale or exchange of lands have been held capable of specific enforcement; e.g., a sale of pulp wood to be taken

from the land (St. Regis Paper Co. v. Santa Clara Lumber Co., 173 N. Y. 149, 65 N. E. 967); an agreement by a vendee to secure the discharge of a mortgage (Woodruff v. Germansky, 233 N. Y. 365, 135 N. E. 601); an agreement to build a party wall (Rindge v. Baker, 57 

N. Y. 209, 15 Am. Rep. 475); an agreement to lease premises (Kolasky v. Michels, 120 N. Y. 635, 24 N. E. 

278); an agreement for further assurance of title (Werner v. Wheeler, 142 App. Div. 358, 368, 127 N. Y. S. 158, and authorities therein cited). 

§ 130. Contracts Concerning Personal Property. 

The general rule is that equity will not decree specific performance of a contract for the sale of personal property, for the reason that in most cases money damages afford a complete and adequate remedy for the breach of such a contract. For example, one who has a contract for the purchase of sugar, which the vendor refuses to deliver, can buy other sugar at the market price and recover the difference between what he is obliged to pay and the contract price. Thus he has what he contracted for and is indemnified for any pecuniary loss. Fox v. 

Fitzpatrick, 190 N. Y. 259, 269, 82 N. E. 1103, citing with approval Pomeroy's Equity Jurisprudence, 3d ed., sec. 1402; 

Clements v. Sherwood-Dunn, 108 App. Div. 327, 95 N. Y. S. 

766, affd. 187 N. Y. 521, 79 N. E. 1102; Harle v. Brennig, 131 App. Div. 742, 746, 116 N. Y. S. 51. Where for any reason, however, money damages are not adequate, as where property similar to that contracted for cannot be purchased, equity will enforce specifically a contract concerning personal property as readily as it will one concerning real property. For example, in Waddle v. Cabana, 220 

N. Y. 18, 114 N. E. 1054, a contract for the purchase of one hundred shares of stock was specifically enforced when it appeared that all of the stock except eleven shares was in the hands of the defendant, the Court saying, at p. 26: "The value of the stock in suit can be ascertained only

SPECIFIC PERFORMANCE 

131 in the most speculative way. A contract for the purchase of stock freely sold in the market would not be thus enforced for an adequate remedy at law exists in such cases, but to deny this remedy when the stock has no ascertainable value, is nearly all owned by one man and can be obtained only from him, and only as a favor and for special reasons, would be to deny to appellant the substantial benefit of the contract (citing cases) and defeat the relief which should in conscience be given." 

A like case is Butler v. Wright, 186 N. Y. 259, 78 N. E. 

1002, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 671. Since a corporation can only purchase its own stock out of surplus, a contract by which the corporation binds itself to purchase its own stock at a future time is unenforceable. Topken, Loring & Schwartz v. Schwartz, 249 N. Y. 206, 163 N. E. 735.

A contract for the delivery of a chattel which has a peculiar value, such as a rare painting or a family heirloom, will be specifically enforced. Indeed this remedy has been enforced where the chattels were tortiously withheld. A leading and famous case is Pusey v. Pusey, 1 Vernon's Ch. 

273, 23 Eng. Rep. (Reprint) 465, in which the complainant asked that a horn which for centuries had been the emblem of ownership of his estate be delivered to him. In New 

York the doctrine has been applied to Indian wampum belts. 

Onondaga Nation v. Thacher, 29 Misc. 428, 61 N. Y. S. 1027, affd. 53 App. Div. 561, 65 N. Y. S. 1014, affd. 169 N. Y. 

584, 62 N. E. 1098.
