Under the principles stated above the courts have decreed specific performance of a contract for the sale of a steamship (Menier v. Donald, 98 Misc. 684, 165 N. Y. S. 

50, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 670); for the delivery of rare drawings and sketches (Lang v. Thacher, 48 App. 

Div. 313, 62 N. Y. S. 956); to assign a patent (Spears v. 

Willis, 151 N. Y. 443, 45 N. E. 849); to assign future in ventions (Klauder-Weldon Dyeing Machine Co. v. Wel don, 166 App. Div. 415, 151 N. Y. S. 1068. See, also, Nat.

Cash Register Co. v. Remington Arms Co., 242 N. Y. 99, 151 N. E. 144).

The case is much stronger if a trust or confidential relation exists between the parties. Johnson v. Brooks, 93 

N. Y. 337; Spears v. Willis, supra.

In 1911 the New York State legislature enacted section 

149 of the Personal Property Law, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 672, giving a court of equity the power, "if it thinks fit," to decree specific performance of a contract for the sale of specific or ascertained goods. The general view is that this was merely declaratory of the rules that already existed (See McKinney's Consolidated Laws, p. 246, note) and the courts, in their decisions since the act was passed, have reiterated the principles already expressed. 

Waddle v. Cabana (1917), supra; Rawll v. Baker-Vawter 

Co. (1919), 187 App. Div. 330, 335, 176 N. Y. S. 189; 

Schwartz v. Marjolet, Inc., 214 App. Div. 530, 212 N. Y. S. 

464. It would seem, therefore, that this enactment has made no change in the existing law. 

§ 131. Agreements to Leave Property by Will. 

Agreements to leave property by will will be specifically enforced provided the agreement is clearly proved. For example, in Winne v. Winne, 166 N. Y. 263, 59 N. E. 832, 82 Am. St. Rep. 647, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 673, it appeared that the mother of the plaintiff made an agreement with one Mrs. Winne whereby the plaintiff was to live with Mrs. Winne as her own child, in return for which 

Mrs. Winne was to leave him all of her property on her death. The plaintiff performed the contract on his part but Mrs. Winne died intestate. The Court held, in an interesting and instructive opinion, that the agreement could be enforced specifically. Such contracts must, however, be established by clear and convincing proof. As stated by the Court in Hamlin v. Stevens, 177 N. Y. 39, 47, 69 

N. E. 118:

SPECIFIC PERFORMANCE 

133 "Contracts of the character in question have become so frequent in recent years as to cause alarm, and the courts have grown conservative as to the nature of the evidence required to establish them, and in enforcing them, when established, by specific performance. Such contracts are easily fabricated and hard to disprove, because the sole contracting party on one side is always dead when the question arises."

In the same opinion, the Court said further, at p. 50: "Such contracts should be in writing, and the writing should be produced, or, if ever based upon parol evidence, it should be given or corroborated in all substantial particulars by disinterested witnesses.' 99 While the Court of Appeals has been careful to point out that this is not intended to supersede the rule that a plaintiff in a civil action is never required to prove his case by more than a fair preponderance of evidence (See McKeon v. Van Slyck, 223 N. Y. 392, at p. 397, 119 N. E. 851) the principle that in order to make out a preponderance the evidence must be clear and convincing has been adhered to consistently. McKeon v. Van Slyck, supra; Taylor v. Higgs, 202 N. Y. 65, 95 N. E. 30; Wallace v. Wallace, 216 N. Y. 

28, 109 N. E. 872; Ward v. N. Y. Life Ins. Co., 225 N. Y. 

314, 120 N. E. 207; Matter of Sherman, 227 N. Y. 350, 125 

N. E. 546.

Proof of such a contract may be found in a mutual or joint will. In Rastetter v. Hoenninger, 314 N. Y. 66, 108 

N. E. 210, a case involving a joint will by husband and wife, it was held that while as a will an instrument could be revoked at pleasure, as a contract, if supported by an adequate consideration, it could be enforced in equity, and that upon the death of the wife and acceptance of her property by the husband the agreement became irrevocable on his part. In 

Morgan v. Sanborn, 225 N. Y. 454, 112 N. E. 696, it appeared that a childless husband and wife executed wills giving all of their property to each other under an agreement by which the survivor was to distribute the property by will

among the next of kin of the husband and wife, the distribution to be according to the judgment of the survivor. The husband died first and the wife left all of her property by I will to her own next of kin. It was held that the next of kin of the husband, while they could not object to a probate of the will, could maintain an action to compel specific performance of the agreement. This case also furnishes an excellent illustration of the application of the maxim equality is equity, for it was held that since the court could not exercise the discretion reposed in the survivor it would divide the property equally between the two sets of next of kin. See, also, Hermann v. Ludwig, 186 App. Div. 287, 174 

N. Y. S. 469, affd. 229 N. Y. 544, 129 N. E. 908. Lally v. 

Cronen, 247 N. Y. 58, 159 N. E. 723.

It should be noted in passing that contracts to leave real property by will come within the statute of frauds. Burns v. McCormick, 223 N. Y. 230, 135 N. E. 273. See discussion in section 141, infra.

An agreement not to contest a will may be enforced in equity. In Re Cooks Will, 244 N. Y. 63, 154 N. E. 823.

Courts of equity have been very liberal in enforcing antenuptial contracts to leave property by will. Phalen v. U. 

S. Trust Co., 186 N. Y. 178, 78 N. E. 943, 7 L. R. A. (N. S.) 

734, and authorities therein cited. It was held in Adams v. 

Swift, 169 App. Div. 802, 155 N. Y. S. 873, that a will executed in pursuance of such an agreement, where there had been performance by the other party, became irrevocable. 

§ 131-a. Specific Performance of Gifts of Land. 

An agreement, even resting in parol, to give lands to another will be specifically enforced where the promisee enters upon the lands and makes improvements thereon &c. 

Freeman v. Freeman, 43 N. Y. 34, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 678; Messiah Home v. Rogers, 212 N. Y. 315, 106

N. E. 59. Such an agreement is personal to the promisee. 

Pershall v. Elliot, 249 N. Y. 183, 163 N. E. 554.

SPECIFIC PERFORMANCE 

135 

§ 132. 

Miscellaneous Contracts. 

Other obligations, which have been held capable of being enforced specifically, include the following: an agreement to issue an insurance policy (Ellis v. Albany City Fire Ins. Co., 

50 N. Y. 402, 10 Am. Rep. 495; Christy v. Homeopathic Mut.

Life Ins. Co., 93 N. Y. 345; Van Loan v. Farmers' Mut. Fire 

Ins. Assn., 90 N. Y. 280; International Ferry Co. v. Am. 

Fidelity Co., 207 N. Y. 350, 101 N. E. 160); the duty of a railroad to provide farm crossings (Jones v. Seligman, 81 

N. Y. 190); the obligation of a corporation to pay dividends on its preferred stock before paying dividends on its common stock (Boardman v. Lake Shore & Mich. So. Ry. Co., 84 N. Y. 157); a husband's promise to provide a specified house for his wife as part of a separation agreement (Doty v. Rensselaer County Mut. Fire Ins. Co., 194 App. Div. 841, 185 N. Y. S. 466); an agreement made by the guardian of the infant owners of certain real property to pay an attorney one third of the value of said property as compensation for his services in recovering the same in an ejectment suit, which agreement was specifically enforced in a proceeding to sell the property (Matter of Hynes, 105 N. Y. 560, 12 

N. E. 60); a stipulation to vacate a judgment and discontinue an action (Deen v. Milne, 113 N. Y. 303, 20 N. E. 

861); an agreement to publish plaintiff's advertisement and no other advertisement of a similar article during a stated period (Goddard v. American Queen, 44 App. Div. 454, 61 

N. Y. S. 133). 

On the other hand, the following obligations have been held incapable of specific enforcement: an agreement on the part of a lessor to repair damages caused by a fire (Beck v. Allison, 56 N. Y. 366, 15 Am. Rep. 430); an agreement to arbitrate in a foreign state (Matter of Inter-Ocean Food 

Products, Inc., 206 App. Div. 426, 201 N. Y. S. 536); a promise to make monthly payments under a separation agreement (Thompson v. Thompson, 178 App. Div. 610, 165 

N. Y. S. 892); an agreement to make advances to meet the obligations of a third party (Bradford, E. & C. R.R. Co. v.

N. Y., L. E. & W. R.R. Co., 123 N. Y. 316, 25 N. E. 499, 11

L. R. A. 116); an agreement to make a lease which would be illegal (Palombi v. Volpe, 249 N. Y. 194, 163 N. E. 607). 

§ 133. Remedy Discretionary. 

The rule has been stated repeatedly that specific performance cannot be demanded as matter of right but rests in the sound discretion of the court. A few of the many cases in which this principle has been laid down are Waddle v. 

Cabana, 220 N. Y. 18, 114 N. E. 1054; Conger v. N. Y., W. 

S. & B. R.R. Co., 120 N. Y. 29, 23 N. E. 983, Bachrach's 

Cases in Equity, p. 681; Winne v. Winne, 166 N. Y. 263, 59 

N. E. 832, 82 Am. St. Rep. 647, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 673; Losee v. Morey, 57 Barb. 561, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 668; Bomeisler v. Forster, 154 N. Y. 229, 48 N. 

E. 534, 39 L. R. A. 240, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 34.

This is but an application of the doctrine that the court will not make an inequitable decree. Where, therefore, there is misrepresentation, fraud, undue influence or mistake, or where the contract is unconscionable, equity will not enforce it. "Courts of equity refuse to enforce harsh and unfair bargains. If moral coercion produces a contract; if it is executed under practicable compulsion; if one party acts unfairly and the other yields to the pressure of circumstances, equity will refuse specific performance even though in law the contract would be enforceable." Scheinberg v. 

Scheinberg, 249 N. Y. 277, 164 N. E. 98.

In Conger v. N. Y. W. S. & B. R.R. Co., supra, the relative benefit to the plaintiff, injury to the defendant and rights of the public were taken into consideration. In 

Margraf v. Muir, 57 N. Y. 155, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 683, the fact that the contract was unconscionable was decisive. In Hammer v. Michael, 243 N. Y. 445, 154 N. E. 

305, the court refused to enforce a contract which was oppressive and inequitable, though entered into by defendant's excusable mistake. In Groesbeck v. Morgan, 206 N. Y.

SPECIFIC PERFORMANCE 

137 

385, 99 N. E. 1046, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 68, delay plus a change in circumstances made a decree inequitable. 

In Buckmaster v. Thompson, 36 N. Y. 558, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 686, the contract was too ambiguous to be enforced. In Palmer v. Gould, 144 N. Y. 671, 39 N. E. 

378, specific performance was refused on the ground that the attorney and agent for the vendor concealed the fact that he was also agent for the vendee. The rule may, therefore, be stated, in the language of the Court in Sherman v. Wright, 49 N. Y. 227, 230, as follows: "Courts of equity will not in any case interfere to decree a specific performance, except in cases where it would be strictly equitable to make such a decree, and in allowing or denying relief of that character will look not only to the nature of the transaction, but also to the character of the parties, and if one of the parties is guardian or trustee, the interests of the ward or cestui que trust will be considered."

In Winne v. Winne, supra, the character of the plaintiff was taken into consideration, in accordance with the principles laid down in the foregoing quotation. 

§ 133-a. Certainty. 

Contracts which are ambiguous will not be enforced. 

Buckmaster v. Thompson, 36 N. Y. 558, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 686. Nor will a contract be enforced where a material element is left open for future consideration. 

Thus in Ansorge v. Kane, 244 N. Y. 395, 155 N. E. 683, the amount to be paid on the signing of the contract was to be later agreed upon and the court refused enforcement. 

A like ruling was made in Keystone Hardware Co. v. Tague, 246 N. Y. 79, 158 N. E. 27, where the contract provided that the due date of a mortgage to be given was "subsequently to be agreed upon.' 99 The rule does not apply to a case where the term of the mortgage, rate of interest or date of delivery of the deed

are left blank since these will be implied by law. N. E. D. 

Holding Co. v. McKinley, 246 N. Y. 40, 157 N. E. 923; 

Wertheimer v. Boehm 241 N. Y. 575, 150 N. E. 561; Keystone Hardware Co. v. Tague, supra. 

§ 134. Practicability. 

"Contracts which require the performance of varied and continuous acts, or the exercise of special skill, taste and judgment, will not, as a general rule, be enforced by courts of equity, because the execution of the decree would require such constant superintendence as to make judicial control a matter of extreme difficulty." Standard Fashions Co. v. Siegel-Cooper Co., 157 N. Y. 60, 66, 51 N. E. 

408, 43 L. R. A. 854, 68 Am. St. Rep. 749, Bachrach's

Cases in Equity, p. 687. In Breeden v. Hopkins, 210 App. 

Div. 412, 206 N. Y. S. 282, the court refused to decree specific performance of a contract to drill an oil well in Wyoming. See, also, Cuban Production Co. v. Rodriguez, 124 

App. Div. 363, 108 N. Y. S. 785, and authorities cited in 

Standard Fashion Co. v. Siegel-Cooper Co., supra.

An agreement for the rendition of personal services will not be enforced specifically, although where the services are unique and extraordinary an injunction may be granted preventing the services being rendered for someone else. (See section 151, infra.) This has been called negative specific performance. McCall Co. v. Wright, 198 N. Y. 143, 91 N. E. 516, 31 L. R. A. (N. S.) 249; Dockstader v. Reed, 121 App. Div. 846, 106 N. Y. S. 795; Posner Co. v. Jackson, 223 N. Y. 325, 119 N. E. 573.

Equity will not decree specific performance when it is impossible for the defendant to perform. Saperstein v. 

Mechanics and Farmers Sav. Bank, 228 N. Y. 257, 126 N. 

E. 708. 

§ 135. Mutuality. 

In some of the earlier cases (See especially Wadick v. 

Mace, 191 N. Y. 1, 83 N. E. 571, and authorities therein

SPECIFIC PERFORMANCE 

139 cited) the rule was laid down that in order to warrant a decree of specific performance the contract must be mutual in its obligation and remedy; in other words, that where a contract cannot be specifically enforced against one of the parties equity will not enforce it against the other. The question usually arises where a vendee assigns a contract for the sale of land and the assignee does not, by the terms of the assignment, assume the obligations of the contract. 

In such case, there is no privity of contract between the vendor and assignee and the court will not, therefore, compel specific performance at the suit of the vendor unless there be other evidence that the vendee has assumed the duty of performance. The leading modern case is Epstein v. Gluckin, 233 N. Y. 490, 135 N. E. 861. In that case the vendee had assigned a contract for the sale of land, the assignee not assuming the obligations of the contract. 

When the assignee sued for specific performance he was met by the contention of the vendor that the suit would not lie since the vendor could not have enforced specific performance against the assignee by reason of the fact that the assignee had not assumed the obligations of the contract. The Court ruled, however, that the suit would lie, saying, at p. 493: "If there ever was a rule that mutuality of remedy existing, not merely at the time of the decree, but at the time of the formation of the contract, is a condition of equitable relief, it has been so qualified by exceptions that, viewed as a precept of general validity, it has ceased to be a rule today (citing authorities). What equity exacts today as a condition of relief is the assurance that the decree, if rendered, will operate without injustice or oppression either to plaintiff or to defendant (citing authorities).

Mutuality of remedy is important in so far only as its presence is essential to the attainment of that end. The formula had its origin in an attempt to fit the equitable remedy to the needs of equal justice. We may not suffer it to petrify at the cost of its animating principle."

The Court also held that by bringing the suit in equity the assignee assumed the duty of performance and subjected himself to the conditions of the judgment. This case was followed by H. & H. Corporation v. Broad Holding Cor poration, 204 App. Div. 569, 198 N. Y. S. 763, in which the Appellate Division held that where the assignee demands performance of the contract, the rule of Epstein v. Gluckin, supra, applies and specific performance may be enforced against him. No appeal was taken to the Court of Appeals. 

This was followed by Langel v. Betz, 250 N. Y. 159, 164

N. E. 890, where the court held that a request of the assignee for an adjournment of the closing date was not sufficient proof that the assignee assumed the duty of performance. The court, however, added this significant paragraph to its opinion: "No controlling authority can be found which holds that a mere demand for performance by the vendee's assignee creates the right in the complaining vendor to enforce the contract against him. (H. & H. Corporation v. Broad Holding Corporation, 204 App. Div. 569, 198 

N. Y. S. 763.) That question may be reserved until an answer is necessary." 

The next case was Ballen v. Potter, 251 N. Y. 224, 167

N. E. 424, where the assignee sued the vendor and vendee, Payne, for the down payment, claiming that on an adjourned date of closing they were not ready and willing to perform. The defendants counterclaimed for specific performance. The court held that an additional adjournment should have been granted the vendor to enable him to perfect his title since time was not of the essence. (See Sec. 

137.) The closing paragraph of the opinion states: "A majority of the judges of the court are of the opinion that under our rulings in Crowley v. Lewis, 239 

N. Y. 264, 146 N. E. 374, and Langel v. Betz, 250 N. Y. 

159, 164 N. E. 890, the remedy of specific performance should have been confined to the defendant Payne and

SPECIFIC PERFORMANCE 

141 that the relief as to the plaintiffs should be limited to a dismissal of the complaint. As to this, the writer of this opinion holds a different view."

Crowley v. Lewis, referred to in this paragraph, holds that a contract under seal cannot be enforced against a party not named in the contract. By the reference to Langel v. Betz, the court evidently held that the acts of the assignee in being ready, able and willing to perform were not sufficient to constitute an assumption of the duty of performance.

It seems difficult to reconcile all that was said in Epstein v. Gluckin, supra, with what has been held in the later cases. 

In these cases the court seems to have based its decisions solely on the question whether there was sufficient evidence to show express or implied assumption of the obligations of the contract.

The cases seem to have definitely established two rules: (1) That by bringing a suit in equity to compel performance, the assignee assumes the duty of performing the contract; (2) that by asking for an adjournment of the closing of title he does not, nor does the fact that the assignee is ready, able and willing to perform on the closing date, show such assumption. The effect of the assignee's demanding performance or suing for the down payment have not been definitely passed upon, nor has the court indicated what other acts it would hold were sufficient to show assumption of the duty of performance.

The Appellate Division has gone further than the Court of Appeals. Thus the First Department in Forsyth Leas ing Company v. Sacks, 225 App. Div. 440, 233 N. Y. S. 395, held that where a vendor had conveyed to a grantee with notice, the grantee could counterclaim for specific performance against an assignee of a contract of sale who sued to recover the down payment. The Second Department in 

Epstein v. Kroopf, 218 App. Div. 519, 218 N. Y. S. 644, held that a purchaser from one who held a contract was

entitled to a vendee's lien against the original vendor. It will be necessary to wait until the Court of Appeals authoritatively settles these and like questions. 

Specific performance of a contract which provided for part payment by the execution of a bond and mortgage cannot be compelled at the suit of the assignee where the bond and mortgage of the original vendor are not tendered. 

Lojo Realty Co. v. Johnson's Estates, 227 App. Div. 292, 

237 N. Y. S. 460, aff'd., 253 N. Y. 579, 171 N. E. 791. 

On the theory of Epstein v. Gluckin, supra, an option to purchase property at a stipulated price has been held capable of specific performance. Crocker v. Page, 210 App. 

Div. 735, 206 N. Y. S. 481, affd. 240 N. Y. 638, 148 N. E. 738.

Since specific performance is based upon the contract, one who has himself refused to perform or has rescinded the contract is not entitled to specific performance. Steinhardt v. Baker, 163 N. Y. 410, 57 N. E. 629; Whalen v. 

Stuart, 194 N. Y. 495, 87 N. E. 819. 

§ 136. 

Demand for Performance. 

Where the contract does not make a request or demand essential to the right to specific performance no demand is necessary. The plaintiff must, however, plead performance or a readiness and willingness to perform on his part. 

Bruce v. Tilson, 25 N. Y. 194; Freeson v. Bissel, 63 N. 

Y. 168. 

§ 137. Time Not of Essence. 

Unless clearly made so by the contract, time is not of the essence of a contract to convey real property and, where the situation of the parties has not been so changed as to render it inequitable, a court of equity will decree specific performance notwithstanding a delay on the part of one of the contracting parties. Day v. Hunt, 112 N. Y. 

191, 19 N. E. 414, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 699;

SPECIFIC PERFORMANCE 

143 

Ballen v. Potter, 251 N. Y. 224, 167 N. E. 424. On the same principle, if the vendor is unable to perform at the time of the commencement of the action on account of a defect in his title but is able to perfect his title while the action is pending, equity will decree specific performance. 

Haffney v. Lynch, 143 N. Y. 241, 38 N. E. 298, Bachrach's 

Cases in Equity, p. 701; Schiffer v. Dietz, 83 N. Y. 300, 312; 

Weinheimer v. Ross, 205 N. Y. 518, 99 N. E. 145. 

§ 138. Parol Modification of Contract Under Seal. 

While specific performance of a contract under seal will not be decreed against an undisclosed principal (Crowley v. Lewis, 239 N. Y. 264, 146 N. E. 347) and while a contract under seal may not be modified by an oral executory agreement (Cammack v. Slattery, 241 N. Y. 39, 148 N. E. 

781), as previously discussed (sec. 96, supra) there may be such reliance upon an oral modification of a contract under seal as to make it inequitable not to enforce specific performance. Harris v. Shorall, 230 N. Y. 343, 136 N. E. 

572; Imperator Realty Co. v. Tull, 228 N. Y. 447, 127 N. 

E. 263; Nat. Cash Register Co. v. Remington Arms Co., 242 N. Y. 99, 151 N. E. 144. 

$139. Doubtful Titles. 

Equity will not compel a purchaser to accept a doubtful title; or, as has been aptly stated, equity will not compel a purchaser to buy a law suit. Pomeroy's Specific Performance of Contracts, 3d ed., sec. 203, note, and authorities there cited; Fleming v. Burnham, 100 N. Y. 1, 2 N. E. 

905; Cerf v. Diener, 210 N. Y. 156, 104 N. E. 126; Chesebro. v. Moers, 233 N. Y. 75, 134 N. E. 842, 21 A. L. R. 1270; 

Brokaw v. Duffy, 165 N. Y. 391, 59 N. E. 196. 

§ 140. 

Allowance for Incumbrances.

In an action for specific performance of a contract for the sale of real property, if there is an incumbrance upon the property covenanted to be conveyed and the prospect-

ive purchaser is willing to take title if an allowance be made for such incumbrance, the court can fix and determine an abatement therefor. For example, specific performance with abatement from the purchase price may be decreed where the wife of the vendor refuses to join in the deed. Feldman v. Lisinsky, 239 N. Y. 81, 145 N. E. 746; 

Bostwick v. Beach, 103 N. Y. 414, 9 N. E. 4, Bachrach's 

Cases in Equity, p. 705; Jones v. Barnes, 105 App. Div. 

287, 94 N. Y. S. 695. Where there is an unsubstantial variation due to the exercise of the right of eminent domain after the signing of the contract, the vendee may be compelled to take, but not where the variance is substantial. 

Compare Reife v. Osmers, 252 N. Y.320, 169 N. E. 399, with 

Rifkin v. Ed. Zit Holding Corporation, 254 N. Y. 352, 173 

N. E. 219. 

§ 141. Statute of Frauds-Part Performance. 

While the statute of frauds requires that all contracts for the sale of real property or any interest therein must be in writing (Real Prop. Law, sec. 259) there is a further provision that nothing contained in the article abridges the powers of equity to compel specific performance of agreements in case of part performance (id., sec. 270). This is but a recognition by statute of a long established equitable principle and by it "the legislature declared its intention not to interfere with that well-recognized head of equity which decrees specific performance of a contract within the provisions of the statute of frauds, when the parol agreement has been partly carried into execution." Canda v. Totten, 157 N. Y. 281, 287, 51 N. E. 989. "It has been long the settled rule, in England, as here, that when a parol agreement for the conveyance of real estate, void by the statute of frauds, has been proved and part performance has been shown by the acts of the party seeking relief, which could have been done with no other design than that of performance, if an action at law is not an adequate remedy, the agreement will be specifically en-

SPECIFIC PERFORMANCE 

145 forced." McKinley v. Hessen, 202 N. Y. 24, at p. 30, 95 

N. E. 32, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 712. See, also,

Lobdell v. Lobdell, 36 N. Y. 327; Cooley v. Lobdell, 153 

N. Y. 596, 47 N. E. 783; Dunckel v. Dunckel, 141 N. Y. 

427, 36 N. E. 405, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 716.

The theory on which these cases are decided is that, not to give effect to part performance would be to allow the party permitting the acts to treat them as though the agreement had not been made. Where the party who has in part, performed his agreement cannot be placed in the same situation in which he was before the performance, an irreparable injury is threatened and equity will intervene on the ground that it would be a fraud if the transaction were not completed.

Where, therefore, a party enters upon land and makes improvements thereon in pursuance of an oral agreement to convey the land, equity will compel the conveyance. Freeman v. Freeman, 43 N. Y. 34, 3 Am. Rep. 657, Bachrach's

Cases in Equity, p. 678; Messiah Home for Children v. 

Rogers, 212 N. Y. 315, 106 N. E. 59; Canda v. Totten, supra; McKinley v. Hessen, supra.

It is doubtful whether the entering upon land and making improvements is really a part performance of the contract to convey. These acts would seem rather to be evidence that a contract was made and the true theory would seem to be that by permitting these acts on the faith of the agreement, the vendor is estopped to set up the Statute. 

However, for convenience, the courts have grouped acts which indicated ownership with acts which constituted part performance of the contract and have discussed both under the rule of part performance. (See discussion of Judge 

Vann in Cooley v. Lobdell, 153 N. Y. 596, 602, 47 N. E. 783, and cases cited below). As stated by the Court in the leading case of Burns v. McCormick, 233 N. Y. 230, 232, 135 

N. E. 273: "Not every act of part performance will move a court of equity, though legal remedies are inadequate, to enforce

an oral agreement affecting rights in land. There must be performance ‘unequivocally referable' to the agreement, performance which alone and without the aid of words of promise is unintelligible or at least extraordinary unless as an incident of ownership, assured, if not existing."

In that case it appeared that the decedent promised the plaintiffs that if they gave up their home and business and boarded with and cared for decedent during his life, his house and lot, with its furniture and equipment, would be theirs upon his death. The plaintiffs performed their part of the agreement but the decedent failed to leave them the property. The statute of frauds was interposed as a defense to the plaintiffs' suit for specific performance. The 

Court held that the defense was good and that the acts of part performance were not "solely and unequivocally referable to a contract for the sale of land." 

In Neverman v. Neverman, 254 N. Y. 496, 173 N. E. 838, a son constructed a house on land belonging to his mother, partly with his own money, paid taxes and insurance and made repairs, under her oral agreement to leave him the property upon her death, but the court held that these acts were insufficient to take the contract out of the Statute of 

Frauds. See also, Sleeth v. Sampson, 237 N. Y. 69, 142 

N. E. 355; Woolley v. Stewart, 222 N. Y. 347, 118 N. E. 847. 

The cases which treat acts indicating ownership, other than taking possession and making improvements, as acts of part performance, generally have some element of confidential relationship involved and deal with property which has been obtained under an oral contract by reason of the confidential relationship as distinguished from a contract to convey property in which claimant theretofore had no interest. These cases would seem more properly to come under the head of Constructive Trusts arising from fraud which have been discussed heretofore (Section 81), and to have no place in the treatment of specific performance, except as a means of enforcing the trust. The courts, how-

SPECIFIC PERFORMANCE 

147 ever, have seemed to combine the two. See Jeremiah v. 

Pitcher, 26 App. Div. 402, aff'd. 163 N. Y. 574, 57 N. E. 

1113; Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 141, where a father conveyed to his daughter under her oral agreement to convey to his nominee and his acts of collecting the rents, paying the taxes, water rates, etc., were referred to as acts of part performance; Foreman v. Foreman, 251 N. Y. 237, 167 

N. E. 428, where the husband paid the consideration and had title taken in his wife's name under her oral agreement to convey to him, and where his acts of collecting the rents, paying the taxes, insurance, interest and costs of improvements were referred to as acts of part performance. 

See also, Sinclair v. Purdy, 235 N. Y. 245, 139 N. E. 255; 

Gallagher v. Gallagher, 135 App. Div. 457, aff'd. 202 N. Y. 

572, 96 N. E. 1115.

No decision of the Court of Appeals has yet held that mere confidential relationship, standing alone, was sufficient to take a contract for the conveyance of land out of the Statute of Frauds, which did not also involve this doctrine of unjust enrichment. Wood v. Rabe, 96 N. Y. 414; 

Goldsmith v. Goldsmith, 145 N. Y. 313, 39 N. E. 1067.

The true doctrine would therefore seem to be that equity will impose a trust where property has been acquired under an oral agreement, induced by a confidential relationship, and where acts of the parties in dealing with the property are evidence of the agreement, but that specific performance of a contract for the conveyance of land, where there are no such questions involved, will only be granted in cases involving acts of true part performance of the agreement as defined above.

Payment of the consideration, alone, is not such part performance as will take the case out of the Statute. Miller v. 

Ball, 64 N. Y. 286; Cooley v. Lobdell, supra; Cogger v. 

Lansing, 43 N. Y. 550; Milholland v. Payne, 169 App. Div. 

712, aff'd. 218 N. Y. 675, 113 N. E. 1061; Russell v. Briggs, 165 N. Y. 500, 59 N. E. 303.

The courts have given a reason for this holding, that since the purchase money can be recovered back in an action at law and the parties thus restored to their original positions, there is no occasion for a resort to equity. Cooley v. 

Lobdell, supra.

An ante-nuptial agreement to leave property by will in consideration of marriage is taken out of the statute by the marriage and the execution of the will. Adams v. 

Swift, 169 App. Div. 802, 155 N. Y. S. 873; Lyons v. The 

Maccabees, 192 App. Div. 109, 182 N. Y. S. 212. Marriage alone is not sufficient. Hunt v. Hunt, 171 N. Y. 396, 64 

N. E. 159, 59 L. R. A. 306. 

§ 142. Parties Compelled to Make Good Their Represen tations.

As already noticed under the subject of Fraud, parties may be compelled in equity to make good their representations by conveying land. This remedy is usually applied by imposing a trust ex maleficio. A striking case, already referred to, is Piper v. Hoard, 107 N. Y. 73, 13 N. E. 626, 1 Am. St. Rep. 789, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 404. 

See sec. 76, supra.

CHAPTER XVI INJUNCTIONS § 143. Mandatory and Prohibitory Injunctions Defined. 

An injunction is a process of a court of equity commanding a defendant to perform some act or restraining a defendant from the commission or continuance of an act. The former is known as a mandatory injunction; the latter, as a prohibitory injunction. Bispham's Principles of Equity, 9th ed., secs. 399, 400. An example of a mandatory injunction may be found in Corning v. Troy Iron and Nail Factory, 40 N. Y. 191, in which a defendant was compelled to restore water to its natural channel, from which he had unlawfully diverted it. An injunction to restrain a defendant from negotiating a promissory note procured by fraud would be an example of a prohibitory injunction. See Gillette Clipping Machine Co. v. Elting, 170 App. Div. 185, 155 N. Y. S. 989. 

§ 144. Injunctions Classified as to Time of Issuance. 

As to time of issuance, injunctions are divided into three classes, as follows: (1) Restraining order or stay. This is issued on such notice as the court or judge may direct. C. P. A., sec. 882.

It is effective until the hearing and decision of a motion for an injunction pendente lite. (2) Preliminary or temporary injunction. This is issued after a hearing, upon affidavits, of a motion and is effective until the decision of the court after trial upon the merits. 

The subject of preliminary injunctions, or injunctions pendente lite, will be discussed more fully in the following section. (3) Permanent injunction. This is issued after trial on the merits. 149

§ 145. Preliminary or Temporary Injunctions. 

As stated by Mr. Justice Taft, in Toledo, A. A. & N. M. 

Ry. Co. v. Pennsylvania Co., 54 Fed. 730, 741: "The office of a preliminary or temporary injunction is to preserve the status quo until, upon final hearing, the court may grant full relief. Generally, this can be accomplished by an injunction prohibitory in form, but it sometimes happens that the status quo is a condition not of rest, but of action, and the condition of rest is exactly what will inflict the irreparable injury upon complainant, which he appeals to a court of equity to protect him from. In such a case, courts of equity issue mandatory writs before the case is heard on its merits."

An injunction, especially a mandatory one, will, therefore, rarely issue before trial, where it would give the plaintiff practically all the relief he could get by the trial of the action. Bachman v. Harrington, 184 N. Y. 458, 77 N. E. 

657, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 726. An illustrative case is Yome v. Gorman, 242 N. Y. 395, 152 N. E. 126, in which relatives attempted to remove the remains of certain persons from a Roman Catholic Cemetery to unconsecrated ground. This attempt was resisted by the Cemetery and the court issued a temporary injunction to restrain the 

Cemetery authorities from preventing the removal. The injunction was reversed, the Court of Appeals saying, at p. 401: "The order restrains the defendants from interfering with the removal of the bodies by the plaintiff during the pendency of the action. If it stands there will be nothing left to try, for the bodies will be removed before the cause is brought to a hearing. Such an injunction, if ever permissible in advance of final judgment, is plainly inappropriate unless the undisputed facts are such that a trial is a futility (citing Bachman v. Harrington, supra). If there are motives to be probed and opposing equities to be weighed there must be the searching scrutiny of a trial and the sanction of a judgment."

INJUNCTIONS 

151 

A preliminary injunction is unauthorized when it does not appear from the complaint that the plaintiff is entitled to the final relief for which the action is brought. McHenry v. Jewett, 90 N. Y. 58, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 724; 

Selchow v. Baker, 93 N. Y. 59. 

The remedy being discretionary (Castoriano v. Dupe, 145 

N. Y. 250, 39 N. E. 1065) an injunction pendente lite will not issue where it would be of no advantage to the plaintiff and would seriously damage the defendant (Barney v. City of New York, 83 App. Div. 237, 82 N. Y. S. 124, Bachrach's

Cases in Equity, p. 721); where the plaintiff suffers no damage (Loukes v. Payne, 140 App. Div. 776, 125 N. Y. S. 

850); where the plaintiff has delayed unduly (Tonawanda, etc., Co. v. Tonawanda, 198 App. Div. 760, 190 N. Y. S. 

874); or where other circumstance makes the relief inequitable (Herman v. Hartwood Holding Co., Inc., 193 

App. Div. 115, 183 N. Y. S. 402; People v. Clark, 139 App. 

Div. 687, 124 N. Y. S. 527; Flynn v. N. Y. W. & B. Ry. Co., 139 App. Div. 199, 123 N. Y. S. 759). 

§ 146. 

Injunction to Restrain Action at Law. 

As stated by Pomeroy, in his Equity Jurisprudence, 4th ed., sec. 1380: "The use of injunctions to stay actions at law was almost coeval with the establishment of the chancery jurisdiction. 

It is no exaggeration to say that, during its formative periods, the equitable jurisdiction was built up through the instrumentality of the injunction restraining the prosecution of legal actions where the defendants sought the aid of chancery * * *. This was not accomplished, however, without a long and severe opposition from the common law judges which continued until the reign of James I. The jurisdiction then firmly established by judicial authority has never since been questioned."

It should be noted that the litigants and not the courts are restrained. Western R. R. Co. v. Nolan, 48 N. Y. 513.

​The prosecution of an action in a foreign jurisdiction may be enjoined. For example, in Greenberg v. Greenberg, 218 App. Div. 104, 218 N. Y. S. 87, the defendant was enjoined from prosecuting an action for divorce in Mexico, the matrimonial domicile being in New York and neither party ever having resided in Mexico.

Equity will not entertain an action to restrain the prosecution of an action at law unless special reasons demonstrate that full justice cannot be done in the latter action. 

If the equitable action holds out no promise of relief which may not be secured in the legal proceeding, there is no occasion for interference. 'Burke v. Burke, 212 N. Y. 

303, 106 N. E. 62. In the case last cited it was held that the validity of certain settlements which had been made could be determined as well in the pending action at law as they could in an equitable action to restrain the legal proceeding. On the other hand, in Bomeisler v. Forster, 154 N. Y. 229, 48 N. E. 534, 39 L. R. A. 240, Bachrach's

Cases in Equity, p. 34, in which it appeared that releases had been given to avoid the scandal and disrepute which would follow the trial of the legal action, it was held that equity would enjoin, since the validity of the releases could be tried out in the equitable action without going into the issues involved in the legal proceeding. 

§ 147. Multiplicity of Suits. 

One of the recognized heads of equitable jurisdiction to restrain proceedings at law is to prevent a multiplicity of actions. In Norfolk & New Brunswick Hosiery Co. v. Ar nold, 143 N. Y. 265, 38 N. E. 271, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 731, for example, it appeared that the defendant had brought three successive actions to recover installments of royalties which came due at different times and threatened to bring successive actions as further installments became due. The court enjoined the prosecution of the suits until the matter in controversy could be decided. The same ruling was made in Third Ave. Railroad Co. v. Mayor, 54

INJUNCTIONS 

153 N. Y. 159, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 734, which was an action to restrain the prosecution of more than one of seventy-seven actions which had been brought to recover a penalty.

The parties to the actions must be the same. Dolbeer v. 

Stout, 139 N. Y. 486, 34 N. É. 1102. 

See, also, Municipal Gas Co. v. Public Service Comm., 225 

N. Y. 89, 121 N. E. 772, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 740; 

Board of Supervisors v. Deyoe, 77 N. Y. 219, Bachrach's 

Cases in Equity, p. 750. 

§ 148. 

Numerous Claimants to Common Fund. 

The principle applicable to a situation where there are numerous claimants to a common fund is well illustrated by 

Guffanti v. Nat. Surety Co., 196 N. Y. 452, 90 N. E. 174, 134 Am. St. Rep. 848, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 743, in which it appeared that a person with whom more than one hundred and fifty persons had deposited money had converted the money to his own use and disappeared, leaving only a limited fund to satisfy the numerous claimants. was held that a court of equity could best supervise the distribution of the money pro rata among the various claimants. The defendant, having by legal proceedings delayed the distribution of the fund for four years, was also charged with interest thereon. id., 234 N. Y. 192, 137 N. E. 22. It

So where several persons are injured as a result of a single accident and the wrongdoer is insolvent, a judgment creditor may bring an action in behalf of himself and all others similarly situated, for an equitable distribution of the fund provided by the bond furnished in accordance with sec. 282b of the Highway Law. In such an action, equity will fix a time within which creditors must prove their claims and then distribute the fund equitably. Bleimeyer v. Public Service &c. Co., 250 N. Y. 264, 165 N. E. 286.

The same principles have been applied to a suit in behalf of all the creditors of a corporation to enforce the liability

of stockholders. Mathez v. Neidig, 72 N. Y. 100; Hirshfeld v. Fitzgerald, 157 N. Y. 166, 51 N. E. 997, 46 L. R. A. 839. 

See, also, Pfohl v. Simpson, 74 N. Y. 137; Bouton v. Van 

Buren, 229 N. Y. 17, 127 N. E. 477. 

§ 149. Injunction to Prevent Enforcement of Judgment 

Obtained at Law.

The rule has been stated by Chief Justice Marshall, in 

Marine Ins. Co. of Alexandria v. Hodgson, 7 Cranch 332, 336, 3 L. ed. 362, as follows: "Any fact which clearly proves it to be against conscience to execute a judgment, and of which the injured party could not have availed himself in a court of law; or of which he might have availed himself at law, but was prevented by fraud or accident unmixed with any fault or negligence in himself or his agents, will justify an application to a court of Chancery."

For example, where a defendant is prevented from defending by false and fraudulent promises or representations that the proceedings against him will not be prosecuted further, or when his witnesses have been fraudulently tampered with or removed, or he is absent from sickness, accident or other justifiable reason, or where he has not been properly summoned or notified, relief will be given. Mayor of City of New York v. Brady, 115 N. Y. 599, 617, 22 N. E. 

237; Dobson v. Pearce, 12 N. Y. 156, 62 Am. Dec. 152,

Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 2. Thus, a surety may impeach collaterally a judgment obtained by fraudulent collusion between the plaintiff and the defendant for the purpose of charging the surety. Brownell v. Snyder, 122 App. Div. 

246, 106 N. Y. S. 771. 

In N. Y. & Harlem R. R. Co. v. Haws, 56 N. Y. 175, it appeared that a finder of a sum of money on a train delivered it to the railroad company. He subsequently brought an action against the railroad company and recovered judgment. The real owner then appearing, the court enjoined the enforcement of the judgment.

INJUNCTIONS 

155 In conformity with long settled principles, where the relief can be secured at law, either by interposing a defense in the original action or by a motion for a new trial, equity will not intervene. To continue the quotation from Marine 

Ins. Co. of Alexandria v. Hodgson, supra: "It is not sufficient to authorize the interference of the court that it is shown that the claim upon which the judgment was obtained was unfounded or that there was a good defense to the action or that the court erroneously decided the law or that the defendant omitted to avail himself of his defense if before the judgment the facts were known or might by the exercise of reasonable diligence have been ascertained by him. It is the duty of a defendant to make his defense if he has any when he is sued and if he omits to do it he is, in general, concluded by the judgment." 

Thus, in Fuhrman v. Fanroth, 254 N. Y. 479, 173 N. E. 

685, the claim was based on fraud in the original transaction on which the judgment was based, and the court declined to interfere, holding that the defense should have been presented in the original action. The court said that the basis of the action in equity is fraud in procuring the judgment.

The fraud for which a judgment may be impeached must be in some matter other than the issue in controversy in the action. In Crouse v. McVicker, 207 N. Y. 213, 100 N. E. 

697, 45 L. R. A. (N. S.) 1159, it was held that the trial court properly refused to intervene where it was claimed that a stipulation on which a judgment was entered was procured by fraud, for the reason that the application should be made in the original action. The Court of Appeals, at p. 218 of its opinion, quoted with approval the following statement from United States v. Throckmorton, 98 U. S. 61, 66: 1 "On the other hand, the doctrine is equally well settled that the court will not set aside a judgment because it was founded on a fraudulent instrument, or perjured evidence,

or for any matter which was actually presented and considered in the judgment assailed.”

In another action to set aside a judgment obtained at law, it was held that a complaint which charged that the defendants had conspired with others to cheat and defraud the plaintiff by perjury and false testimony, and thus obtained a judgment against him, did not state a cause of action. Ross v. Wood, 70 N. Y. 8. A similar case is Mayor of City of New 

York v. Brady, supra. See, also, Standard Fashion Co. v. 

Thompson, 137 App. Div. 588, 122 N. Y. S. 300, and authorities therein cited; O'Neil v. McKinley Music Co., 214 App. 

Div. 181, 212 N. Y. S. 7. 

In Ingalls v. Merchants' Nat. Bank, 51 App. Div. 305, 307, 64 N. Y. S. 911, the Court quoted with approval the following statement of the rule in Adams' Doctrine of 

Equity, p. 197: "First: That if, after judgment, additional circumstances are discovered not cognizable at law, but converting the controversy into one of equitable jurisdiction, the Court of 

Chancery will interpose. "Second: That even though the circumstances so discovered would have been cognizable at law, if known in time, yet if their non-discovery has been caused by fraudulent concealment, the fraud will warrant an injunction. "Third: But that if the newly discovered facts would have been cognizable at law, and there has been no fraudulent concealment, the mere fact of their late discovery will not of itself create an equity * ** And still less can any equity arise if the facts were known at the time of the trial and the grievance complained of has been caused either by a mistake in pleading, or other mismanagement, or by a supposed error in the judgment of the court." 

§ 150. Interpleader Defined. 

"The definition of interpleader is not, and cannot now be, disputed. It is where the plaintiff says, I have a fund in my

INJUNCTIONS 

157 possession in which I claim no personal interest, and to which you, the defendants, set up conflicting claims; pay me my costs, and I will bring the fund into court, and you shall contest it between yourselves." Hogart v. Cutts, Cr. & Ph. 

197, 18 Eng. Ch. 197, 41 Eng. Repr. 465. The Court will then enjoin the prosecution of an action by either defendant against the plaintiff. 

For example, in Crane v. McDonald, 118 N. Y. 648, 23 

N. E. 991, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 746, the plaintiff held in his hands a sum of money due from him on a contract with one Jennie L. Graves. The sum was claimed by 

Martha McDonald as assignee and by George E. Goodrich on the ground that he had an attorney's lien under which he had procured an attachment. Upon the payment of the money into court, the Court interpleaded the parties and enjoined the prosecution of an action brought by Mrs. 

McDonald against the plaintiff. The Court, at p. 654, stated that the essentials to a bill of interpleader are the following: "(1) That two or more persons have preferred a claim against the complainant; (2) that they claim the same thing; (3) that the complainant has no beneficial interest in the thing claimed; and (4) that he cannot determine, without hazard to himself, to which of the defendants the thing belongs.' 99 

In Brook v. Randolph, 201 App. Div. 30, 193 N. Y. S. 565, two parties had brought an action against defendant for the same funds and the court allowed interpleader by order, pursuant to the provisions of section 287 of the Civil Practice Act. The right has also been granted where rival brokers claimed the same commission. Trembley v. Marshall, 118 App. Div. 839, 103 N. Y. S. 680; Dardonville v. 

Smith, 133 App. Div. 234, 117 N. Y. S. 216.

The plaintiff must show not only that there is an adverse claim but that such adverse claim has some reasonable basis on which to rest. Pouch v. Prudential Ins. Co., 204 N. Y.

281, 97 N. E. 731, Ann. Cas. 1913 C 1191. When the amount is in dispute and the plaintiff does not stand indifferent between the claimants, the action cannot be maintained. 

Empire Engineering Corp. v. Mack, 217 N. Y. 85, 111 N. E. 

475. Where the party disputes the claim, the proper procedure would seem to be to bring all the parties into court in the law action. Sec. 287 C. P. A.

The practice in New York is governed by sections 285, 286 and 287 of the Civil Practice Act. The action, however, is still equitable. Clark v. Mosher, 107 N. Y. 118, 14 N. E. 

96, 1 Am. St. Rep. 798; White v. White, 194 N. Y. S. 114. 

§ 151. Actions in Nature of Interpleader. 

A bill in the nature of an interpleader is one in which the complainant asks some relief over and above a mere injunction against suit by the contesting parties, and states facts which entitle him to such relief independent of the adverse claims of the several defendants. Van Winkle v. 

Owen, 54 N. J. Eq. 253, 34 Atl. 400. See, also, Board of 

Supervisors v. Deyoe, 77 N. Y. 219, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 750. For example, where a mortgagor wishes to redeem a mortgaged estate, and there are conflicting claims between third persons as to their title to the mortgage money, he may bring them before the court to ascertain their rights, and to have a decree of redemption, and to make a secure payment to the party entitled to the money. 

By such bill the plaintiff seeks relief as well as protection. 

Pusey & Jones Co. v. Miller, 61 Fed. 401.

However, a court of equity, as a rule, will not take cognizance of distinct and separate claims of different persons in one suit, when the court is unable to dispose of the controversy in a single action. Under such circumstances a bill in the nature of interpleader may be denied. Empire Engineering Corp. v. Mack, 217 N. Y. 85, 111 N. E. 475.

In one instance, a party had received a commission by falsely representing that he had been the procuring cause

INJUNCTIONS 

159 of a contract. In an action by another party who alleged that he was entitled to such commission, it was held that the party possibly liable to pay the commission to one or the other of the parties could file a plea in the nature of interpleader, against the party who had defrauded him, in order to settle the matter. Coombs v. Jackson, 238 S. W. (Tex.) 992. 

§ 152. 

Jurisdiction of Equity to Enjoin Waste, Trespass and Nuisance. 

The common law courts exercised exclusive jurisdiction over cases of waste and private nuisance, the action for damages being regarded as adequate. In the development of equity, however, this view changed, and the jurisdiction of equity to enjoin threatened waste, nuisance and continuous trespass is now firmly established. Pomeroy's Equity 

Jurisprudence, 4th ed., sec. 237. A court of equity may also award damages when they are incidental to the equitable relief, but not otherwise. Sadlier v. City of New 

York, 185 N. Y. 408, 78 N. E. 272. 

§ 153. Waste. 

As stated by the Court in Kane v. Vanderburgh, 1 Johns. 

Ch. 11, at p. 12, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 758: "Chancery goes greater lengths than the courts of law in staying waste. It is a wholesome jurisdiction, to be liberally exercised in the prevention of irreparable injury, and depends on much latitude of discretion in the Court.” 

The common law remedies for waste were insufficient, chiefly because the common law court did not put a stop to the injury which was going on. Hence, courts of equity interposed by injunction to restrain persons from continuing to commit waste, and the equitable remedy has proven so simple and effective that it has to a great extent superseded the common law remedies. Palmer v. Young, 108 

Ill. App. 252.

Equity will not intervene in cases of tenants in common even to restrain waste unless it be of a malicious character or so unusual or unreasonable as to constitute a wanton destruction of the estate. Mott v. Underwood, 148 N. Y. 463, 42 N. E. 1048, 32 L. R. A. 270. But See, Sec. 525, R. P. L.

Equity will, in many cases, restrain waste, although the lease under which the defendant tenant holds contains the clause "without impeachment of waste," which takes away the remedy at law, as where the tenant exercises his power in an unreasonable manner, and against conscience. Kane v. Vanderburgh, supra; Duncome v. Felt, 81 Mich. 332, 45 

N. W. 1004.

As a rule, equity will interfere only to prevent or stay future waste. For example, where a tenant has committed waste by chopping down trees, the timber thus cut has become converted into personal property and trover will lie for it. In cases wherein equity grants relief for waste already committed, as for example, an accounting, it is merely incidental to the jurisdiction assumed to prevent a multiplicity of suits and to save the party from the necessity of resorting to an action of trover in a court of law. 

Watson v. Hunter, 5 Johns. Ch. 11, 9 Am. Dec. 295. 

Under New York practice, an injunction to restrain waste may issue during the pendency of any action to recover a judgment affecting the title to, or the possession, use or enjoyment of real property. C. P. A., sec. 981. See, also, Real Prop. Law, secs. 520, et seq; Littlejohn v. Leffingwell, 40 App. Div. 13, 57 N. Y. S. 839. 

§ 154. 

Trespass.

The use of injunctions to prevent trespass is of comparatively modern origin. See opinion of Chancellor Kent in 

Livingston v. Livingston, 6 Johns. Ch. 497, 10 Am. Dec. 353.

However, even in this case, in 1822, Chancellor Kent recognized the power of a court of equity to grant an injunction to prevent a trespass where the mischief would be ir-

INJUNCTIONS 

161 reparable or a multiplicity of suits could be avoided. To the same effect is Wheelock v. Noonan, 108 N. Y. 179, 15 

N. E. 67, 2 Am. St. Rep. 405, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 760. It is still true that the unquestioned jurisdiction of equity to restrain a trespass is sparingly used and it must appear that the remedy at law is inadequate. Donovan v. 

Kissena Park Corp., 181 App. Div. 737, 168 N. Y. S. 1035,

Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 764. Equity will restrain the maintenance of an easement when such maintenance is continuous and an action at law relating thereto will be inadequate. Heyman v. Biggs, 223 N. Y. 118, 119 N. E. 243. 

In Jerome v. Ross, 7 Johns. Ch. 315, 11 Am. Dec. 484, Chancellor Kent said: "The objection to the injunction in cases of private trespass, except under very special circumstances, is that it would be productive of public inconvenience, by drawing cases of ordinary trespass within the cognizance of equity, and by calling forth upon all occasions its power to punish by attachment, fine and imprisonment, for a further commission of trespass, instead of the more gentle common law remedy by action, and the assessment of damages by a jury. In ordinary cases, this latter remedy has been found amply sufficient for the protection of property, and I do not think it advisable, upon any principle of justice or policy, to introduce the chancery remedy as its substitute, except in strong and aggravated instances of trespass, which go to the destruction of the inheritance, or where the mischief is remediless."

In numerous instances, injunctions have issued to prevent trespass. For example, in Hollenbeck v. St. Mark's 

Lutheran Church, 154 App. Div. 328, 138 N. Y. S. 1063, an owner was enjoined from allowing snow and ice from his roof to be deposited in a narrow space between his building and that of an adjoining owner. A similar case is 

Davis v. Niagara Falls Tower Co., 171 N. Y. 336, 64 N. E. 

4, 57 L. R. A. 545, 89 Am. St. Rep. 817. In Barnes v. Hagar 

148 N. Y. S. 395, affd. 166 App. Div. 952, 151 N. Y. S. 602,

an injunction was issued to restrain an owner of cattle from allowing said cattle to run at large and commit a continuous trespass. In Bishop v. Hughes, 117 App. Div. 425, 102

N. Y. S. 595, an injunction was issued to compel the defendant to remove his personal property from the premises of the plaintiff. In N. Y. C. & H. R. R. Co. v. City of Rochester, 127 N. Y. 591, 28 N. E. 416, the City was restrained from allowing sewage to run on plaintiff's land. In Grosser v. City of Rochester, 148 N. Y. 235, 42 N. E. 672, the

City was restrained from entering upon certain lands where the condemnation proceedings to acquire the lands had been defective.

It is often a difficult matter to determine whether or not an injunction shall issue when the actual injury to the plaintiff is slight as compared to the great loss which the defendant will incur if the injunction issues. This involves a so-called "balancing of injuries" between the parties. In 

Whalen v. Union Bag & Paper Co., 208 N. Y. 1, 101 N. E. 

805, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 770, the court held that where the evidence clearly establishes an unlawful invasion of the plaintiff's rights, an injunction will not be denied because the plaintiff's loss from the alleged wrong is slight as compared with the defendant's expense of abating the condition. However, in McCann v. Chasm Power Co., 211 

N. Y. 301, 305, 105 N. E. 416, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 768, the Court declared: "A court of equity can never be justified in making an inequitable decree. If the protection of a legal right even would do a plaintiff but comparatively little good and would produce great public or private hardship, equity will withhold its discreet and beneficent hand and remit the plaintiff to his legal rights and remedies."

The court, therefore, refused to decree an injunction to protect a merely technical or unsubstantial right of a riparian owner at the risk of endangering the supply of electricity for public and private use for a large number of persons in a community.

INJUNCTIONS 

163 The courts have followed this doctrine in several cases, the principal one being Forstmann v. Joray Holding Co., 244 N. Y. 22, 154 N. E. 652, where the court refused to enforce a restrictive covenant which had nearly expired. See also, Lyons v. Water Commissioners, 228 App. Div. 585, 240 

N. Y. S. 647; Powlowski v. Mohawk Golf Club, 204 App. 

Div. 200, 198 N. Y. S. 30. 

§ 155. Same Elevated Railroad Cases. 

These cases are peculiar to the State of New York. The courts hold that when an elevated railroad structure is constructed in the street, it constitutes an illegal trespass upon the easements of light, air and access which are appurtenant to the adjacent land. In an action at law, the owner of the property interfered with cannot recover damages based upon the assumption that such trespass is to be permanent. He can recover only the damages which he has sustained up to the commencement of the action. 

In equity, however, he may sue to enjoin the continuance of the trespass and since the railroad company has the right by the exercise of eminent domain to acquire the easements, in such an action the court determines the amount of the damage which the owner has sustained and would sustain if the trespass were permanently continued and provides that upon the payment of that sum the plaintiff shall give a deed or convey the right to the defendant and the court will refuse an injunction when the defendant is willing to pay upon the receipt of such a conveyance. The court does not adjudge that the defendant shall pay such sum and that the plaintiff shall so convey. It provides that if the conveyance is made and the money paid no injunction shall issue. Condemnation proceedings are thus avoided. Since the easements are appurtenant to the land, only the owner of the land at the time of the suit may bring the action and in the action he recovers not only past damages but his permanent damages as well. Pappenheim v. Met. El. R. R. 

Co., 128 N. Y. 436, 28 N. E. 518, 13 L. R. A. 401, 26 Am.

St. Rep. 486; Pegram v. N. Y. El. R. R. Co., 147 N. Y. 135, 41 N. E. 424. Where premises are conveyed and the right of action for damages to the easements is reserved in the conveyance, the grantee holds all such damages subsequently awarded in trust for the grantor. West. Union 

Tel. Co. v. Shepard, 169 N. Y. 170, 62 N. E. 154, 58 L. R. A. 

115, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 202. An implied obligation is imposed upon the grantee as the holder of the legal title to do what is necessary to enable the grantor to obtain for his beneficial interest the damages which it was contemplated he was to have and this reservation is binding upon subsequent grantees even though there is no mention of such reservation in the conveyance by which the subsequent grantees take title. In such a case, where the grantee is requested to bring or join in the action against the railroad company to recover the damages reserved by the agreement and he refuses, the grantor can bring the action making the grantee, as well as the railway company, a party defendant and the whole matter can be determined in the one action. Drucker v. Manhattan Ry. Co. 213 N. Y. 543, 108 N. E. 74. 

§ 156. Nuisance Defined. 

In his treatise on Equity Jurisprudence, 4th. ed., sec. 

1926, Pomeroy has said: "The term nuisance has in equity no different signification from that given it at law. Anything which is a nuisance in law is also a nuisance in equity. *** There is no such thing as an equitable nuisance. This is not saying that the jurisdiction of law and that of equity are co-extensive; it is simply pointing out that equity in the determination of what constitutes a nuisance follows the law.' 

A condition is a nuisance when it clearly appears that the enjoyment of life and property is materially lessened thereby and the physical comfort of persons in their homes is materially interfered with. McCarty v. Natural Carbonic Gas Co., 189 N. Y. 40, 81 N. E. 549, 13 L. R. A. (N.

INJUNCTIONS 

165 

S.) 465, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 780. For example, the operation in a residential district of an open air boxing arena has been held to constitute a nuisance entitling the nearby property owners to an injunction. Russell v. Nostrand Athletic Club, Inc., 212 App. Div. 543, 209 N. Y. S. 

76, modified and affd. 240 N. Y. 681, 148 N. E. 756.

The distinction between trespass and nuisance is that the former is a direct wrong, while the latter may be the result of an act not wrongful in itself, but only in the consequences which flow from it. Hamlin v. Bender, 92 Misc. 

16, 155 N. Y. S. 963, affd. 173 App. Div. 996, 159 N. Y. S. 1117. 

§ 157. Classification of Nuisances. 

Nuisances may be divided into two classes: (1) Private nuisances, those which affect individuals, and (2) Public nuisances, those which affect the people at large.

The quality of the wrong is the same and the jurisdiction of the courts rests upon the same principles in both classes. 

Matter of Debs, 158 U. S. 564, 593, 39 L. ed. 1092, 15

Sup. Ct. Rep. 900. The distinction is necessary chiefly in order to determine who may bring the action. 

§ 158. Private Nuisance. 

"It is a well-settled doctrine that equity will restrain a private nuisance at the suit of the injured party. This remedy will not, however, be granted in every instance of alleged nuisance. *** The equitable jurisdiction is, therefore, based upon the notion of restraining irreparable mischief or of preventing vexatious litigation, or a multiplicity of suits." Pomeroy's Equity Jurisprudence, 4th ed., sec. 1350.

The basis of the jurisdiction is the well established rule that no one may make an unreasonable use of his own prem-

ises to the material injury of his neighbors' premises. The law of private nuisance is a law of degree and usually turns on the question of fact whether the use is reasonable or not under all the circumstances. No hard and fast rule controls the subject, for a use that is reasonable under one set of facts would be unreasonable under another. McCarty v. Natural Carbonic Gas Co., 189 N. Y. 40, 81 N. E. 549, 13 L. R. A. (N. S.) 465, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 780.

For example, a use of property may be a nuisance in a crowded city that would not be such in an undeveloped territory.

Formerly, a writ of injunction was but rarely issued in the case of a nuisance until the plaintiff's right had been established at law. But a suit at law is no longer a necessary preliminary in most states of the Union. Campbell v. 

Seaman, 63 N. Y. 568, 20 Am. Rep. 567, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 772. In that case the court enjoined the use of a brick manufacturing process which caused noxious gases to be generated and borne by the wind to adjacent lands, thereby injuring trees and vegetation. It was held to be immaterial that the injury was only occasional in view of the fact that it was intermittent. The court also deemed it no bar to the plaintiff's right to an injunction that the brickyard had been used in the same way before the plaintiff had purchased the adjoining land, in view of the fact that the plaintiff had not acquiesced in the nuisance for the statutory period of twenty years.

The rules laid down in Campbell v. Seaman, supra, have been applied to cases of storage of gunpowder and other explosives adjoining plaintiff's premises (Heeg. v. Licht, 80 

N. Y. 579, 36 Am. Rep. 654); to the operation of a lighting plant (Bly v. Edison Elec. Illum. Co., 172 N. Y. 1, 64 N. E. 

745, 58 L. R. A. 500; Pritchard v. Edison Elec. Illum. Co., 179 N. Y. 364, 72 N. E. 243); to the operation of iron works, where plaintiff's house was jarred and invaded by smoke, fumes and soot (Friedman v. Columbia Machine Works, 99 

App. Div. 504, 91 N. Y. S. 129) and many others.

INJUNCTIONS 

167 In McCarty v. Natural Carbonic Gas Co., supra, the court recognized the rule that whether or not the emission of noxious gases amounts to a nuisance depends upon the facts of each case. Such question is often a question of law, but where the facts are complicated it may be a question of fact, and in no case should the injunction be broader than the protection of the plaintiff demands.

A permanent injunction may be issued to put a stop to a nuisance even though the amount of the damages has not been fixed, particularly where strong and aggravated instances of a continuing trespass are shown. Garvey v. 

Long Island Railroad Co., 159 N. Y. 323, 54 N. E. 57, 70 

Am. St. Rep. 550, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 785. See, also, Little Falls Fibre Co. v. Ford & Son, Inc., 126 Misc. 

126, 212 N. Y. S. 630.

The fact that numerous persons are injured does not make the nuisance a public one. If it is one wrongful act comprehending the property of several in its injurious process, all may unite in an action or one or more may sue in behalf of all. Greer v. Smith, 155 App. Div. 420, 140 N. Y. 

S. 43. 

§ 159. 

Public Nuisance. 

"A court of equity has jurisdiction to restrain existing or threatened public nuisances by injunction, at the suit of the attorney-general in England, and at the suit of the state, or the people, or municipality, or some other proper officer representing the commonwealth in this country; the public nuisance must be established by clear evidence before the punitive remedy will be granted. A public nuisance will also be restrained at the suit of a private person who suffers therefrom a special and particular injury distinct from that suffered by him in common with the public at large; but this injury must be real, and such that the legal remedy of damages would not be adequate. Pomeroy's Equity 

Jurisprudence, 4th ed. sec. 1349. See, also, Hamlin v. Ben-

der, 92 Misc. 16, 155 N. Y. S. 963, affd. 173 App. Div. 

996, 159 N. Y. S. 1117. 

To illustrate: In the case of Callanan v. Gilman, 107 

N. Y. 360, 14 N. E. 264, 1 Am. St. Rep. 831, Bachrach's

Cases in Equity, p. 791, the unreasonable and continuous use of a public sidewalk was restrained to the extent that such use was unnecessary and unreasonable. The court recognized the right of a tradesman to temporarily obstruct the passage on the sidewalk, but denied his right to obstruct the sidewalk for a period of from four to five hours on each business day. See, also, Bradley v. Degnon Contracting 

Co., 224 N. Y. 60, 120 N. E. 89; Lyman v. Village of Potsdam, 228 N. Y. 398, 127 N. E. 312. In Cranford v. Tyrrell, 128 N. Y. 341, 28 N. E. 514, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 797, the court recognized the principle that, while, as a general rule, only the public authorities can interfere to suppress a common nuisance, a private person who has sustained special damage by reason of such nuisance may recover the same and restrain the continuance of the nuisance. 

The court, therefore, at the suit of an adjoining neighbor restrained the defendant from maintaining a house of illfame on his premises. The same rule was recognized in Manhattan Bridge Three-Cent Line v. Third Ave. Ry. Co., 154 

App. Div. 704, 139 N. Y. S. 434, an action to restrain the operation of a street railroad over streets as to which it had not obtained a franchise, in which it was held that the plaintiff must allege and prove special damage in order to establish its right to abate a public nuisance. 

In Hamlin v. Bender, supra, the court, at the suit of the 

Commissioner of Safety of the city of Utica, enjoined the conducting of a motion picture show on Sunday, where the theatre was located near a large church and the attendants at such services were obliged to pass the theatre and the box-office as well as the signs and posters advertising the exhibition, and it appeared that people congregated about the entrance of the theatre to such an extent as to cause a congestion of travel.

INJUNCTIONS 

169 

The Penal Law makes a public nuisance a crime (Sec. 

1530, 1532). This, however, does not divest the court of equity of jurisdiction if it be also a violation of property rights, and the party aggrieved has no other adequate remedy for the prevention of the irreparable injury which will result from the failure or inability of a court of law to redress such rights. In re Debs, 158 U. S. 564; Cranford v. Tyrell, supra; People v. Rubenfeld, 254 N. Y. 245, 172 

N. E. 485.

The City may restrain the maintenance of an encroachment on the street on the theory that it is a public nuisance. 

City v. Rice, 198 N. Y. 124, 91 N. E. 283, 28 L. R. A. (N. S.) 

375; City v. Knickerbocker Trust Co., 104 App. Div. 223, 93 

N. Y. S. 937; People v. Ahearn, 124 App. Div. 840, 109 N.

Y. S. 249. A like right exists in favor of an adjoining property owner who can show special injury (Ackerman v. True, 175 N. Y. 353, 67 N. E. 629) but not where he is in pari delicto and can show no material injury. ser v. Brown, 149 N. Y. 163, 43 N. E. 524.) 

§ 160. 

Trade Names. 

(Worm- As a rule, a person has a perfect right to use his own name in his own business. As a general principle of law, a person's name is his own property, and he has the same right to use it as he has to use any other species of property. 

It is also true, however, that no man has a right to sell his goods as those of another. He cannot, by fraud or artifice, injure the business of another by inducing the public to believe that his product is the product of another, and courts of equity may enjoin the improper use of a trade name. World's Dispensary Medical Assn. v. Pierce, 203 

N. Y. 419, 96 N. E. 738, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 798.

In the case last cited, for example, in which it appeared that a physician, by the name of Ray V. Pierce, had built up a business in the manufacture and sale of "Dr. Pierce's Remedies," a competitor, by the name of Robert J. Pierce, who was not a licensed physician, was enjoined from placing on

the market remedies under the name of "Dr. Pierce's Remedies" or from using the word "Pierce" in advertising such remedies unless prefixed by the words "Robert J." or "Robert."

In Westphal v. Westphal's World's Best Corp., 216 App. 

Div. 53, 215 N. Y. S. 4, it appeared that one Paul Westphal had built up a large business in the manufacture and sale of a popular hair tonic, known under the trade name of "Westphal's," and that his grandson, who bore his name, had deliberately and for the purpose of destroying the plaintiff's business, organized the defendant corporation, of which he became president, for the purpose of putting on the market a rival hair tonic under the name "Westphal."

An Appellate Division of the Supreme Court upheld the injunction issued by the court below restraining the use by the defendant of the name "Westphal" in connection with the sale of its hair tonic. This was affirmed by the Court of Appeals, 243 N. Y. 639, 154 N. E. 638. 

Similarly, in Forman Co. v. Forman Mfg. Co., Inc., 119 

Misc. 87, 193 N. Y. S. 597, in which it appeared that the plaintiff had been engaged in the fur business in the city of 

Rochester for the past ten years under the name of the "B.

Forman Co.," the defendant was enjoined from engaging in the same business in the same locality under the name of "Forman Mfg. Co., Inc.," although the name used by the defendant was that of its principal stockholders and the name under which the defendant's business was incorporated. 

The underlying principle of these cases is that the name is so similar that the public may be confused, and that some people may do business with one person in the belief that they are doing business with another. Eastern Const. Co. v. Eastern Engineering Corp., 246 N. Y. 459, 159 N. E. 397.

The principle applies, however, when no question of business is involved. Thus, a corporation organized under the name of "The Society of The War of 1812 in the State of 

New York" was enjoined from using such name when it appeared that an earlier society organized for patriotic and

INJUNCTIONS 

171 benevolent purposes had been incorporated under the name of "The Society of The War of 1812." Society of 1812 v. 

Society of 1812, 46 App. Div. 568, 62 N. Y. S. 355, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 810. Likewise, the use of the words "Good Samaritans" in the name of a fraternal order has been enjoined where such name had already been used by and identified with a similar organization. Grand Lodge, etc., v. Johnson, 107 Misc. 249, 177 N. Y. S. 500. See, also, B. P. O. Elks v. Improved B. P. O. Elks, 205 N. Y. 459, 98 

N. E. 756, Ann. Cas. 1913 E 639; Salvation Army in U. S. 

v. Am. Salvation Army, 135 App. Div. 268, 120 N. Y. S. 471. 

Where a certificate of incorporation is filed with the same name as that of an existing corporation, or where the name so closely resembles that of the existing corporation as to be calculated to deceive, the action of the secretary of state is not conclusive, and courts of equity have frequently granted relief to a prior corporation aggrieved. Millspaugh v. Cassedy, 191 App. Div. 221, 229, 181 N. Y. S. 276; Pansy 

Waist Co., Inc., v. Pansy Dress Co., Inc., 203 App. Div. 585,. 

196 N. Y. S. 825. However, a broader latitude has been allowed in the matter of similarity of names of banking institutions than in ordinary commercial enterprises. Hence, the "New York Trust Company" was not granted an injunction to restrain the use by others of the name, "New 

York County Trust Company." New York Trust Co. v. N. Y. 

County Trust Co., 125 Misc. 735, 211 N. Y. S. 785, affd. 215 

App. Div. 699, 212 N. Y. S. 882.

In all cases of trade names and their protection, the intervention of courts of equity is largely a matter of discretion, and in the absence of fraud or deception, the court may, in its discretion, deny to the plaintiffs equitable relief. For example, where the New York Hygeia Ice Co. had no knowledge of the use of the word "Hygeia" by any other ice company until after its business was in full operation, and there was no evidence of actual deception, the court declined to enjoin the use of such word by the defendant. Hygeia 

Water Ice Co. v. N. Y. Hygeia Ice Co., 140 N. Y. 94, 35 N. E.

1 

417, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 813. See, also, Schinasi v. Schinasi, 169 App. Div. 887, 155 N. Y. S. 867. Furthermore, as stated by the Court in Buffalo Typewriter Exchange v. McGarl, 240 N. Y. 113, 117, 147 N. E. 546: "It must be assumed that the public will use reasonable intelligence and discrimination with reference to the names of corporations with which they are dealing, or intend to deal, the same as with individuals having the same or similar names.”

Hence, an injunction to restrain the use of the words "Typewriter Exchange," as part of the name of the defendant corporation, was refused where there was no evidence that the words were used to deceive or did deceive any one. 

Buffalo Typewriter Exchange v. McGarl, supra. 

In Charles S. Higgins Co. v. Higgins Soap Co., 144 N. Y. 

462, 469, 39 N. E. 490, 27 L. R. A. 42, 43 Am. St. Rep. 769, the Court of Appeals laid down the following rule; and re-stated it in Corning Glass Works v. Corning Cut Glass 

Co., 197 N. Y. 173, 179, 90 N. E. 449: "Whether the court will interfere in a particular case must depend upon circumstances; the identity or similarity of the names; the identity of the business of the respective corporations; how far the name is a true description of the kind and quality of the articles manufactured or the business carried on; the extent of the confusion which may be created or apprehended, and other circumstances which might justly influence the judgment of the judge in granting or withholding the remedy." 

§ 161. Trademarks.
