Even though the trademark which a party uses is unregistered, a court of equity will restrain others from using it in such manner as to deceive the public. See Fisher v. 

Star Co., 231 N. Y. 414, 132 N. E. 133, 19 A. L. R. 937. (A writ of certiorari in this case was denied by the United

INJUNCTIONS 

173 

States Supreme Court, Star Co. v. Fisher, 257 U. S. 654, 42 

Sup. Ct. Rep. 94.)

As a rule, a merchant cannot acquire a proprietary right to the exclusive use of a certain form or color of package. 

But such proprietary interest may be acquired where the size, style and dimensions are accompanied with certain emblems, labels or other devices and a certain name, although each one of these might be used separately by another. Fischer v. Blank, 138 N. Y. 244, 33 N. E. 1040, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 820. 

The use of descriptive words or phrases, such as "Jewelry of Distinction" and "Silverite" may be protected by injunction against the fraudulent use of the same by competitors. 

Fishel & Sons, Inc., v. Distinctive Jewelry Co., Inc., 196 

App. Div. 779, 188 N. Y. S. 633. It has been held illegal to name a marine engine "Cadyford" in imitation of the name "Ford." Ford Motor Co. v. C. N. Cady Co., 208 N. Y. S. 

574. However, protection has been denied to the label "Roachsault," when placed on an article known as roach salt, on the ground that common words of this character which merely give a correct description of the article will not be protected as trademarks. Barrett Chemical Co. v. 

Stern, 176 N. Y. 27, 68 N. E. 65, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 824. On the other hand, the terms "Sliced Animals" or "Sliced Birds," as applied to games or puzzles, have been held to be arbitrary words, in no manner descriptive of the articles, and therefore entitled to protection. Selchow v. 

Baker, 93 N. Y. 59, 45 Am. Rep. 169, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 815.

The use of the trademark "The Vulture" on boxes of matches has been held to be an infringement of the trademark "The Vulcan," when accompanied by other circumstances calculated to mislead. T. A. Vulcan v. Myers, 139 

N. Y. 364, 34 N. E. 904. And, in the field of clothing manufacture, a trademark device consisting of the letters "GGG” intertwined in script has been protected against the use of a similar device employing two script "G's" with an "&"

between. Goldman & Bros., Inc., v. Goldstein, 125 Misc. 737, 211 N. Y. S. 872.

T. A. Vulcan v. Myers, supra, is authority for the rule that it is not necessary for the plaintiff to show that any person has been actually misled in such cases or that the defendant intended to deceive others. It is the liability to injury which the courts seek to prevent.

An injunction will be refused where the trademark or name for which protection is sought is one which is itself calculated to deceive the public. Worden & Co. v. Calif. Fig 

Syrup Co., 187 U. S. 516, 47 L. ed. 282, 23 Sup. Ct. Rep. 

161; Fetridge v. Wells, 13 How. Pr. 385. 

§ 162. 

Trade Secrets. 

It is well settled that a court of equity will restrain a party from making a disclosure of secrets communicated to him in the course of confidential employment, regardless of the character of the secrets, so long as they are important to his employer. The employee cannot gain the knowledge and then set it up against his employer. Little v. Gallus, 4 App. Div. 569, 38 N. Y. S. 487.

Clearly, secrets in reference to the customers of the employer may be protected. Goldschmidt v. Sachs, 162 N. Y. S. 

323. See section 163. 

Secret processes need not be patented or patentable, in order to be protected as trade secrets. Tabor v. Hoffman, 118 N. Y. 30, 23 N. E. 12, 16 Am. St. Rep. 740; Vulcan 

Detinning Co. v. Assmann, 185 App. Div. 399, 173 N. Y. S. 

334; Eastern Extracting Co. v. Greater N. Y. Extracting 

Co., 126 App. Div. 928, 110 N. Y. S. 738. For example, in

Tabor v. Hoffman, supra, the court restrained a competitor from using information surreptitiously obtained in reference to secrets connected with the manufacture of an unpatented improved pump, the patent on the manufacture of the original pump having expired.

An employee who violates his contract of employment by

INJUNCTIONS 

175 quitting prior to its expiration and accepting employment from a competitor may be enjoined from disclosing his employer's trade secrets in violation of his contract, even though some of the information was acquired under a contract prior to the one which was the basis of the action. 

McCall Co. v. Wright, 198 N. Y. 143, 91 N. E. 516, 31 L. R. 

A. (N. S.) 249. Furthermore, an employee may be prevented from revealing trade secrets even where the term of his employment is at will and has not been fixed for a definite period. See Clark Paper & Mfg. Co. v. Stenacher, 236 N. Y. 312, at p. 317, 140 N. E. 708, 29 A. L. R. 1325, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 845. 

However, there is no betrayal of confidence unless there is a secret to protect. Knowledge of a patented process known to an employee prior to the employment in question is not entitled to protection as a "trade secret." Kaumagraph Co. v. Stampagraph Co., 235 N. Y. 1, 138 N. E. 485. Moreover, there is a distinction between the acquisition by an employee of trade secrets and the acquisition of mere skill, training and experience. The use of such talents in other employment cannot be enjoined. Clark Paper & Mfg. Co. v. Stenacher, supra. 

§ 163. Unfair Competition. 

"The doctrine of unfair trade amounts to no more than this: One person has no right to sell goods as the goods of another, nor to do other business as the business of another, and on proper showing will be restrained from so doing.” 

White Studio v. Dreyfoos, 221 N. Y. 46, at p. 49, 116 N. E. 796. 

Unfair competition is distinguished from a case involving a trademark in that unfair competition does not involve the exclusive right to the use of a name, symbol or device. 

A word may not be capable of becoming a trademark and yet there may be an unfair use of it which will constitute unfair competition. See Sartor v. Schaden, 125 Iowa 696, 101 N. W. 511. For example, in Westcott Chuck Co. v.

Oneida Nat. Chuck Co., 199 N. Y. 247, 92 N. E. 639, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 802, the patent of a certain article had expired, but while the defendant had the right to manufacture and sell articles of the character in question, the court held that it had no right to place on such articles the size number, numerals or the names adopted by the plaintiff, and that such unfair competition in trade might properly be restrained. Likewise, in Fisher v. Star Co., 231 

N. Y. 414, 132 N. E. 133, 19 A. L. R. 937, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 805, the court restrained the use in cartoons by others of the well-known characters "Mutt and Jeff," at the suit of the originator of the same. The court declared that it was not confined in the exercise of its equitable powers to preventing unfair competition among manufacturers and dealers in goods, and held that the same principles applied to the use of the cartoons in question. Clearly, in cases where infringement of copyright is involved the court may accord protection to the injured party. See Underhill v. Schenck, 238 N. Y. 7, 143 N. E. 773, 33 A. L. R. 303.

It is obviously unfair and fraudulent for an employee to obtain a list of customers for the benefit of a competitor of his employer. Hence, it is proper for a court of equity to award an injunction to restrain the use of such a list by such a competitor, and the court may also permanently restrain such competitor from soliciting business from any such customers in any manner. Conviser v. Brownstone & Co., 209 App. Div. 584, 205 N. Y. S. 82. 

However, unless restrained by contract, an employee may, if it involves no breach of confidence, solicit the trade of his former customers, provided there is no misrepresentation for "equity has no power to compel a man who changes employers to wipe clean the slate of his memory." Scott v. 

Scott, 186 App. Div. 518, 174 N. Y. S. 583.

INJUNCTIONS 

177 

§ 164. Negative Covenants Covenants Restricting Use of Land.

The principles underlying the enforcement of restrictive covenants on real property by injunction in equity are clearly stated by Chief Judge Cardozo, in his opinion in Evangelical Lutheran Church v. Sahlem, 254 N. Y. 161, 166 

N. E. 455. He said: "By the settled doctrine of equity, restrictive covenants in respect of land will be enforced by preventive remedies while the violation is still in prospect, unless the attitude of the complaining owner in standing on his covenant is unconscionable or oppressive. Relief is not withheld because the money damage is unsubstantial or even none at all. *** Inequity there may be in standing on the letter of a covenant when the neighborhood has so altered that the ends to be attained by the restriction have been frustrated by the years (Trustees of Columbia College v. Thacher, 87 N. Y. 311, 316; McClure v. Leaycraft, 183 N. Y. 36, 75 

N. E. 961; Batchelor v. Hinkle, 210 N. Y. 243, 104 N. E. 

629). Inequity there may be in a demand for a mandatory injunction that will tear a completed building down, when the builder has acted in good faith, the covenant is presently to expire and the havoc wrought by demolition will be disproportionate, in a degree shocking to the conscience, to any corresponding benefit (Forstmann v. Joray 

Holding Co., 244 N. Y. 22, 154 N. E. 652). Few formulas are so absolute as not to bend before the blast of extraordinary circumstances. In the award of equitable remedies, there is often an element of discretion, but never a discretion that is absolute or arbitrary. In equity as at law there are sign posts for the traveler."

In the case from which this quotation was taken, all of the adjoining owners except one had agreed to vary the restrictions on the property so as to permit the erection of a church and signed consents accordingly. The church tried to get this one owner to consent, but he refused and not-

withstanding this, the church took title to the land and started to erect its building, and then brought an action to procure a judgment that the restrictive covenants were no longer in effect. The Supreme Court and Appellate 

Division both said that the owner was not entitled to equitable relief, but was to be limited in respect of remedies to an action for damages at law. The Court of Appeals reversed, holding that the one owner had an absolute right to have the restrictive covenants enforced, and that there was nothing in his conduct or in the circumstances surrounding the transaction which would deprive him of his right in equity to enforce the covenants.

As stated above, however, where the condition of the property by which the premises are surrounded has become so altered that the reason for the covenants is gone and it would be inequitable to enforce them, the injunction will be denied. Trustees of Columbia College v. Thacher, 87 N. Y. 

311; Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 828. The remedy by injunction may be denied even in cases where an action at law for damages would be sustained. Bull v. Burton, 227 N. Y. 101, 124 N. E. 111.

In dealing with these cases, the court follows the principles already expressed in the section dealing with trespass, and refuses to protect a merely technical or unsubstantial right when such a decree would be inequitable (see 

Sec. 154 ante).

A court of equity may remove a restrictive covenant as a cloud on title. St. Stephens Church v. Church of Transfiguration, 201 N. Y. 1, 94 N. E. 191. A declaratory judgment may be used to accomplish the same purpose. Evangelical Lutheran Church v. Sahlem, supra. 

§ 165. Negative Covenants-Contracts Involving Per sonal Services.

An employer may enjoin his employee, who is under contract to render personal services to him for a stated period,

INJUNCTIONS 

179 from rendering similar services to another during the same period when the circumstances are such that the employer would sustain irreparable damage thereby. For example, where a theatrical manager made a contract with a distinguished actress for a certain season it was held that he might secure an injunction to prevent her appearance at a rival theatre, even though the actress might be pecuniarily able to respond in damages in an action at law. Daly v. 

Smith, 38 N. Y. Sup. Ct. 158, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 834. See, also, Shubert v. Angeles, 80 App. Div. 625, 80 

N. Y. S. 146. However, equity will not restrain an actor from acting for others between seasons when the contract does not bind him to such an agreement. Lawrence v. Dixey, 119 App. Div. 295, 104 N. Y. S. 516. Moreover, it must appear that the relief obtainable by the injured party at law is inadequate. Hence, if there is no proof that the services of the defendant are special or unique, or that the employee is possessed of valuable trade secrets, a court of equity may refuse to grant an injunction. Hammerstein v. Mann, 137 

App. Div. 580, 122 N. Y. S. 276; Clark Paper & Mfg. Co. v. 

Stenacher, 236 N. Y. 312, 140 N. E. 708, 29 A. L. R. 1325, Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 845; Dockstader v. Reed, 121 

App. Div. 846, 106 N. Y. S. 795. 

The case of McCall Co. v. Wright, 198 N. Y. 143, 91 N. E. 

516, 31 L. R. A. (N. S.) 249, has been cited frequently as holding that mutuality of contract between employer and employee is unnecessary, but that case, as pointed out by 

Judge Crane, in Clark Paper & Mfg. Co. v. Stenacher, supra, at p. 317, was with regard to trade secrets. It had previously been held that the contract must be mutual (See 

Lerner v. Tetrazzini, 71 Misc. 182, 129 N. Y. S. 889, affd. 

144 App. Div. 928, 129 N. Y. S. 1132, affd. 207 N. Y. 709, 101 N. E. 1109) and Clark Paper & Mfg. Co. v. Stenacher, supra, indicates that this rule is still in force. 

An agreement by a vendor, upon the sale of a business, not to enter into a similar business in the same locality for a specified period will be enforced in equity at the suit of the

vendee or his assigns, provided the restraint is reasonable. 

Francisco v. Smith, 143 N. Y. 488, 38 N. E. 980; N. Y. Bank 

Note Co. v. Hamilton Bank Note Co., 180 N. Y. 280, 73 

N. E. 48; Diamond Match Co. v. Roeber, 106 N. Y. 473, 487, 13 N. E. 419, 60 Am. Rep. 474; Am. Ice Co. v. Meckel, 109 App. Div. 93, 95 N. Y. S. 1060; Jochum Bros. v. Ridgewood Pie Baking Co., 210 App. Div. 428, 206 N. Y. S. 252. 

§ 166. Injunctions in Relation to Strikes, Boycotts, etc. 

In reference to the use of injunctions in labor disputes, Pomeroy has said, in his Equity Jurisprudence, 4th ed., sec. 2027: "Some means can, under no circumstances, be justified. "The trader is not a free lance; he may fight as a soldier but not as a guerilla. *** His weapons must be furnished by the law of trade.' (Martell v. White, 185 Mass. 255, 69 

N. E. 1085.) Fraud in all its forms is not a legitimate weapon for use on a competitor. Likewise, actual coercion is universally condemned. The earliest utterances of the courts condemn without qualification the use of force and intimidation. Obviously, a trader should not be permitted to take or keep customers away from his rival by the use of actual violence. Nor any more should strikers be permitted to secure from their employer higher wages or shorter hours things commendable in themselves-by burning his property or keeping other employees away with club or gun. It is equally clear that actual threats of violence belong in the same class. But when specific conduct other than violence and actual threats of violence is presented, a conflict at once arises in the determination of what acts will be treated as coercive.”

Much difficulty has arisen in determining when it is appropriate for a court of equity to intervene by injunction in strikes, boycotts and similar labor disputes, and there are a maze of decisions, especially of the Special Term and the Appellate Division, which cannot altogether be reconciled. 

The first case of importance in this state was National

INJUNCTIONS 

181 

Protective Association v. Cummings, 170 N. Y. 315, 63

N. E. 369. In that case, the court laid down the broad principle that equity will permit the broadest freedom to employer and employee; that, except as bound by contract, an employer may discharge at will and the employee may work or refuse to work at will; what one may do, a number may do; and that, therefore, employees have a right to strike for any lawful purpose, such as better working conditions; working only with other union employees, etc. 

This was followed by a number of like decisions, the more important of which are Jacobs v. Cohen, 183 N. Y. 207, 76 

N. E. 5; Peo. v. Marcus, 185 N. Y. 257, 77 N. E. 1073; 

Kissam v. U. S. Printing Co. 199 N. Y. 76, 92 N. E. 214;

Adair v. U. S., 208 U. S. 175; Loewe v. Lawlor, 208 U. S. 

274. The subject was clarified by the notable decision of the Court of Appeals in Exchange Bakery, Inc. v. Rifkin, 245 N. Y. 260, 157 N. E. 130, where the court stated, among other things, that picketing without a strike is no more unlawful than a strike without picketing. The court seemed to indicate that the purpose of the strike was the determining factor and said that if the purpose was lawful, all that remained to be determined was whether the means were lawful. They said that picketing may not be accompanied by violence, trespass, threats or intimidations, express or implied; that no crowds may be collected on or near the employers' property and that the free entrance of strangers, customers or employees must not be impeded. They also called attention to the well-known equitable principle that equity could only be used to give protection for the future, and that whatever wrongs had been perpetrated in the past must be remedied at law. This was followed by Interborough Rapid Transit Co. v. Lavin, 247 N. Y. 65, 159 N. E. 

863, which held that persons should not be enjoined from attempting to persuade workers, belonging to one union, to join another union in the absence of an express agreement with the employer. This in turn was followed by 

Nann v. Raimist, 255 N. Y. 507, 174 N. E. 690, which held

that, where in a controversey between rival unions, there had been violence and misrepresentation, and where the defending union had shown a complete disregard for the decree of the court, all picketing might be prohibited. 

The rule would now seem to be, therefore, that a court of equity may intervene only where there is an attempt to effectuate an unlawful purpose or to effectuate a lawful purpose by unlawful means.

With respect to boycotts, the rule would seem to be that where illegal methods are used, a boycott will be enjoined, but not otherwise. Compare Auburn Draying Co. v. 

Wardell, 227 N. Y. 1, 124 N. E. 97, Bachrach's Cases in 

Equity, p. 865 with Bossert v. Dhuy, 221 N. Y. 342, 117 

N. E. 582. 

§ 167. 

Taxpayers' Actions.

It is provided in section 51 of the General Municipal Law of the State of New York that "All officers, agents, commissioners and other persons acting, or who have acted, for and on behalf of any county, town, village or municipal corporation in this state, and each and every one of them, may be prosecuted, and an action may be maintained against them to prevent any illegal official act on the part of any such officers, agents, commissioners or other persons, or to prevent waste or injury to, or to restore and make good, any property, funds or estate of such county, town, village or municipal corporation by any person or corporation whose assessment, or by any number of persons or corporations, jointly, the sum of whose assessments shall amount to one thousand dollars, and who shall be liable to pay taxes on such assessment in the county, town, village or municipal corporation to prevent the waste or injury of whose property the action is brought, or who have been assessed or paid taxes therein upon any assessment of the above-named amount within one year previous to the commencement of any such action." 

Before the enactment of the taxpayer's statute, an alleged

INJUNCTIONS 

183 delinquency in the administration of public affairs could not be called in question by a taxpayer unless he showed some individual interest, distinct from that which belonged to every inhabitant of the town or county. Roosevelt v. 

Draper, 23 N. Y. 318. 

In Ayers v. Lawrence, (1874) 59 N. Y. 192, 195, Allen, J., describing the conditions existing before the enactment of the statute, said: "Municipal corporations had become to an alarming extent the prey of the spoiler, and the taxpayer, upon whom the loss fell and the burden of the wrong-doing ultimately rested, was remediless. The wrong was ordinarily accomplished, not by a waste or destruction of the property or funds of the corporation in actual possession, but by the issue and sale of the bonds or other obligations of the municipality, and a conversion or misappropriation of the proceeds. It was by abuse or misuse of the credit of the corporation, creating a lien upon its property if it had any, but especially pledging its future resources and the power of taxation vested in the municipal government, that the funds and property of the corporation were wasted, and the taxpayer burdened and his property incumbered. By the rules of law, as established by the courts, the taxpayers were entirely without remedy, no matter how gross the fraud or wanton the robbery, and notwithstanding the officers of the corporation, those whom the law had put in authority to watch over and protect their constituents and guard their interests, were faithless to their duty or confederate with the wrong-doers Neither was there authority in the State, by its attorney general, to intervene by action to protect the property rights and interests of municipal corporations. 

This utter helplessness of the taxpayer, and the fact that he was entirely at the mercy of officials who might prove unworthy of and criminally unfaithful to their trust, became an evil calling loudly for correction. Frauds had been accomplished and municipalities wasted and burdened without redress, and the act was passed with a view to remedy

the felt defect in the law, and give the taxpayer a concurrent action with the corporation for the prevention or correction of the wrongs mentioned in it."

This remedy at the suit of taxpayers has steadily widened in scope since its beginnings in 1872 and the Court of Appeals has declared that the statute should be liberally construed. Stetler v. McFarlane, 230 N. Y. 400, 130 N. E. 

591. In Altschul v. Ludwig, 216 N. Y. 459, 111 N. E. 216,

Bachrach's Cases in Equity, p. 850, the Court of Appeals held that the mere illegality of an official act is not sufficient under this statute to justify relief by injunction but that, when waste or injury is not involved, it must appear that the threatened illegal act will imperil the public interests or produce some public mischief. In the instant case a taxpayer successfully sought to restrain the erection of a theatre in violation of the building code. In Western New 

York Water Co. v. Buffalo, 242 N. Y. 202, 151 N. E. 207, the Court reiterated this rule in refusing an injunction to restrain the delivery of water outside the City limits, holding that while the act may be illegal, the City or taxpayer was not injured thereby.

Nor is the remedy available to restrain a municipality from entering into or carrying out a contract simply because it appears unwise or extravagant. To do so would be to substitute the court for the elected city officials. Talcott v. Buffalo, 125 N. Y. 280, 26 N. E. 263. 

The courts have applied the remedy to the following situations, among others: the turning over of the expenditure of public moneys to an unofficial citizens' committee, at the same time holding that the legislature may authorize expenditures of public money for a city celebration (Schieffelin v. Hylan, 236 N. Y. 254, 140 N. E. 689); the illegal operation by the City of an omnibus line at a loss (Belt Line Ry. 

Corp. v. City of New York, 118 Misc. 665, 195 N. Y. S. 

203); the illegal reinstatement of a public officer (Schieffelin v. Dolan, 204 App. Div. 351, 198 N. Y. S. 213); the illegal payment of a pension (Schieffelin v. Enright, 200 App.

INJUNCTIONS 

185 

Div. 312, 192 N. Y. S. 729); and a judgment illegally consented to by the Corporation Counsel (Bush v. O'Brien, 164 

N. Y. 201, 58 N. E. 106).

On the other hand, the Court of Appeals refused to restrain the preparation for the election of delegates to a constitutional convention under an alleged unconstitutional statute, on the ground of lack of jurisdiction to determine the question in an action of this nature. Schieffelin v. Komfort, 212 N. Y. 520, 106 N. E. 675, L. R. A. 1915 D 485.

Nor will it be granted when madamus is available. Southern 

Leasing Co. v. Ludwig, 217 N. Y. 100, 111 N. E. 470.
